### [1] [A particle method for the space homogeneous Lenard-Balescu equation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30808)
*José Antonio Carrillo, Timon Gutleb, Andrea Medaglia, Yijia Tang*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops a structure-preserving particle method for the spatially homogeneous Lenard-Balescu equation, reformulates the dielectric function to make its nonlocal nonlinear evaluation tractable, and validates the scheme with numerical tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Lenard-Balescu equation improves on the Landau equation by including dynamic screening through a dielectric function, but its nonlocal nonlinear dielectric term makes numerical approximation difficult. The authors seek a particle method that preserves conservation laws and entropy dissipation.

Method: The authors construct a particle method from a regularized variational formulation analogous to a prior Landau-collision-operator strategy. They rewrite the dielectric function as integrals involving orthogonal and parallel components relative to the frequency variable, reducing its evaluation to an integral of the directional derivative of the solution on a hyperplane orthogonal to the frequency variable and a one-dimensional Hilbert transform. This equivalent form enables an efficient numerical approximation of the Lenard-Balescu kernel.

Result: The semi-discrete method guarantees conservation laws and entropy dissipation. Several numerical tests validate the method and are used to explore qualitative properties of the Lenard-Balescu equation; specific quantitative results are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: A structure-preserving particle method for the spatially homogeneous Lenard-Balescu equation is proposed, with a tractable dielectric-function formulation and efficient kernel approximation, validated numerically and suitable for exploring qualitative behavior.

Abstract: We propose a structure preserving particle method to numerically approximate the spatially homogeneous Lenard-Balescu equation for collisional plasmas. The Lenard-Balescu operator is an improvement of the Landau equation with a dielectric function taking the dynamic screening effect into account. Our particle method is based on the variational formulation of the Lenard-Balescu equation under suitable regularization analogous to the strategy followed for the Landau collision operator in [12]. The method guarantees the conservation laws and the entropy dissipation at the semi-discrete level. The novel difficulty of the nonlocal nonlinear dependence of the dielectric function is tackled by rewriting it as integrals in terms of orthogonal and parallel components to the frequency variable. This equivalent form reduces the evaluation of the dielectric function to numerically computing an integral of the directional derivative of the solution on a hyperplane orthogonal to the frequency variable and its one-dimensional Hilbert transform. An efficient implementation of the numerical approximation for the Lenard-Balescu kernel is derived by exploiting this expression of the dielectric function. We finally validate our method by means of several numerical tests, further allowing us to explore qualitative properties of the Lenard-Balescu equation.

</details>


### [2] [Single-Shot Single-Stage Stellarators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29090)
*Pedro F. Gil, Lanke Fu, Alan A. Kaptanoglu*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a single-stage stellarator design framework that optimizes plasma shapes together with near-optimal, engineering-feasible coils, eliminating the usual separate plasma and coil design stages. It demonstrates single-shot cold-start and warm-start optimizations yielding reactor-relevant quasi-axisymmetric and quasi-isodynamic stellarator configurations with self-consistent free-boundary solutions and improved coil feasibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stellarator plasmas and coils are geometrically complex and are typically designed separately, so a high-performance plasma may not be reproducible with coils that can actually be manufactured and assembled. The work aims to close this gap between plasma performance and engineering-feasible coil design.

Method: The authors present a single-stage optimization framework in which the plasma shape is optimized while, for every candidate shape, near-optimal coils satisfying hard engineering limits are found. The method requires no manually tuned weights and steers the plasma toward shapes producible by real coils. It is applied to single-shot optimization from tokamak cold-starts and to warm-started optimization from a W7-X-derived boundary, with self-consistent free-boundary solutions.

Result: From a single-shot cold-start, the method designs a finite-beta stellarator comparable to reactor-scaled NCSX, and a compact aspect-ratio 2.5, volume-averaged beta of 2% quasi-axisymmetric stellarator with one field period and self-consistent bootstrap current. From a W7-X-derived boundary, it designs a quasi-isodynamic stellarator with reactor-relevant stability properties and then warm-starts a single coil-set solution producing a high-performance quasi-isodynamic stellarator at every pressure from vacuum to volume-averaged beta of 4%. These self-consistent free-boundary solutions typically have better coils than a traditional separate-design approach at comparable or better plasma performance.

Conclusion: Robust single-stage stellarator optimization is possible, and the proposed approach provides a direct route to the design of reactor-scale stellarators by jointly achieving high plasma performance and manufacturable coil sets.

Abstract: In stellarator design, the plasma and the coils are so geometrically complex that they are usually designed separately, so that a high-performance plasma may be impossible to reproduce with coils that can actually be manufactured and assembled. We present a new method to close this gap. In this framework, the plasma shape is optimized while, for every candidate shape, near-optimal coils that satisfy hard engineering limits are found. Without any manually-tuned weights, the method steers the plasma towards shapes that real coils can produce. Moreover, we demonstrate the first single-shot optimization runs starting from tokamak ``cold-starts''. From a single-shot cold-start, we design a finite-$β$ stellarator comparable to reactor-scaled NCSX, as well as a compact aspect-ratio 2.5 and $\langleβ\rangle=2\%$ quasi-axisymmetric stellarator with one field period and self-consistent bootstrap current. Starting from a boundary derived from W7-X, we design a quasi-isodynamic stellarator with reactor-relevant stability properties. We then use this to warm-start an optimization for a single coil-set solution that produces a high-performance QI stellarator at every pressure value from vacuum to $\langleβ\rangle=4\%$, addressing directly the ``start-up'' phase of a reactor. The self-consistent free-boundary solutions for these stellarators typically have better coils than a traditional separate-design approach, at comparable or better plasma performance, illustrating that robust single-stage methods are possible and providing a direct route to the design of reactor-scale stellarators.

</details>


### [3] [Relativistic Harmonic Generation in Spatiotemporal Plasma Photonic Crystals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30084)
*Haojie Zhang, Boyuan Li, Xiangyan An, Rong Huang, Feng Liu, Zheng-Ming Sheng, Min Chen*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes a spatiotemporal plasma photonic crystal formed at a solid surface by intense pump pulses and probed by a separate pulse. Its periodicity sets allowed harmonic frequencies and emission angles, while Floquet bands explain probe energy gain and predict wavelength-dependent amplification, supported by particle-in-cell simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spatiotemporal photonic crystals can control light frequency, momentum, and energy, but extending this capability to relativistic laser intensities requires a plasma-based platform.

Method: The authors propose a spatiotemporal plasma photonic crystal (SPPC) generated at a solid surface driven by intense pump pulses, with a separate probe interacting with the modulation. They analyze the SPPC's spatial and temporal periodicity and complex Floquet bands to determine allowed harmonics, emission angles, and energy transfer, and compare the interpretation with particle-in-cell simulations.

Result: The SPPC periodicity determines the allowed harmonic frequencies and emission angles, and its complex Floquet bands explain how the probe draws energy from the modulation. The model also predicts wavelength-dependent probe amplification when the probe accesses amplifying SPPC modes, and it agrees with particle-in-cell simulations.

Conclusion: The work provides a plasma-based route to relativistic spatiotemporal photonic-crystal physics and an experimentally testable connection between SPPC band structure and energy transfer, including harmonic generation and probe amplification. Specific experimental parameters are not provided in the abstract.

Abstract: Spatiotemporal photonic crystals offer a route to controlling the frequency, momentum, and energy of light. Extending this capability to relativistic laser intensities calls for a plasma-based platform. Here we propose a spatiotemporal plasma photonic crystal (SPPC) formed at a solid surface driven by intense pump pulses, with a separate probe interacting with the resulting modulation. The spatial and temporal periodicity of the SPPC determines the allowed harmonic frequencies and emission angles, while its complex Floquet bands explain how the probe draws energy from the modulation. Beyond harmonic generation, this picture predicts wavelength-dependent probe amplification as the probe accesses amplifying modes of the SPPC, providing an experimentally testable connection between the band structure and energy transfer. Agreement with particle-in-cell simulations supports this interpretation.

</details>


### [4] [Radial Evolution of Correlation Anisotropy in PUNCH Observations of the Young Solar Wind](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29573)
*Zhuoran Gao, Yan Yang, Francesco Pecora, Rohit Chhiber, Riley Amos, Steven R. Cranmer, Jiaming Wang, William H. Matthaeus, Craig E. DeForest, Sarah E. Gibson, Nicholeen Viall, Chris R. Gilly, Ronnie Killough, Jillian Redfern, William L. Wells, Matthew N. Beasley, Glenn T. Laurent, Robin C. Colaninno, Nick R. Waltham, J. Marcus Hughes, Daniel B. Seaton, Samuel Van Kooten, Ritesh Patel, Chris Lowder, Derek Lamb*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: Using PUNCH wide-field, high-cadence polarized white-light images, the paper tracks the radial evolution of solar wind density anisotropy via a two-dimensional correlation function. Correlation contours become approximately circular near 100 solar radii, while from 30 to 60 solar radii they extend preferentially along the radial direction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Solar wind turbulence exhibits wavevector and variance anisotropy under the mean magnetic field, large-scale shear, and expansion. The transition from the magnetically controlled outer corona to the less constrained solar wind may drive fluctuations toward isotropy, so the study aims to confirm and extend earlier in-situ and STEREO-based evidence for anisotropy evolution with heliocentric distance using PUNCH observations.

Method: The authors compute a two-dimensional correlation function C(l_perp, l_parallel) of polarized white-light brightness as a function of radial and transverse scales, using PUNCH wide-field, high-cadence imaging to infer the nature of density anisotropy and its spatial development at different heliospheric positions.

Result: At larger heliocentric distances of about 100 solar radii, circular correlation-function contours indicate a transition toward approximate isotropy. Between 30 and 60 solar radii, the correlation structure preferentially extends along the radial direction, which may imply possible dominance of two-dimensional variation in the transverse plane.

Conclusion: PUNCH's very large field of view reveals a fundamental transition of solar wind turbulence toward isotropy with increasing heliocentric distance, confirming and expanding earlier indications of radial anisotropy evolution.

Abstract: Solar wind turbulence, evolving in the presence of a mean magnetic field, large-scale shear, and expansion, exhibits several types of anisotropy, including wavevector anisotropy and variance anisotropy. As the solar wind expands from the corona into the interplanetary space, the magnetically controlled outer corona transitions into the dynamic and less constrained solar wind, and fluctuations may evolve toward isotropy. This is suggestive of an evolution of anisotropy with distance from the Sun, a feature observed in earlier results using surveys of in-situ data, as well as more limited images of solar wind density from STEREO. Here we confirm and expand on those results employing data from the PUNCH mission, whose wide-field, high-cadence imaging enables anisotropy tracking at different heliospheric positions. We evaluate a two-dimensional (2D) correlation function $C(\ell_{\perp}, \ell_{\parallel})$ of polarized white-light brightness as a function of radial $\ell_\parallel$ and transverse $\ell_\perp$ scales to infer the nature of density anisotropy and its development in space. At larger heliocentric distances ($\sim 100 R_\odot$), circular contours of the correlation function indicate a transition toward approximate isotropy. The correlation structure at heliocentric distances from $30R_\odot$ to $60R_\odot$ preferentially extends along the radial direction, which might imply the possible dominance of the two-dimensional variation in the transverse plane. In this way, the very large field of view of PUNCH images reveal a fundamental transition of solar wind turbulence toward isotropy.

</details>


### [5] [A novel mechanism for coherent radio emission: particle-in-cell simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29352)
*Xiaowei Zhou, Dejin Wu, Ling Chen, Zongjun Ning*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The paper uses 2.5D particle-in-cell simulations to study the nonlinear evolution of the moderately relativistic electron bi-stream instability in charge- and current-neutralized magnetized plasmas, focusing on wave excitation, wave-particle interactions, non-Maxwellian features, and anisotropic electron heating, with possible implications for coherent radio emission.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Electron phase-space holes are known to arise from nonlinear relaxation of the electron beam instability, but the excitation of electromagnetic waves associated with these holes is still not fully understood. The study aims to clarify this nonlinear evolution and its potential relevance to coherent radio emission in astrophysical plasmas.

Method: The authors perform 2.5-dimensional particle-in-cell simulations to systematically investigate the nonlinear evolution of the moderately relativistic electron bi-stream instability, a special case of the electron beam instability with equal densities of two counter-propagating energetic electron beams, in charge- and current-neutralized plasmas under weak and strong magnetization.

Result: The simulations provide new insights into wave excitation, nonlinear wave-particle interactions, the formation of non-Maxwellian features, and anisotropic electron heating in magnetized plasmas. The abstract does not provide quantitative results or direct observational confirmation.

Conclusion: The study proposes a simulation-based mechanism with potential implications for coherent radio emission in astrophysical plasmas, such as solar zebra-pattern bursts, but the abstract does not establish a definitive causal link or quantitative emission predictions.

Abstract: It has been established that electron (phase-space) holes, i.e., EHs, can arise from the nonlinear relaxation of the electron beam instability. Excitation of electromagnetic waves related to the EHs is, however, still not fully understood. Using 2.5-dimensional particle-in-cell simulations, we systematically investigate the nonlinear evolution of the (moderately relativistic) electron bi-stream instability (a special case of the electron beam instability where the densities of the two counter-propagating energetic electron beams are equivalent) in charge- and current-neutralized plasmas under weak and strong magnetizations. This study provides new insights into the wave excitation, the nonlinear wave-particle interactions, the formation of non-Maxwellian features, and the anisotropic electron heating in magnetized plasmas, with potential implications for coherent radio emission in astrophysical plasmas (e.g., solar zebra-pattern bursts).

</details>


### [6] [Electron Parameters in H2:Ar:CH4 Plasma Maintained by Microwave and Laser Radiation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29229)
*Svetlana Avtaeva, Alexey Medvedev*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: Electron density and temperature were measured by optical emission spectroscopy in an atmospheric-pressure 95H2:3Ar:2CH4 plasma maintained by 2.47 GHz microwave radiation and 10.6 μm CO2 laser pulses. The authors describe the hybrid plasma as two states: a dense, hotter state during laser pulses and a lower-density, cooler state between pulses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The abstract does not state an explicit motivation beyond characterizing electron parameters in a microwave- and laser-maintained atmospheric-pressure hybrid plasma and simplifying its description through the concept of two plasma states caused by pulsed laser energy injection.

Method: The plasma was generated in a quasi-cylindrical reactor serving as a resonator for TM011-mode pulsed-periodic 2.47 GHz microwave radiation, with CO2 laser radiation at 10.6 μm. Hα and Hβ emission lines were analyzed using a dual-Lorentzian approximation; Stark broadening was used to measure electron density, and the Hα/Hβ intensity ratio was used to estimate electron temperature. A two-state description was introduced to represent the hybrid plasma during and between laser pulses.

Result: In the hybrid state during the laser pulse, electron density was (1–2.5)×10^17 cm^-3 and electron temperature was ~0.5–0.6 eV. Between laser pulses, electron density was 1.8×10^14–5×10^15 cm^-3, more than an order of magnitude lower than during the pulse but 2–10 times higher than in microwave plasma at (1–5)×10^14 cm^-3; electron temperature in this state was 0.16–0.24 eV. The dense plasma region with electron density ~10^17 cm^-3 had an estimated radius of ~0.5–1 mm.

Conclusion: The hybrid plasma can be simplified as two states associated with pulsed laser energy injection. Laser pulses produce a strongly increased electron density and higher electron temperature, while between pulses the electron density remains above that of microwave-only plasma; the high-density region is spatially small.

Abstract: Electron parameters in an atmospheric-pressure hybrid plasma in the 95H2:3Ar:2CH4 mixture maintained by pulsed-periodic microwave radiation (2.47 GHz) and CO2 laser radiation (10.6 μm) were studied. The plasma was generated in a quasi-cylindrical reactor, which was a resonator for TM011-mode microwave radiation. It was shown that in the hybrid plasma emission spectra the Hα and H{\betta} lines are strongly broadened and can be approximated by the sum of two Lorentzian contours (dual-Lorentzian approximation). The Stark broadening of the Hα and H{\betta} lines was used to measure the electron density in the plasma, and the ratio of their intensities allowed to estimate the electron temperature. To simplify the description of the hybrid plasma properties the concept of two plasma states caused by the pulsed injection of laser energy into the plasma was introduced. The hybrid plasma state, corresponding to the plasma state during the laser pulse, is characterized by a high electron density of (1-2.5)*10^17 cm-3 and an electron temperature of ~0.5-0.6 eV. The electron density in the state corresponding to the time intervals between laser pulses (1.8*10^14-5*10^15 cm-3) is more than an order of magnitude lower than the electron density in the first state, but it is 2-10 times higher than the electron density in microwave plasma ((1-5)*10^14 cm-3). The electron temperature in this state is 0.16-0.24 eV. The radius of the dense plasma region with an electron density of ~10^17 cm-3 is estimated to be ~0.5-1 mm.

</details>


### [7] [Formation of Multiple BGK-like Structures in the Time-asymptotic State of Collisionless Vlasov-Poisson Plasmas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28898)
*Hugo A. Carril, Jorge A. Gidi, Roberto E. Navarro, Jaime A. Araneda*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: High-resolution Vlasov-Poisson simulations show that a finite-amplitude perturbation in a collisionless Maxwellian plasma evolves into multiple vortex-like BGK-like structures that fill phase space and reduce filamentation, with power-law spectra indicating energy transfer to smaller spatial and velocity scales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The abstract does not explicitly state the motivation, but it implies a need to test and refine the common picture that the time-asymptotic state is a steady nonlinear superposition of two counter-propagating BGK modes.

Method: The authors use high-resolution Vlasov-Poisson simulations of a finite-amplitude perturbation in a collisionless Maxwellian plasma, without external forcing or an energetic plasma population.

Result: The plasma self-consistently evolves into a time-asymptotic state containing multiple vortex-like structures that gradually fill phase space and reduce filamentation. The electric field and space-averaged particle distribution function exhibit a power-law broad spectrum, consistent with an energy cascade toward smaller scales in both position and velocity space.

Conclusion: The time-asymptotic regime is better described as a nonlinear superposition of multiple BGK-like modes associated with nearly constant phase-speed waves rather than solely two counter-propagating BGK modes.

Abstract: The time-asymptotic state of a finite-amplitude perturbation in a collisionless and Maxwellian plasma is typically represented as a steady state of two non-linearly superposed, counter-propagating Bernstein-Greene-Kruskal (BGK) modes. Using high-resolution Vlasov-Poisson simulations, we show that the plasma evolves self-consistently into a time-asymptotic state of multiple vortex-like structures that gradually fill the phase-space and reduce filamentation. This occurs without the need for external forcing or the presence of an energetic plasma population. This finding suggests that the time-asymptotic regime of the plasma is rather akin to a non-linear superposition of multiple BGK-like modes associated with nearly constant phase-speed waves. The electric field and the space-averaged particle distribution function exhibit a power-law broad spectrum, which is consistent with an energy cascade towards smaller scales in both position and velocity spaces.

</details>


### [8] [Statistical Signatures of Velocity-Space Turbulence in the Earth's Magnetosheath](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28546)
*Tien Vo, Robert Ergun, Alexandros Chasapis, Oreste Pezzi, Andrea Larosa, Sergio Servidio, Luca Sorriso-Valvo, Viet Doan, Paul Cassak, Trevor Bowen, Yi Qi*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: Using Magnetospheric Multiscale measurements in Earth's magnetosheath, this study provides the first statistical evidence for velocity-space turbulence signatures in collisionless plasma ion distributions, identifying isotropic -3/2 and anisotropic -2 spectral populations organized by ion beta and bulk flow, with velocity-space spectral indices correlated with real-space energy transfer rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Collisionless plasma particle distributions are often far from local thermodynamic equilibrium and contain free energy that drives complex velocity-space dynamics. A velocity-space cascade analogous to spatial turbulence has been proposed, but observational evidence across phase space remains limited.

Method: The authors performed a statistical study using Magnetospheric Multiscale mission measurements from a special campaign in Earth's magnetosheath, analyzing ion velocity-space spectra across a wide range of plasma conditions and comparing them with spatial spectral properties and estimated real-space energy transfer rates.

Result: Two main ion velocity-space spectral populations were observed: an isotropic -3/2 spectrum and an anisotropic -2 spectrum organized by ion beta and bulk flow. Spatial and velocity-domain spectral power laws were uncorrelated, while the velocity-space spectral index was correlated with estimated real-space energy transfer rates.

Conclusion: Ion spectral properties are systematically organized by plasma conditions and linked to real-space turbulence, with implications for turbulence and the energy partition between thermal and non-thermal particles in space and astrophysical plasmas.

Abstract: In collisionless plasmas, the particle distributions are often far from local thermodynamic equilibrium, providing a significant source of free energy that leads to complex velocity-space dynamics. In turbulent plasmas, the possibility of a cascade of the free energy in velocity space, analogous to the spatial cascade of fluid and electromagnetic field energies, has been suggested. However, observational evidence of plasma turbulence throughout phase space remains limited. Using measurements from the Magnetospheric Multiscale mission in a special campaign in the Earth's magnetosheath, we present the first statistical study of signatures consistent with velocity-space turbulence across a wide range of plasma conditions. We observe two main populations in the ion velocity-space spectrum -- an isotropic $-3/2$ spectrum and an anisotropic $-2$ spectrum organized by the ion $β$ and bulk flow. In addition, the spectral power laws in the spatial and velocity domains are uncorrelated, while the velocity-space spectral index is correlated with estimated real-space energy transfer rates. These results reveal how the ion spectral properties are systematically organized by plasma conditions and related to real-space turbulence. This connection has broad implications for turbulence and the energy partition between thermal and non-thermal particles in space and astrophysical plasmas.

</details>


### [9] [CNN-Based Locked Mode Detection and Prospects for Prediction Utilizing Density Fluctuation Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28266)
*Randall Clark, Maxim Nurgaliev, Georgy Subbotin, Jie Chen, Terry L. Rhodes, Dmitri M. Orlov*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: A CNN using Radial Interferometer Polarimeter density-fluctuation signals was trained to detect rotating MHD modes that lock in tokamak plasmas; a second-stage filter improved locked-mode detection to 88.5% with 83.7% precision, while revealing limits of fluctuation-based prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Locked modes can degrade tokamak confinement and cause disruptions, threatening sustained operation of fusion pilot plants. Conventional magnetic diagnostics may not survive reactor conditions and are delayed by magnetic diffusion, motivating a non-magnetic, fluctuation-based alternative for identifying mode slowing and locking.

Method: The study used the Radial Interferometer Polarimeter as an alternative diagnostic for observing deceleration of rotating MHD modes into locked modes. Labels were constructed to train a CNN to distinguish modes that lock from those that approach locking but do not lock. A second-stage detection method applied persistence and classification-margin filters to the CNN output. The approach excludes born-locked modes because it relies on prior mode rotation.

Result: The trained CNN learned to identify low-frequency, descending MHD modes but struggled to distinguish modes that would continue toward locking from those that would not. The second-stage detection method detected 88.5% of locked modes with 83.7% precision.

Conclusion: An FPP-relevant, non-magnetic diagnostic can accurately detect locked modes using density fluctuation signals, but fluctuation-based prediction has fundamental limitations in achievable prediction time and is restricted to locked modes with prior rotation.

Abstract: The deceleration of Magnetohydrodynamic (MHD) modes in tokamak plasmas is an observed behavior commonly associated with growing mode amplitude. As rotating MHD modes decelerate to a non-rotating state, they can form Locked Modes (LMs), which can degrade plasma confinement and cause disruptions. LMs therefore pose a serious threat to the sustained operation of otherwise-stable plasmas in Fusion Pilot Plants (FPPs). Identification of MHD modes and any subsequent locking is typically performed with an array of magnetic signals; these magnetic signals may not survive in the harsh reactor environment of an FPP and external magnetic measurements are delayed by magnetic diffusion through the tokamak shielding. This work uses the Radial Interferometer Polarimeter (RIP) as an alternative diagnostic to identify the slowing of rotating MHD modes into LMs. Because this fluctuation-based approach relies on observing prior mode rotation, detection and prediction are restricted to LMs with prior rotation and exclude born-locked modes. Labels were constructed to train a Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) to distinguish MHD modes that lock from those that may appear to approach a locking state but ultimately do not lock. The trained CNN learned to identify low-frequency, descending MHD modes but struggled to distinguish modes that would continue toward locking from those that would not. A second-stage detection method significantly improved performance, detecting 88.5% of LMs with a precision of 83.7% by applying persistence and classification-margin filters to the CNN output. These results demonstrate the capability of an FPP-relevant, non-magnetic diagnostic for accurate LM detection while also identifying fundamental limitations in the achievable prediction time using a fluctuation-based approach.

</details>


### [10] [Bypassing the Nanoampere Limit: A Thick-Target Methodology for Ultra-Low-Energy Resonances in the $^{11}$B(p,$α$)2$α$ Reaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30439)
*Xu-Yang Luo, Wen-Qing Wei, Shi-zheng Zhang, Wen Jiang, Bu-bo Ma, Zi-qian Zhao, Zhao Wang, Guo-Dong Wang, Lu-Lin Shi, Rui Cheng, Yan-Shi Zhang, Di Luo, Xiao Xiao, Guan-Chao Zhao, Bing Liu, Dieter H. H. Hoffmann, Jie-Ru Ren, Yong-Tao Zhao*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a correction-free thick-target method for measuring the ultra-low-energy cross section and resonances of the 11B(p,α)2α reaction at microampere beam currents, validated at the 148 keV resonance and applied in preliminary searches below 100 keV.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Precise low-energy cross-section measurements for 11B(p,α)2α are important for nuclear astrophysics and fusion energy research, but the extremely small cross section makes conventional thin-target experiments impractical because nanoampere beam-current limits produce impractically low count rates at ultra-low proton energies.

Method: A correction-free thick-target methodology operating at 20–60 microamperes is used, with Monte Carlo simulations of full reaction kinematics to determine the absolute reaction yield without empirical normalization factors. The work also compares measured reaction probability with a theoretical extrapolation to probe resonances below 100 keV.

Result: The method provides a gain of over three orders of magnitude in beam current. At the 148 keV center-of-mass resonance, the extracted resonance energy is 148.0 keV and the level width is 5.3 keV, in good agreement with published data. Preliminary measurements at 75 keV and 100 keV show no evidence for additional low-energy resonances.

Conclusion: The work provides a robust new methodology for measuring cross sections and resonance parameters of the p11B reaction and an efficient tool for systematic resonance searches in the ultra-low-energy regime.

Abstract: Precise measurements of the low-energy cross section for the $^{11}$B(p,$α$)2$α$ reaction is critical for nuclear astrophysics and fusion energy research, yet such measurements are experimentally demanding due to the reaction's extremely small cross section. Conventional thin-target experiments are fundamentally limited to nanoampere beam currents, which restrict count rates to impractical levels at ultra-low proton energies. To overcome this limitation, we report a correction-free thick-target methodology capable of operating at beam currents of 20-60 microamperes, corresponding to a gain of over three orders of magnitude. Monte Carlo simulations of the full reaction kinematics are employed to determine the absolute reaction yield, eliminating the requirement for empirical normalization factors. At the well-known 148~keV (c.m.) resonance peak, our extracted resonance energy ($E_{\mathrm{r}} = 148.0$~keV) and level width ($Γ_{\mathrm{cm}} = 5.3$~keV) are demonstrated in good agreement with published data. A scheme via comparing the measured reaction probability with a theoretical extrapolation result is furtherly presented to probe low-energy resonances below 100~keV region. Preliminary measurements at proton energies of 75~keV and 100~keV reveal no evidence for additional low-energy resonances. This work provides a robust new methodology to measure cross section and resonance parameters for the p$^{11}$B reaction and offers a highly efficient tool for systematic resonance searches in the ultra-low-energy regime.

</details>


### [11] [High-rate accumulation of ${8.9\times10^9}$ positrons using a linac-driven buffer-gas trap combined with a SiC remoderator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30238)
*P. Adrich, I. Belosevic, M. Chung, P. Cladé, P. Comini, P. Crivelli, P. Debu, A. Douillet, S. Geffroy, S. Guellati-Khelifa, P. Guichard, P-A. Hervieux, L. Hilico, P. Indelicato, S. Jonsell, J-P. Karr, B. Kim, S. Kim, E-S. Kim, N. Kuroda, B. Lee, L. Liszkay, D. Lunney, G. Manfredi, B. Mansoulié, V. Martimort, M. Matusiak, S. Niang, V. Nesvizhevsky, F. Nez, K. Park, N. Paul, E. Perez, P. Pérez, C. Regenfus, C. Roumegou, J-Y. Roussé, Y. Sacquin, F. Schmidt-Kaler, K. Szymczyk, T. A. Tanaka, B. Tuchming, B. Vallage, D. P. van der Werf, D. Won, S. Wronka, P. Yzombard*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: A linac-driven positron source combined with a modified buffer-gas trap, a SiC remoderator, and a high-field Penning-Malmberg accumulator captured (8.9 ± 0.9)×10^9 positrons in under 35 minutes at 4.5×10^6 e+/s, more than doubling the previous trapped-positron population and substantially increasing the accumulation rate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The work aims to provide large, high-rate positron populations for creating dense positronium targets, which would be used to produce keV-antihydrogen beams by charge exchange with antiprotons from the CERN AD/ELENA facility.

Method: The system combines a low-energy electron linac, a modified Surko-type buffer-gas trap enhanced by a silicon carbide remoderator, and a high-field Penning-Malmberg trap operated as a positron accumulator. Time-resolved annihilation measurements were used to study capture and cooling dynamics, and CO2 and SF6 were compared as buffer gases.

Result: The system accumulated (8.9 ± 0.9)×10^9 positrons in less than 35 minutes, with an average accumulation rate of 4.5×10^6 e+/s. This exceeds the largest previously reported trapped-positron population by more than a factor of two and was achieved at an 18-fold higher average accumulation rate. The SiC-remoderator-based scheme more than doubled trapping efficiency and contributed to a more than threefold increase in accumulation rate versus the original configuration; trapping saturation occurred at lower gas pressure with SF6 than with CO2.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate high-rate positron accumulation with a linac-driven buffer-gas trap and SiC remoderator, enabling larger trapped positron clouds intended for dense positronium targets and keV-antihydrogen production. The abstract does not provide details of the resulting antihydrogen beam performance.

Abstract: We report the accumulation of $(8.9 \pm 0.9)\times10^9$ positrons in less than 35 minutes in a Penning--Malmberg trap connected to the GBAR electron-linac-based positron source. To our knowledge, this exceeds the largest previously reported trapped-positron population by more than a factor of two and was achieved at an 18-fold higher average accumulation rate of $4.5\times10^6$~e$^+$/s. The accumulated positron clouds are intended for the creation of dense positronium targets to produce keV-antihydrogen beams by charge exchange with antiprotons delivered by the CERN AD/ELENA facility. The system combines a low-energy electron linac, a modified Surko-type buffer-gas trap enhanced by a silicon carbide remoderator, and a high-field Penning--Malmberg trap operated as a positron accumulator. Time-resolved annihilation measurements provide insight into the capture and cooling dynamics, while comparisons of CO$_2$ and SF$_6$ show that the trapping signal reaches saturation at lower gas pressures with SF$_6$. Measurements show that the SiC-remoderator-based trapping scheme more than doubles the trapping efficiency, contributing to a more than threefold increase in the positron accumulation rate compared with the original configuration.

</details>


### [12] [Plasma-enabled magnet-free LSW search for eV-scale axions with laser-stimulated regeneration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30098)
*Xiangyan An, Min Chen, Yipeng Wu, Boyuan Li, Wenchao Yan, Peng Yuan, Binbin Yan, Feng Liu, Zhengming Sheng, Jie Zhang*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: Proposes a magnet-free light-shining-through-a-wall search for 0.1-1 eV axions using plasma-based axion generation and laser-stimulated regeneration; particle-in-cell simulations indicate broad mass coverage at relativistic laser intensity, with projected axion-photon coupling limits around 10^-7 GeV^-1.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional light-shining-through-a-wall experiments rely on strong magnetic fields to mediate photon-axion conversion and reconversion. At eV-scale axion masses, photon-axion phase mismatch causes these conversions to lose coherence and substantially reduces experimental sensitivity.

Method: The paper proposes a magnet-free light-shining-through-a-wall scheme using plasma-based axion generation and laser-stimulated regeneration. At low laser intensity, a two-color dispersion-matched source generates axions in a narrow mass range set by plasma dispersion. At relativistic laser intensity, a single-pulse plasma-broadened source generates axions over a broad mass range, as shown by particle-in-cell simulations; the Fourier spectrum of the E·B source term broadens around the laser branch, consistent with mixing between the laser and low-frequency plasma waves. For detection behind the wall, a counter-propagating laser stimulates axion decay into a signal photon and a second photon emitted into the laser mode, with the laser frequency chosen to satisfy phase matching.

Result: For representative parameters, requiring at least one regenerated photon yields projected upper limits on the axion-photon coupling of order 10^-7 GeV^-1 over m_a = 0.1-1 eV.

Conclusion: The proposed plasma-enabled, magnet-free light-shining-through-a-wall scheme addresses the eV-scale phase-mismatch limitation of conventional magnetic LSW searches and provides projected sensitivity to axion-photon coupling over 0.1-1 eV. The abstract reports projections and simulations, not an experimental measurement.

Abstract: In conventional light-shining-through-a-wall (LSW) experiments, strong magnetic fields mediate the conversion of photons into axions and their subsequent reconversion into photons. At eV-scale axion masses, however, photon--axion phase mismatch causes these conversions to lose coherence, substantially reducing the experimental sensitivity. We propose a magnet-free LSW scheme that addresses this limitation via plasma-based axion generation and laser-stimulated regeneration. At low laser intensity, a two-color dispersion-matched source generates axions in a narrow mass range set by the plasma dispersion. At relativistic laser intensity, a single-pulse plasma-broadened source generates axions over a broad mass range, as shown by particle-in-cell simulations. The Fourier spectrum of the source term $\mathbf{E}\cdot\mathbf{B}$ broadens around the laser branch, consistent with mixing between the laser and low-frequency plasma waves. For axion detection behind the wall, a counter-propagating laser stimulates axion decay into a detectable signal photon and a second photon emitted into the laser mode, with the laser frequency chosen to satisfy phase matching. For representative parameters, the requirement of at least one regenerated photon yields projected upper limits on the axion-photon coupling on the order $10^{-7}\,\mathrm{GeV}^{-1}$ over $m_a=0.1$--$1\,\mathrm{eV}$.

</details>

### [13] [Optimization of Piezo Operation for Superconducting TESLA Cavities at EuXFEL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31086)
*M. Grecki, J. Branlard, M. Diomede, M. Herrmann, C. Schmidt*

Main category: physics.acc-ph

TL;DR: The paper examines optimization of piezoelectric actuator excitation for tuning superconducting TESLA cavities at EuXFEL, aiming to maintain effective Lorentz Force Detuning compensation while reducing voltage, current, and current slope to improve efficiency, reliability, and machine stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-Q superconducting cavities must be precisely tuned to the RF frequency to ensure stable electromagnetic fields and minimize RF power consumption. At EuXFEL, fast piezoelectric tuners compensate disturbances such as Lorentz Force Detuning, but the piezos require high-voltage and high-current drive signals and are vulnerable to overvoltage, overcurrent, and overheating. Protection is critical because replacing damaged piezos in fully assembled modules is infeasible, and piezo-induced vibrations can affect machine stability.

Method: The paper explores optimization of piezo excitation at EuXFEL by reducing voltage, current, and current slope while ensuring effective Lorentz Force Detuning compensation. It details the optimization methods and results, with applicability to other facilities using superconducting cavities.

Result: The abstract states that methods and results for optimized piezo excitation at EuXFEL are detailed and are applicable to other superconducting-cavity facilities. No specific quantitative performance results are provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: Optimizing piezo excitation can improve efficiency, reliability, and machine stability while preserving effective Lorentz Force Detuning compensation. The approach is presented as applicable to other facilities with superconducting cavities; specific quantitative conclusions are not provided in the abstract.

Abstract: Superconducting cavities with high-Q factor require precise tuning to match the RF frequency, ensuring stable electromagnetic fields and minimizing RF power consumption. At the European XFEL (EuXFEL) accelerator, TESLA cavities are tuned using slow tuners (step motors) for coarse adjustments and fast tuners (piezoelectric actuators) for fine-tuning and compensating disturbances such as Lorentz Force Detuning (LFD). Piezo actuators, critical to this system, require high-voltage (up to 100V) and high-current (up to 1A) driving signals for effective LFD compensation. However, they are vulnerable to overvoltage, overcurrent, and overheating, and their protection is crucial since replacing damaged piezos in fully assembled modules is infeasible. Additionally, piezo-induced vibrations can affect the machine's stability. Optimizing piezo excitation, by reducing voltage, current, and current slope while ensuring effective LFD compensation, improves equally efficiency, reliability and machine stability. This paper explores the optimization of piezo excitation at EuXFEL, detailing methods and results applicable to other facilities with superconducting cavities as well.

</details>


### [14] [Initial Field Test of a Quadrupedal Robot at SLAC Beam Dump East (BDE): A Sandbox for Robotics in Accelerator Operations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29639)
*M. Bai, M. A. Montironi, A. Saad, T. Thayer*

Main category: physics.acc-ph

TL;DR: This paper reports an initial field test of a Boston Dynamics SPOT quadrupedal robot with a SPOT Arm payload at SLAC's Beam Dump East facility to assess the technical feasibility and operational considerations of field robotics in accelerator environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Field robotics could improve operational efficiency, facility sustainability, and reduce personnel exposure to radiation and other hazards in large particle accelerator facilities, but adoption has been cautious due to cost versus return on investment, equipment collision risk, radiation tolerance, and poor network connectivity in enclosed accelerator areas.

Method: The authors conducted a test drive of a Boston Dynamics SPOT quadrupedal robot equipped with a SPOT Arm payload at SLAC's Beam Dump East facility and evaluated the technical feasibility and operational considerations of deploying quadrupedal field robotics within accelerator environments.

Result: The paper presents findings from the test drive and reports practical challenges, including protective suits that impede navigation sensors and low-light infrared navigation in unlit spaces. Quantitative performance results are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: The test provides initial operational experience for quadrupedal field robotics in accelerator facilities and outlines potential development pathways toward adaptive learning and autonomous execution for future routine field robotics operations.

Abstract: Deploying field robotics in large scale particle accelerator facilities offers significant potential to enhance operational efficiency, promote facility sustainability, and reduce personnel exposure to radiation and other hazards. As key underlying technologies mature, field robotics are seeing broader deployment across various industrial sectors. However, accelerator facilities have remained cautious in adopting these systems, primarily due to concerns regarding cost versus return on investment and the risk of equipment collisions. In addition, operational experience with SLAC's Remotely Operated Accelerator Monitoring platform, ROAM highlighted critical technical challenges, including radiation tolerance and poor network connectivity in enclosed accelerator areas. This paper presents the findings from a recent test drive of a Boston Dynamics SPOT quadrupedal robot equipped with a SPOT Arm payload conducted at SLAC's Beam Dump East facility. We evaluate the technical feasibility and operational considerations of deploying quadrupedal field robotics within accelerator environments. Furthermore, we report key practical challenges, such as protective suits that impede navigation sensors and low light infrared navigation in unlit spaces. Finally, we outline potential development pathways toward adaptive learning and autonomous execution for future routine field robotics operations in accelerator facilities.

</details>


### [15] [A muonium laser ionization system for muon acceleration studies at J-PARC](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30375)
*Shusei Kamioka, Meng Lyu, Katsuyoshi Homma, Tsutomu Mibe, Shinki Nakamura, Yu Oishi, Koichiro Shimomura, Tatsuya Shinozaki, Hikaru Sunagawa, Satoshi Wada, Takumi Yamada, Takayuki Yamazaki*

Main category: physics.acc-ph

TL;DR: A 122-nm/355-nm laser ionization system for thermal muonium was developed and commissioned at J-PARC, producing ultraslow muons for subsequent radio-frequency acceleration studies; muonium ionization was confirmed with a 5.7 keV muon transport line at approximately 6 muons per second.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Muon accelerator studies require an ultraslow muon source, which can be provided by laser ionization of thermal muonium atoms prior to radio-frequency acceleration.

Method: The authors developed and commissioned a muonium ionization laser system in the H2 area of the J-PARC Materials and Life Science Experimental Facility. It generates coherent 122-nm pulses for muonium excitation and 355-nm pulses for photoionization, together with a vacuum-ultraviolet pulse transport system and vacuum-ultraviolet pulse diagnostics. Ionization was tested using a 5.7 keV muon transport line.

Result: Muonium ionization was confirmed, and a muon rate of approximately 6 s^-1 was observed using the 5.7 keV muon transport line.

Conclusion: The ionization system is operational and provides a platform for subsequent muon accelerator studies.

Abstract: This paper reports the development and commissioning of a muonium ionization laser system installed in the H2 area of the J-PARC Materials and Life Science Experimental Facility for muon accelerator studies. In this accelerator scheme, ultraslow muons generated by laser ionization of thermal muonium atoms serve as the particle source for subsequent radio-frequency acceleration. The laser system generates coherent 122-nm pulses for muonium excitation and 355-nm pulses for photoionization of the excited muonium. A vacuum-ultraviolet (VUV) pulse transport system and VUV pulse diagnostics were also developed. Muonium ionization was confirmed using a 5.7~keV muon transport line, and a muon rate of approximately 6~s$^{-1}$ was observed. These results establish operation of the ionization system and provide a platform for subsequent muon accelerator studies.

</details>


### [16] [Towards Automatic Ion Identification in Storage Rings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28070)
*Anna Jule Heß, David Freire-Fernández, Moritz Porstendörfer, Wolfram Korten, Yury A. Litvinov*

Main category: physics.acc-ph

TL;DR: A proof-of-concept automated harmonic de-overlapping method for resonant Schottky spectra in heavy-ion storage rings exploits smoothly varying peak profiles versus mass-to-charge ratio. Applied to a 245 MHz spectrum from 11,452 injections at GSI's Experimental Storage Ring, it organizes 16 of 32 detected structures into five candidate families and reconstructs their fundamental-frequency representation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Resonant Schottky detectors enable non-destructive, single-ion-sensitive measurements of revolution frequencies and intensities, but adjacent harmonic ranges can overlap within the detector band. Frequency alone is therefore insufficient to determine an ion's mass-to-charge ratio, and automated methods are needed to de-overlap harmonics and provide initial anchors for ion identification.

Method: The method uses an empirical ansatz in combined Schottky and Isochronous Mass Spectrometry: for a fixed ion-optical setting, each peak corresponding to an ion species with a given mass-to-charge ratio has a characteristic profile that evolves smoothly with changing mass-to-charge ratio. The same local structure at different harmonics should retain nearly identical shapes after frequency rescaling. The approach was applied to a 245 MHz spectrum accumulated from 11,452 injections at the Experimental Storage Ring at GSI, organizing detected structures into candidate families and reconstructing their fundamental-frequency representation.

Result: The method organized 16 of 32 detected structures into five candidate families and reconstructed their fundamental-frequency representation. This reduced the complex mixed-harmonic spectrum to a smaller candidate set, addressing the challenging task of finding initial anchors for ion identification.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates a proof-of-concept for automated harmonic de-overlapping in Schottky spectra from heavy-ion storage rings. By exploiting spectral shapes and frequency rescaling, it provides candidate families and initial anchors for ion identification; the abstract does not report full automatic identification of all ions or further validation beyond this proof-of-concept.

Abstract: Resonant Schottky detectors provide non-destructive, single-ion-sensitive measurements of revolution frequencies and intensities of ion species stored in a heavy-ion storage ring. These high-\(Q\) cavities allow analysis of high-order harmonics of the revolution frequencies to magnify absolute frequency separations. Adjacent harmonic ranges can, however, overlap within the detector band, making frequency alone insufficient for determining the ion's mass-to-charge ratio $(m/q)$. We present a proof-of-concept for an automated harmonic de-overlapping method using spectral shapes in combined Schottky and Isochronous Mass Spectrometry. The empirical ansatz is that, for a fixed ion-optical setting, each peak, corresponding to a specific ion species with given $m/q$ ratio, has a characteristic profile that evolves smoothly with changing $m/q$. Occurrences of the same local structure at different harmonics should therefore retain almost identical shapes after frequency rescaling. Applied to a 245\,MHz spectrum accumulated from 11\,452 injections at the Experimental Storage Ring at GSI, the method organizes 16 of 32 detected structures into five candidate families and reconstructs their fundamental-frequency representation. Reducing the complex mixed-harmonic spectrum to this smaller candidate set solves the challenging task of finding initial anchors for the ion identification.

</details>

### [17] [Geometry-conditioned differentiable surrogate with adjoint Monte Carlo sampling for scintillator detector co-design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30580)
*Lorenzo Pepa, Mohammad Ali Marashli, Nicolas Babio Elguero, Tommaso Dorigo, Irene Fagnani, Carlo Gulizia, Pablo Martinez Ruiz del Arbol, Fredrik Sandin, Pietro Vischia*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper develops a geometry-conditioned, natively differentiable neural surrogate for scintillation photon transport, trained using an adjoint Monte Carlo optical-response model, and validates it for light-guide geometry optimization in scintillator detector co-design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Repeated photon-transport evaluations for optimizing light-guide geometries in scintillator-based imaging detectors make direct Monte Carlo simulation computationally costly; differentiable surrogates can accelerate these evaluations and support detector readout co-design to improve information extraction from particle interactions.

Method: The authors start from a modular, event-driven Monte Carlo model of scintillation photon generation and transport and construct a reciprocal adjoint Monte Carlo model of a spatially and temporally resolved optical response field by launching weighted photon trajectories from the light-guide readout face. They apply this to a scintillator cell with two mirrored, curved, single-clad light guides and use the adjoint model to train a geometry-conditioned neural surrogate that predicts collection probabilities and conditional transport-time distributions across a constrained guide-design family. The surrogate is natively differentiable for gradient-based optimization.

Result: A forward-reciprocal benchmark and validation on held-out geometries established the trained model's accuracy and identified its limitations. The abstract does not provide quantitative performance metrics.

Conclusion: The developed tools provide a foundation for rapid optical-response studies and detector readout co-design.

Abstract: Optimizing light-guide geometries for scintillator-based imaging detectors requires repeated photon-transport evaluations, making direct Monte Carlo simulation computationally costly. Differentiable surrogates can accelerate these evaluations and support detector readout co-design aimed at improving information extraction from particle interactions. In this work we describe a surrogate model for photon transport. We develop it starting from a modular, event-driven Monte Carlo model for the generation and transport of scintillation photons, and a reciprocal Monte Carlo construction (an adjoint model) of a spatially and temporally resolved optical response field. The latter evaluates the collectible light output and its temporal structure from a scintillator volume by launching weighted photon trajectories from the light-guide readout face. We apply this approach to a scintillator cell with two mirrored, curved, single-clad light guides. We employ the adjoint model to train a geometry-conditioned neural surrogate that predicts collection probabilities and conditional transport-time distributions across a constrained guide-design family. The surrogate is natively differentiable, lending itself to be an element of a differentiable pipeline for gradient-based optimization. A forward-reciprocal benchmark and validation on held-out geometries establish the accuracy of the trained model and identify its limitations. The developed tools provide a foundation for rapid optical-response studies and detector readout co-design.

</details>


### [18] [SPECTRA-RCWA: A Spectral Propagation Engine for Coupled-Wave Theory with Recursive Admittance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30305)
*Hyoseok Park*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: SPECTRA-RCWA is a GPU forward and adjoint rigorous coupled-wave analysis solver that avoids diagonalizing each layer matrix, using matrix-product evaluations and admittance recursion or scattering-matrix doubling to enable fast, stable inverse design of thick metasurfaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RCWA is the standard full-wave solver for periodic metasurfaces, but each layer solve requires diagonalizing a dense nonsymmetric matrix. Inverse design repeats this for every wavelength, angle, and polarization, making it slow on GPUs; eigendecomposition-based gradients also fail at eigenvalue coincidences or diffraction-order cutoff, and truncated-series solvers lose accuracy in thick layers unless manually sliced.

Method: SPECTRA-RCWA never diagonalizes the layer matrix. Each layer step evaluates three entire functions of the half-size layer matrix using matrix products alone, while an admittance recursion, or scattering-matrix doubling for thick layers, stabilizes evanescent orders. A single adjoint field provides gradients for all design values without a computational graph, and all solves for an objective are batched.

Result: For 144 solves with 578 x 578 layer matrices, value and gradient computation takes 0.25 s on one GPU, 218 times less than a differentiable eigendecomposition code. It maintains single-precision accuracy in layers several wavelengths thick without changing settings and is 47 to 65 times faster than TorchRDIT at matched accuracy. Its single-precision gradient remains within 4e-5 of a double-precision reference at cutoff, where eigendecomposition gradients are wrong by order one. A freeform metagrating was designed in 3.4 min on a desktop processor, deflecting both polarizations by 65 degrees with 75% band-averaged efficiency.

Conclusion: SPECTRA-RCWA provides a fast, stable, GPU-accelerated RCWA forward and adjoint framework for metasurface inverse design, particularly for thick layers and cutoff conditions, and is demonstrated on a broadband freeform metagrating. The abstract does not report any connection to intense lasers or laser-plasma physics.

Abstract: Rigorous coupled-wave analysis (RCWA) is the standard full-wave solver for periodic metasurfaces, but every solve diagonalises a dense nonsymmetric matrix for each layer. Inverse design repeats this step for every wavelength, angle and polarisation of an objective. On a graphics processor it is the slow step, and its derivative fails where eigenvalues coincide or a diffraction order reaches cutoff. Here we introduce SPECTRA-RCWA, an RCWA forward and adjoint solver that never diagonalises the layer matrix. Each layer step uses three entire functions of the half-size layer matrix, evaluated by matrix products alone, and an admittance recursion, or scattering-matrix doubling for thick layers, keeps the evanescent orders stable. One adjoint field gives the gradient for every design value without a computational graph, and all solves of an objective run as one batch. For 144 solves with 578 x 578 layer matrices, the value and the gradient take 0.25 s on one graphics card, 218 times less than with a differentiable eigendecomposition code. The truncated-series solver TorchRDIT loses accuracy beyond a few tenths of a micrometre unless the user slices the layer; SPECTRA-RCWA keeps single-precision accuracy in layers several wavelengths thick with no setting changed and is 47 to 65 times faster at matched accuracy. Its single-precision gradient stays within 4e-5 of a double-precision reference at a cutoff, where eigendecomposition gradients are wrong by order one. A freeform metagrating designed in 3.4 min on a desktop processor deflects both polarisations by 65 degrees with a band-averaged efficiency of 75%.

</details>


### [19] [Localized Strain Tuning of Second-Harmonic Generation in MoS2 Monolayers and Bilayers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31097)
*Martín Aversa, Florencia Cammarata, Camila Borrazás, Nadia C. Vega, David Comedi, Andrea V. Bragas, Gustavo Grinblat*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: Localized AFM-tip deformation reversibly modulates second-harmonic generation in suspended CVD-grown MoS2 monolayers and bilayers, with an estimated sensitivity of about 12% SHG modulation per 1% effective strain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper investigates mechanical control of nonlinear optical emission in atomically thin MoS2 and aims to establish adhesion-mediated deformation as a distinct, reversible mechanism for tuning second-harmonic generation.

Method: CVD-grown MoS2 monolayers and few-layer flakes were suspended over microstructured Si3N4 substrates and locally deformed with an atomic force microscope tip. Force and SHG intensity were measured simultaneously during controlled approach and retraction cycles, and optical modulation was correlated with pull-off force and a phenomenological estimate of effective strain.

Result: Suspended regions showed enhanced SHG relative to substrate-supported regions, with pronounced suppression near clamping boundaries. SHG under compressive loading varied among individual membranes, whereas adhesion-mediated pulling during retraction consistently reduced SHG until tip detachment, after which the signal recovered. The characteristic sensitivity was approximately 12% SHG modulation per 1% effective strain, with comparable behavior in monolayers and bilayers.

Conclusion: Adhesion-mediated mechanical deformation is a distinct and reversible mechanism for controlling nonlinear optical emission in atomically thin MoS2 membranes.

Abstract: We investigate the modulation of second-harmonic generation (SHG) in CVD-grown MoS2 monolayers and few-layer flakes through localized mechanical deformation applied by an atomic force microscope (AFM) tip. MoS2 membranes suspended over microstructured Si3N4 substrates allow simultaneous measurements of force and SHG intensity during controlled approach and retraction cycles. Suspended regions exhibit enhanced SHG relative to substrate-supported regions, with pronounced suppression near the clamping boundaries. While the SHG response during compressive loading varies among individual membranes, adhesion-mediated pulling during retraction consistently reduces the SHG intensity until tip detachment, when the signal recovers. By correlating the optical modulation with the measured pull-off force and a phenomenological estimate of effective strain, we obtain a characteristic sensitivity of approximately 12% SHG modulation per 1% effective strain, with comparable behavior in monolayers and bilayers. These findings reveal adhesion-mediated mechanical deformation as a distinct and reversible mechanism for controlling nonlinear optical emission in atomically thin membranes.

</details>


### [20] [Programmable Broadband Polarization-Preserving Optical Delay Line with Faraday Mirrors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30814)
*Salem F. Hegazy, Salah S. A. Obayya, Bahaa E. A. Saleh*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: A programmable broadband optical delay line using cascaded switched single-mode fiber segments and Faraday mirrors achieves delays up to 1 μs with nanosecond resolution while preserving an arbitrary unknown input polarization, with numerical fidelity above 99% over a 50-nm wavelength range.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to provide a programmable broadband optical delay line that can generate delays up to 1 μs at nanosecond resolution without disturbing an arbitrary and unknown polarization state of the original light.

Method: The delay line uses bidirectional propagation through cascaded segments of standard single-mode fiber, with each segment selecting a short or long path via an optical switch, and terminal reflection by Faraday mirrors at both ends. Broadband operation was assessed by studying fiber and Faraday-mirror dispersion, supplemented by numerical experiments.

Result: The delay line achieved programmable delays up to 1 μs with nanosecond resolution. Numerical experiments showed mean output-polarization fidelity better than 99% over a 50-nm deviation from the central wavelength, often exceeding fidelity obtained without the fiber. The two-pass fiber propagation was found to mitigate dispersive distortion introduced by the Faraday mirror.

Conclusion: Faraday mirrors auto-compensate random fiber-induced polarization fluctuations and restore the initial polarization state, enabling broadband polarization-preserving programmable optical delay. The authors conclude that two-pass propagation in the fiber can surprisingly mitigate Faraday-mirror-induced dispersive distortion.

Abstract: We report a broadband optical delay line programmable for delays up to 1 μs with nanosecond resolution, while preserving an arbitrary and unknown polarization state of the original light. The delay line comprises bidirectional pass in cascaded segments of standard single-mode fibers, along with terminal reflection from Faraday mirrors (FMs) at both ends. Each segment contains either a short path or a long path, selected by an optical switch. The FMs auto-compensate for random polarization fluctuations introduced by the optical fibers, thereby restoring the initial polarization state. Broadband operation of the delay line was assessed by investigating dispersion of both the fiber and the FMs. Surprisingly, we found that the two-pass propagation in the fiber can mitigate dispersive distortion introduced by the FM. Numerical experiments demonstrated that the output polarization state of the delay line has a mean fidelity better than 99% over a range of 50-nm deviation from the central wavelength. Remarkably, the mean fidelity often surpasses that in the absence of the fiber itself.

</details>


### [21] [Pulse-to-pulse Power Fluctuations in Ultra-Fast Fiber Amplifiers with Gain-Managed Nonlinearity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30794)
*Maksim Kozlov, Colin J. McKinstrie, Nitzan Haviv, Pavel Sidorenko*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: A theoretical framework predicts pulse-to-pulse power fluctuations from pump and seed noise and amplified spontaneous emission in gain-managed nonlinear ultrafast fiber amplifiers, showing that nonlinear spectral broadening and frequency-dependent gain reshape relative intensity noise across high- and low-repetition-rate regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to predict pulse-to-pulse power fluctuations caused by pump and seed power noise and by amplified spontaneous emission in ultrafast fiber amplifiers operating in the gain-managed nonlinear regime, avoiding Monte Carlo simulations with stochastic sources.

Method: The authors present a theoretical framework and numerical model that evaluates statistically averaged quantities directly. The model reduces to a semi-analytical monochromatic model when signal bandwidth vanishes and amplified spontaneous emission is negligible, and it accounts for nonlinear spectral broadening of the signal and its perturbations together with frequency-dependent gain.

Result: Nonlinear spectral broadening combined with frequency-dependent gain significantly alters the technical relative intensity noise in both high-power, high-repetition-rate and low-power, low-repetition-rate regimes. In the low-power, low-repetition-rate regime, amplified-spontaneous-emission-related contributions become comparable to those induced by pump and seed fluctuations.

Conclusion: The framework provides direct statistical prediction of pulse-to-pulse power fluctuations in gain-managed nonlinear ultrafast fiber amplifiers, identifying nonlinear spectral broadening, frequency-dependent gain, and amplified spontaneous emission as important contributors across operating regimes.

Abstract: We present a theoretical framework for predicting pulse-to-pulse power fluctuations caused by pump and seed power noise and by amplified spontaneous emission in ultrafast fiber amplifiers operating in the gain-managed nonlinear regime. The numerical model evaluates statistically averaged quantities directly, without Monte Carlo simulations with stochastic sources, and reduces to a semi-analytical monochromatic model in the limit of vanishing signal bandwidth and negligible ASE. We show that nonlinear spectral broadening of the signal and of its perturbations, combined with the frequency-dependent gain, significantly alters the technical relative intensity noise in both the high-power, high-repetition-rate and the low-power, low-repetition-rate regimes, and that in the latter regime the contributions related to amplified spontaneous emission become comparable to those induced by pump and seed fluctuations.

</details>


### [22] [A Highly Tunable (Ti,Zr)N$_x$-Based MIM Plasmonic Sensor for Multidomain Biological, Chemical, and Physical Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30614)
*Mohammad Abrar Kabir, Aosaf Jarif Siddiquee, Abdullah Taharat, A. K. M. Rakib, Rakibul Hasan Sagor*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper proposes a CMOS-compatible metal-insulator-metal plasmonic refractive-index sensor using the ternary nitride (Ti,Zr)Nx in a square-ring resonator with embedded circular nanorods, and demonstrates biological, chemical, and physical sensing with high sensitivity and low detection limit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Noble-metal MIM plasmonic refractive-index sensors are attractive for label-free, real-time detection, but gold and silver constrain tunability, thermal stability, and CMOS process compatibility. The authors seek a CMOS-compatible alternative plasmonic material with broader compositional tunability and a sensing platform applicable across multiple domains.

Method: The authors design a novel MIM plasmonic refractive-index sensor based on a square-ring resonator with embedded circular nanorods, using ternary nitride (Ti,Zr)Nx as the plasmonic material for what the abstract describes as its first application in a MIM sensing platform. The sensor is validated for glucose monitoring, hemoglobin-based anemia classification across four severity levels, discrimination of five major blood constituents, cancer cell identification across six tissue types, air-pressure sensing, and CO2 detection after polyhexamethylene biguanide functionalization. The abstract does not provide fabrication or numerical simulation details.

Result: The sensor achieves a maximum refractive-index sensitivity of 2146.496 nm/RIU and a detection limit of 4.669 × 10^-7 RIU, outperforming previously reported CMOS-compatible plasmonic refractive-index sensors. It shows an air-pressure sensitivity of 6.158 nm/MPa with negligible thermal cross-sensitivity and a CO2 sensitivity of 330.12 pm/ppm via PHMB functionalization, reported as the highest among comparable structures in the literature.

Conclusion: (Ti,Zr)Nx is presented as a broadly tunable, thermally stable, and fully CMOS-compatible alternative plasmonic material for MIM refractive-index sensing. The proposed platform supports biological, chemical, and physical sensing applications and achieves high refractive-index sensitivity and low detection limit, although the abstract does not provide details on experimental fabrication or broader deployment.

Abstract: Metal-Insulator-Metal (MIM) plasmonic refractive index (RI) sensors have attracted considerable attention for label-free, real-time analyte detection; however, their reliance on noble metals such as gold and silver imposes fundamental constraints on tunability, thermal stability, and CMOS process compatibility. In this work, we propose a novel MIM plasmonic RI sensor based on a square ring resonator with embedded circular nanorods, employing the ternary nitride (Ti,Zr)N$_x$ as the plasmonic material, marking its first application in a MIM sensing platform. Compared to binary nitrides and other alternative plasmonic materials reported in the literature, (Ti,Zr)N$_x$ offers broader compositional tunability and greater flexibility in tailoring the optical response while remaining fully CMOS-compatible. The sensor is validated across a comprehensive range of biosensing and physicochemical analyte scenarios, including glucose monitoring for diabetes assessment, hemoglobin-based anemia classification across four severity levels, simultaneous discrimination of five major blood constituents, and cancer cell identification across six tissue types. For physical and chemical sensing, the structure achieves an air pressure sensitivity of 6.158 nm/MPa with negligible thermal cross-sensitivity, and a CO$_2$ detection sensitivity of 330.12 pm/ppm via polyhexamethylene biguanide (PHMB) functionalization, which is the highest reported among comparable structures in the literature. The proposed sensor achieves a maximum RI sensitivity of 2146.496 nm/RIU with a detection limit of $4.669 \times 10^{-7}$ RIU, outperforming previously reported CMOS-compatible plasmonic RI sensors.

</details>


### [23] [Motion of dissipative solitons in quasi-one-dimensional potentials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30594)
*Valentin Besse, Herv{é} Leblond, Dumitru Mihalache, Boris A. Malomed*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper numerically studies how kicked two-dimensional dissipative solitons and soliton pairs move in quasi-one-dimensional periodic and radial-lattice potentials within the cubic-quintic complex Ginzburg-Landau equation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how quasi-one-dimensional potentials control the motion, transverse dynamics, collisions, and radial scattering of two-dimensional dissipative solitons and soliton pairs.

Method: The cubic-quintic complex Ginzburg-Landau equation with quasi-one-dimensional potentials, either spatially periodic or radial-lattice, is solved numerically for inputs of one or two fundamental solitons set in motion by a kick.

Result: A kick parallel to the quasi-one-dimensional potential axis produces free longitudinal soliton motion because the model remains Galilean-invariant in that direction. Transverse motion, which may involve pulse multiplication, occurs only for sufficiently strong kicks; oblique kicks produce a superposition of longitudinal and transverse motion. Collisions between solitons moving longitudinally and diagonally are also considered. In the radial potential, a soliton kicked toward the center may bounce back from the central potential hill or pass it, yielding complex trajectories and splitting.

Conclusion: Quasi-one-dimensional potentials strongly shape dissipative-soliton motion: longitudinal motion is free along the potential axis, transverse motion is kick-dependent and can multiply pulses, and radial potentials can produce bouncing, passage, complex trajectories, and soliton splitting. Quantitative kick thresholds and detailed parameter values are not provided in the abstract.

Abstract: We consider the motion of two-dimensional (2D) dissipative solitons and soliton pairs in the framework of the cubic-quintic complex Ginzburg-Landau equation with quasi-1D potentials, spatially periodic or radial-lattice ones. The equation is solved numerically for inputs representing one or two fundamental solitons, which are set in motion by a kick. For the quasi-1D potential, the kick parallel to the potential axis naturally initiates free motion of the 2D solitons, as the model remains Galilean-invariant in this direction. Motion in the transverse direction, which may entail pulse multiplication, commences if the kick is strong enough. Oblique kicks lead to a straightforward superposition of the longitudinal and transverse motions. Also considered is the collision between 2D solitons moving in the longitudinal and diagonal directions. In the radial potential, the soliton kicked towards the center may bounce back from the central potential hill or pass it, producing complex trajectories and splitting of the moving soliton.

</details>

*Zhenyuan Ren, Di Wu, Tianchi Zhang, Yizhi Chen, Junxian Wang, Zixuan Wei, Jun Qin, Lei Bi*

Main category: physics.optics

<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Integrated magneto-optical isolators are essential for stabilizing optical signals in photonic integrated circuits, but their practical use is limited by high insertion loss caused mainly by the relatively low figure of merit of Ce:YIG magneto-optical thin films deposited on silicon.

They identified garnet-phase Fe-rich and Ce-rich nanoclusters in non-stoichiometric Ce:YIG and used an iron-deficient target to fabricate nearly stoichiometric Ce:YIG films on Si(100), eliminating these nanocluster defects.
