The SiN MZI-type optical isolator demonstrated a record low insertion loss of 1.2 dB and an isolation ratio of 27.6 dB at 1556 nm.

Conclusion: Stoichiometry control of Ce:YIG is an effective route to suppress nanocluster defects and improve magneto-optical performance, enabling a low-loss integrated isolator and representing an important step toward practical integrated magneto-optical nonreciprocal photonic devices.

Abstract: Integrated optical isolators play a crucial role in photonic integrated circuits (PICs) by effectively stabilizing optical signals, making them indispensable components for advanced photonic systems. However, integrated magneto-optical (MO) isolators suffer from their high insertion loss, which is mainly caused by the relatively low FoM of Ce:YIG MO thin films deposited on silicon. Here, we show that film stoichiometry plays a key role in controlling the FoM of Ce:YIG. We observed garnet phase Fe-rich and Ce-rich nano-clusters in non-stoichiometric Ce:YIG. By using an iron-deficient target, we demonstrated that nearly stoichiometric Ce:YIG films on Si(100) could be fabricated, eliminating the nano-cluster defects. Consequently, the film exhibited a saturation Faraday rotation angle of -5523 deg/cm and a figure of merit of 72.7 deg/dB, which is 1.7 times higher compared to nonstoichiometric films. An MZI-type optical isolator on SiN fabricated by this method demonstrated a record low insertion loss of 1.2 dB and isolation ratio of 27.6 dB at 1556 nm wavelength. Our results provide an important step toward the practical application of integrated MO nonreciprocal photonic devices.

</details>


### [25] [Equivalence and origin of discrepancies between transfer matrix method and multiple reflection model of surface induced second harmonic generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30448)
*Deependra Singh Gaur, Jakub Lukeš, Karel Žídek*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper compares the transfer matrix method and the multiple reflection model for surface-induced second harmonic generation in thin films, shows that they can yield different SHG intensities because of inconsistent numerical implementation of interface SHG, and specifies conditions and a physical constraint needed to make the two methods equivalent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SHG spectroscopy is a sensitive tool for studying surfaces and interfaces in thin films, and interpreting measured data and layer boundaries requires a reliable theoretical model of SHG generation. The transfer matrix method and multiple reflection model are both commonly used and are expected to be equivalent, but they can produce controversial results for the same input properties and second-order susceptibility tensors.

Method: The authors theoretically and numerically compare the transfer matrix method and the multiple reflection model using the same input properties and second-order susceptibility tensors. They analyze the origin of discrepancies in the numerical implementation of interface SHG and describe the conditions and physical constraint required for equivalent results.

Result: The two methods can produce different SHG intensities for identical inputs despite their expected equivalence. The discrepancies are caused by inconsistent numerical implementation of interface SHG. The authors describe implementation conditions that achieve equivalence between the models and identify a physical constraint that must be imposed to obtain identical results from both methods.

Conclusion: Equivalence between the transfer matrix method and multiple reflection model is not automatic; it requires consistent numerical treatment of interface SHG under the specified conditions and the identified physical constraint.

Abstract: Second harmonic generation (SHG) spectroscopy is a sensitive tool to study surfaces and interfaces in thin films. To understand the layer boundaries and interpret the measured data, it is essential to use a theoretical model describing the SHG generation in the thin film. The transfer matrix method and multiple reflection model are two numerical techniques which have been commonly utilized in the calculation of surface-induced SHG. Here, we demonstrate that, despite the expected equivalence of the approaches, the two methods can lead to controversial results for the same input properties and second-order susceptibility tensors. The discrepancies resulting in different SHG intensity from both methods are caused by inconsistent numerical implementation of the interface SHG. We describe the conditions for the implementation to achieve the equivalence between the two models. Moreover, we identify the physical constraint that has to be imposed in numerical implementation to get identical results from both methods.

</details>


### [26] [Dark and bright solitons in a multimodal lithium tantalate microresonator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30313)
*Huizong Zhu, Yuyao Guo, Xiangfei Cheng, Yiqun Wu, Juanjuan Lu*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates wavelength-addressed generation of both dark-pulse and bright-soliton microcombs in a single integrated LiTaO3 microring by engineering two TE-like transverse mode families, with TE00 supporting normal-GVD dark pulses and spectrally isolated TE10 supporting anomalous-GVD bright solitons under fixed input polarization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bright-soliton and dark-pulse microcombs provide complementary outputs, and integrating both states in one resonator would expand the capabilities of an integrated coherent source. This requires opposite group-velocity-dispersion channels while combining localized interfamily interaction for dark-pulse formation with spectral isolation for independent bright-soliton pumping.

Method: Two TE-like transverse mode families in a LiTaO3 microring provide wavelength-addressed access to both comb states. The higher-order TE10 family plays two roles: within localized avoided-mode-crossing regions, mode-resolved spectroscopy indicates that TE10 perturbs the normal-GVD TE00 resonance sequence; away from these regions, TE10 retains anomalous GVD and remains spectrally isolated as an independently pumpable bright-soliton channel. At representative pump resonances, the closest resonance of the non-addressed family is separated by 75–192 loaded cavity linewidths. With input polarization fixed, pumping TE00 and TE10 resonances generates dark-pulse and bright-soliton microcombs, respectively.

Result: Dark-pulse and bright-soliton microcombs were generated in the same LiTaO3 microring by pumping TE00 and TE10 resonances, respectively, with fixed input polarization. To the authors' best knowledge, this is the first experimental realization of a dark-pulse microcomb in an integrated LiTaO3 microring.

Conclusion: Transverse-mode-family engineering enables localized modal interaction for dark-pulse formation and independent spectral addressing for bright-soliton pumping within a single microresonator, providing a route to combining complementary microcomb states in an integrated coherent source.

Abstract: Bright-soliton and dark-pulse microcombs provide complementary outputs. Integrating both states in a single resonator would expand the operating capabilities of an integrated coherent source, but requires opposite group-velocity-dispersion (GVD) channels while reconciling localized interfamily interaction for dark-pulse formation with spectral isolation for independent bright-soliton pumping. Wavelength-addressed access to both states is achieved in a LiTaO$_3$ microring through two TE-like transverse mode families. The higher-order TE$_{10}$ family fulfills two distinct roles in different spectral regions. Within localized avoided-mode-crossing regions, mode-resolved spectroscopy implicates TE$_{10}$ in perturbing the normal-GVD TE$_{00}$ resonance sequence. Away from these regions, TE$_{10}$ retains anomalous GVD and remains spectrally isolated as an independently pumpable bright-soliton channel; at representative pump resonances, the closest resonance of the non-addressed family is separated by 75--192 loaded cavity linewidths. With the input polarization held fixed, pumping TE$_{00}$ and TE$_{10}$ resonances generates dark-pulse and bright-soliton microcombs, respectively. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first experimental realization of a dark-pulse microcomb in an integrated LiTaO$_3$ microring. These results establish transverse-mode-family engineering as a route to combining localized modal interaction with independent spectral addressing in a single microresonator.

</details>


### [27] [Computation in flight through precision-processed optical fiber](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30266)
*Kailai Liu, Zhongyao Luo, Xiangpeng Xiao, Zhen Wang, Weiliang Zhao, Mingming Zhang, Hao Wu, Zhijun Yan, Ming Tang*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: FBG-CA is a passive optical-fiber computing module that uses spatially distributed weak fiber Bragg gratings to convolve temporal samples in the backward direction while the forward signal continues carrying the original data stream, enabling computation during transmission.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In distributed sensing and computing systems, optoelectronic conversion, buffering, and scheduling can make data movement a bottleneck.

Method: The authors fabricated FBG-CA by automated through-coating inscription of weak fiber Bragg gratings. The distributed FBGs reflect, delay, and accumulate temporal samples to produce a convolution output backward while the forward signal carries the original data stream to a remote receiver. Quantization-aware training was combined with sharpness-aware minimization to mitigate finite reflectivity resolution and fabrication deviations.

Result: Over 1440 m of computing fiber, the single-wavelength implementation achieved a convolution correlation coefficient of 0.987, 87.90% MNIST accuracy, 79.07% MIT-BIH Arrhythmia accuracy, and an estimated 288 GOPS. With approximately 20% forward-power reduction (0.97 dB), recovered MNIST images reached a mean structural similarity index of 0.9930, and recovered MIT-BIH heartbeat waveforms had a mean squared error of 0.005826. Four wavelength-division-multiplexed channels over 144 m achieved 92.25% accuracy on four-class Fashion-MNIST and an estimated 115.2 GOPS. An architecture-level projection indicated an intrinsic throughput density of 1.6 POPS/m for eight wavelengths at 100 GBaud per wavelength.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate passive, wavelength-scalable computation-in-flight in optical fiber.

Abstract: In distributed sensing and computing systems, optoelectronic conversion, buffering, and scheduling can make data movement a bottleneck. We propose FBG-CA, a passive computing module fabricated by automated through-coating inscription of weak fiber Bragg gratings (FBGs). The spatially distributed FBGs reflect, delay, and accumulate temporal samples to produce a convolution output in the backward direction, while the forward signal carries the original data stream to a remote receiver. This enables transmission and convolution within the same optical propagation event. To mitigate finite reflectivity resolution and fabrication deviations, we combine quantization-aware training with sharpness-aware minimization. Over 1440 m of computing fiber, the single-wavelength implementation achieves a convolution correlation coefficient of 0.987, classification accuracies of 87.90% on MNIST and 79.07% on MIT-BIH Arrhythmia, and an estimated throughput of 288 GOPS. With approximately 20% forward-power reduction (0.97 dB) across the FBG sections, the recovered MNIST images achieve a mean structural similarity index of 0.9930, and the recovered MIT-BIH heartbeat waveforms have a mean squared error of 0.005826. Using four wavelength-division-multiplexed channels over 144 m of computing fiber, FBG-CA achieves 92.25% accuracy on four-class Fashion-MNIST and an estimated throughput of 115.2 GOPS. An architecture-level projection indicates an intrinsic throughput density of 1.6 POPS/m for eight wavelengths at 100 GBaud per wavelength. These results demonstrate passive, wavelength-scalable computation-in-flight in optical fiber.

</details>


### [28] [Polarization switching of double azimuthal backflow in two transverse orbital angular momentum superpositions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30213)
*Zhen-Lai Wang*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: Interference of two equal-intensity rotating-wave structured light beams with different transverse topological charges produces double azimuthal backflow of Poynting and canonical momenta; this backflow is switched by polarization matching and is periodic with an angular period set by the topological-charge difference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to establish a vectorial, polarization-gated mechanism for azimuthal optical backflow without amplitude tuning, motivated by potential polarization-switchable optical manipulation.

Method: The study analyzes the interference of two rotating-wave structured light beams with distinct transverse topological charges and equal intensity, examining the azimuthal components of the Poynting and canonical momenta and the role of a single polarization overlap in the interference terms.

Result: Both azimuthal momentum components reverse jointly, acquiring backflow in a common angular sector while their radial intervals can differ. Matched polarizations switch the double backflow on and mismatched polarizations switch it off. The double backflow is periodic in the azimuthal direction, with its angular period locked to the topological-charge difference and independent of the individual charges.

Conclusion: The results establish a vectorial mechanism for polarization-gated azimuthal backflow, with potential applications in polarization-switchable optical manipulation.

Abstract: Optical backflow in the azimuthal direction can be generated by the interference of two rotating-wave structured light beams with distinct transverse topological charges and equal intensity. Here we show that the interference reverses both azimuthal components of the Poynting and the canonical momenta. Although these two momenta are not generally tied, they reverse jointly here: both acquire azimuthal backflow in a common angular sector, while their radial intervals can differ. The double backflow is switched solely by the polarizations of the two beams, without amplitude tuning. A single polarization overlap enters the interference terms of both momenta but not the single-beam terms of either, so that matched polarizations switch the double backflow on whereas mismatched ones switch it off. The double backflow is periodic in the azimuthal direction, with its angular period locked to the topological charge difference and independent of the individual charges. Our results establish a vectorial mechanism for polarization-gated azimuthal backflow, with potential applications in polarization-switchable optical manipulation.

</details>


### [29] [Strain-driven terahertz phase modulation in piezoelectric and nonlinear optical crystals: mechanisms, material developments, and modulation strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30194)
*Aarifa Muhammed Ashraf, Rajaboopathi Mani*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: This review examines mechanisms, material developments, and modulation strategies for terahertz phase modulation, emphasizing external-stimulus control, strain-driven modulation in non-centrosymmetric piezoelectric and nonlinear optical crystals under piezoelectric resonance, and key modulator performance metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Advances in terahertz sensing, spectroscopy, imaging, and wireless communication have increased demand for high-performance terahertz modulators, motivating the review of external-stimulus and strain-driven approaches for controlling terahertz waves.

Method: The paper reviews recent terahertz modulation advances across materials including 2D semiconductors and metamaterials under external stimuli such as optical pumping and thermal or electric fields. It examines strain-driven terahertz modulation in non-centrosymmetric piezoelectric organic and semi-organic crystals, discusses piezoelectric-resonance coupling between terahertz waves and lattice vibrations, and assesses lithium niobate and other nonlinear optical crystals with suitable piezoelectric resonance peaks. It also overviews tunable-material-integrated modulators and metrics including modulation speed, bandwidth, modulation depth, and insertion loss.

Result: The review highlights the ability of non-centrosymmetric piezoelectric crystals to strongly couple propagating terahertz waves with lattice vibrations under piezoelectric resonance, assesses lithium niobate and other nonlinear optical crystals as candidates for strain-driven terahertz modulation, and summarizes operating principles and performance metrics of tunable-material-integrated terahertz modulators. Quantitative results are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: Strain-driven terahertz modulation in piezoelectric and nonlinear optical crystals, particularly through piezoelectric resonance, is presented as a promising modulation strategy, alongside external-stimulus approaches in 2D semiconductors and metamaterials. The abstract does not provide specific comparative performance values or a detailed future roadmap.

Abstract: Terahertz (THz) technology has witnessed notable developments in sensing, spectroscopy, imaging, and wireless communication which have increased the demand for high-performance THz modulators. Among the various approaches for controlling the THz waves, the application of external stimuli such as optical pumping and thermal or electric fields is widely employed as a promising strategy for modulation. This review focuses on recent advances in THz modulation based on different materials including 2D semiconductors and metamaterials with attention to their response under external stimuli. In addition, strain-driven THz modulation in non-centrosymmetric piezoelectric materials including organic and semi-organic crystals is examined with an emphasis on their ability to establish strong coupling between propagating THz waves and lattice vibrations under piezoelectric resonance conditions. We further highlight earlier reports on strain-driven THz modulation in lithium niobate (LiNbO3) and critically assess the potential of several other nonlinear optical crystals exhibiting piezoelectric resonance peaks suitable for strain-driven THz modulation. An overview of THz modulators integrated with tunable materials is presented highlighting their operating principles and key performance metrics comprising modulation speed, bandwidth, modulation depth and insertion loss.

</details>


### [30] [Designing Incoherent Multispectral Diffractive Optical Networks for Satellite Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30079)
*Xilin Yang, Md Sadman Sakib Rahman, Ryan Lee, Aydogan Ozcan*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper proposes a surrogate model-guided framework for designing incoherent multispectral diffractive optical networks that classify satellite scenes onboard, avoiding transfer of full multispectral data cubes. Numerically, six-channel and three-channel diffractive classifiers achieve blind-testing accuracies of 79.8% and 72.7%, respectively.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional multispectral satellite imaging workflows transmit high-dimensional image cubes to the ground for electronic analysis. The authors aim to enable task-specific, parallel, lightweight onboard inference using diffractive optical computing, eliminating the need to capture and transfer the full multispectral data cube.

Method: A physically compatible surrogate model is first trained: a non-negative fully connected model with a generalized detector readout, producing a task-specific non-negative multispectral intensity transformation. This transformation is transferred to an optical processor, whose diffractive counterpart is synthesized through data-free point-spread-function-based optimization under spatially incoherent illumination.

Result: For six-spectral-channel tri-class satellite-scene recognition of artificial surface, water body, and vegetated land, the diffractive classifier achieved 79.8% blind-testing accuracy, compared with 81.8% for its surrogate. A compact three-spectral-channel design achieved 72.7% blind-testing accuracy, compared with 75.1% for its surrogate.

Conclusion: The surrogate model-guided strategy offers a practical pathway for designing compact, task-specific incoherent multispectral optical processors for resource-constrained remote-sensing inference. The reported demonstrations are numerical.

Abstract: Optical neural networks and diffractive optical computing provide a promising route for performing task-specific inference with high parallelism and lightweight electronic post-processing. These advantages are especially attractive for multispectral satellite imaging, where conventional workflows rely on transmitting high-dimensional image cubes to the ground for downstream electronic analysis. Here, we introduce a computationally efficient surrogate model-guided framework for designing incoherent multispectral diffractive optical networks that directly output class probabilities onboard, eliminating the need to capture and transfer the full multispectral data cube for ground-side inference. We first train a physically compatible surrogate model, represented by a non-negative fully connected model that accommodates a generalized detector readout, yielding a task-specific non-negative multispectral intensity transformation that can be transferred to an optical processor. We then synthesize its diffractive counterpart through data-free point-spread-function-based optimization under spatially incoherent illumination. Using this approach, we numerically demonstrate a six-spectral-channel diffractive classifier for tri-class satellite-scene recognition, achieving a blind-testing accuracy of 79.8% for artificial surface, water body, and vegetated land images, closely approaching the 81.8% accuracy of its surrogate. We further demonstrate a more compact three-spectral-channel design that numerically achieves 72.7% blind-testing accuracy, compared with 75.1% for its corresponding surrogate model. This surrogate model-guided strategy provides a practical pathway for designing compact, task-specific incoherent multispectral optical processors for resource-constrained remote-sensing inference.

</details>


### [31] [Pseudo-Altermagnetism in Non-Hermitian Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29976)
*Chang-Yin Ji, Runze Li, Zhi-Ming Yu, Yugui Yao*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper introduces pseudo-altermagnetism in non-Hermitian systems, using balanced gain and loss as an altermagnetic-spin analog and an antiunitary symmetry O with O k ≠ k. It analyzes a C4zT-symmetric two-dimensional model, predicts movable but unremovable nodal structures and exceptional points, and demonstrates the effects in a non-Hermitian photonic crystal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Altermagnetism is attracting growing attention, and its analogs are being explored on classical platforms. The authors aim to extend this concept to non-Hermitian systems by using balanced gain and loss in place of altermagnetic spin.

Method: The authors define pseudo-altermagnetism through opposite gain and loss sublattices related by an antiunitary symmetry O satisfying O k ≠ k for generic momentum. They study a two-dimensional non-Hermitian system with O = C4zT symmetry, analyze its band structures and nodal degeneracies, and demonstrate the findings in a non-Hermitian photonic crystal.

Result: Pseudo-altermagnetism produces momentum-dependent gain and loss analogous to momentum-dependent spin splitting, along with intrinsic non-Hermitian properties. C4zT-invariant bands are either isolated or paired and both host movable but unremovable nodal structures. Isolated bands exhibit two movable but unremovable Hermitian spectral lines whose generic points are not C4zT invariant; paired bands form a movable but unremovable nodal network of real-part and imaginary-part degeneracy lines, with intersections that are generally exceptional points.

Conclusion: The work establishes a symmetry-based bridge between non-Hermitian physics and altermagnetism and provides mechanisms for Hermitian spectra beyond PT symmetry.

Abstract: Altermagnetism has attracted growing attention, and its analogs have been actively explored across classical platforms. Here, we introduce pseudo-altermagnetism in non-Hermitian systems, where balanced gain and loss plays the role of the altermagnetic spin, and the opposite gain and loss sublattices are related by an antiunitary symmetry $O$ with $O\bm k\neq \bm k$ for generic momentum $\bm k$. Using a two-dimensional non-Hermitian system with $O=C_{4z}\mathcal{T}$ symmetry as a representative example, we find that pseudo-altermagnetism not only leads to a momentum-dependent gain and loss distribution analogous to the momentum-dependent spin splitting of altermagnets, but also exhibits intrinsic non-Hermitian properties. The bands of $C_{4z}\mathcal{T}$-invariant non-Hermitian systems are either isolated or paired by $C_{4z}\mathcal{T}$, and both cases are guaranteed to host movable but unremovable nodal structures, characterized by the zeros of two distinct quantities. An isolated band exhibits two movable but unremovable Hermitian spectral lines, whose generic points are not invariant under $C_{4z}\mathcal{T}$, in sharp contrast to the conventional Hermitian spectra enforced only at the fixed points of an antiunitary symmetry. The paired bands, in turn, feature a movable but unremovable nodal network formed by the union of real-part and imaginary-part degeneracy lines, whose intersections are generally exceptional points. We further demonstrate these findings in a non-Hermitian photonic crystal. Our results establish a symmetry-based bridge between non-Hermitian physics and altermagnetism, and provide novel mechanisms of Hermitian spectra beyond $\mathcal{PT}$ symmetry.

</details>


### [32] [Polarization-cloaked spatiotemporal topological knots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29498)
*Yaning Zhou, Nianjia Zhang, Zhao Zhang, Chunhao Liang, Qian Cao, Houan Teng, Qiwen Zhan, Sergey A. Ponomarenko, Xu Guo, Yangjian Cai, Xin Liu*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper constructs vectorial spatiotemporal optical wavepackets by superposing oppositely wound toroidal vortex modes with orthogonal circular polarizations on the higher-order Poincaré sphere, encoding topological connectivity in the relative phase between polarization components. Stokes analysis, numerical simulations, and time-sliced off-axis interferometry show winding-dependent linked and knotted trajectories in S1 and S2 while S0 retains a common toroidal envelope.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional spatiotemporal topological knots are identified from a scalar complex field, and knotting optical knots with the polarization state of light is difficult to realize for ultrafast localized wavepackets. The work aims to extend control of spatiotemporal topology to the polarization degree of freedom.

Method: Oppositely wound toroidal vortex modes are combined with orthogonal circular polarization states on the higher-order Poincaré sphere to create vectorial electromagnetic spatiotemporal wavepackets whose topological connectivity is encoded in the relative phase between polarization components. Stokes analysis is used to examine S0, S1, and S2; numerical simulations test closure of linked and knotted trajectories, and time-sliced off-axis interferometry experimentally reconstructs three-dimensional S2 textures.

Result: S0 retains a common toroidal envelope, while the phase-sensitive Stokes parameters S1 and S2 reveal winding-dependent linked and knotted trajectories. Numerical simulations verify their closure, and experiments reconstruct the corresponding three-dimensional S2 textures. The topological connectivity is thus separated from the scalar intensity envelope.

Conclusion: The approach extends spatiotemporal topological control to polarization and provides a framework for controlling how topological information is accessed in multidimensional structured light. No quantitative performance metrics are provided in the abstract.

Abstract: Spatiotemporally localized topological knots encode global connectivity within a moving space-time optical wavepacket, circumventing the requirement for spatial-only eigenmodes that extend throughout three-dimensional space-filling. In conventional modalities, this connectivity is identified directly from a scalar complex field. However, knotting optical knots with the polarization state of light proves difficult to realize for ultrafast localized wavepackets. In this work, we combine oppositely wound toroidal vortex modes with orthogonal circular polarization states on the higher-order Poincaré sphere to construct vectorial electromagnetic spatiotemporal wavepackets, in which the topological connectivity is encoded in the relative phase between the polarization components. Stokes analysis shows that S_0 retains a common toroidal envelope, whereas the phase sensitive Stokes parameters S_1 and S_2 reveal winding-dependent linked and knotted trajectories; numerical simulations verify their closure, and time-sliced off-axis interferometry experimentally reconstructs the corresponding three-dimensional S_2 textures. This separation of topological connectivity from the scalar intensity envelope extends the control of spatiotemporal topology to the polarization degree of freedom and provides a versatile framework for controlling how topological information is accessed in multidimensional structured light.

</details>


### [33] [Turbulence Strength Estimation from Multiplexed Orbital Angular Momentum Beams Using Multimode Fibre Speckle](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29382)
*Warwick G. C. Brown, Mitchell A. Cox*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper estimates atmospheric turbulence strength, expressed as the Fried parameter, directly from multiplexed orbital angular momentum beams by using multimode-fibre speckle and an extreme learning machine, without needing to know which OAM modes are present.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Turbulence strength is usually measured with a separate instrument or a Gaussian probe beam, so existing structured-light links do not use their already-carried beams for this measurement. The authors aim to estimate turbulence strength directly in mode-division multiplexed systems.

Method: Multiplexed OAM beams are passed through a passive multimode fibre, producing speckle on a camera. A trained weighted sum of camera pixels, an extreme learning machine, averaged over a few tens of frames, estimates the Fried parameter without knowledge of the input modes. Laboratory tests use emulated turbulence and superpositions of up to eleven OAM modes.

Result: The OAM-based estimate is as accurate as that from a Gaussian probe beam sent through the same fibre. In all but weak turbulence, it is also more accurate than an ideal Strehl meter or an ideal tip/tilt sensor. Exact numerical error values are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: Multimode-fibre speckle from multiplexed OAM beams, combined with an extreme learning machine, provides an efficient way to estimate turbulence strength in mode-division multiplexed free-space optical systems.

Abstract: The strength of atmospheric turbulence governs how much the modes of a free-space optical link mix together. It is usually measured with a separate instrument or with a Gaussian probe beam, neither of which uses the beams that a structured-light link already carries. Here we show that the turbulence strength, expressed as the Fried parameter, can be estimated directly from multiplexed orbital angular momentum (OAM) beams without knowing which modes are present. The beams pass through a passive multimode fibre that turns them into speckle on a camera, and a trained weighted sum of the camera pixels (known as an extreme learning machine), averaged over a few tens of frames, gives the Fried parameter. In the laboratory with emulated turbulence, superpositions of up to eleven OAM modes give an estimate as accurate as that from a Gaussian probe beam sent through the same fibre. In all but weak turbulence the estimate is also more accurate than an ideal Strehl meter or an ideal tip/tilt sensor, providing an efficient way to estimate turbulence strength in mode-division multiplexed systems.

</details>

Main category: physics.optics

  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ultrafast optical fields create novel light-induced states in quantum materials, but identifying these states and elucidating their formation mechanisms requires comprehensive, quantitative access to nonequilibrium electrodynamics across relevant energy scales.

Abstract: Ultrafast optical fields have introduced numerous novel light-induced states in quantum materials. Identifying these states and elucidating their formation mechanisms require comprehensive, quantitative access to nonequilibrium electrodynamics across relevant energy scales. Here, we develop a Yb-laser-based time-resolved terahertz spectroscopy (TRTS) platform combining multi-pass-cell pulse compression, spintronic terahertz generation, and air-biased coherent detection. A single time-domain waveform scan provides continuous phase-resolved spectral coverage to 25 THz ($\sim$100 meV) with a Fourier resolution of 0.1 THz ($\sim$0.4 meV). This capability enables the characterization of both low-frequency charge dynamics and higher-energy resonant excitations in quantum materials under a common temporal reference. The system achieves a single-scan time-domain signal-to-noise ratio of 72 dB with long-term waveform stability, while preserving most of the laser pulse energy for tunable excitation. Measurements on intrinsic GaAs resolve extremely weak phonon features above 23 THz and demonstrate quantitative retrieval of equilibrium and transient complex optical properties. The resulting platform can provide a more complete microscopic picture of light-induced states in quantum materials.

### [35] [Giant thermal transduction via feedback-phase engineering in a thin-film lithium-niobate opto-electro-mechanical oscillator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29263)
*Ran Yin, Yue Yu, Ian Christen, Mengjie Yu*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: A suspended thin-film lithium-niobate mechanical resonator embedded in an opto-electro-mechanical feedback loop uses laser-cavity detuning to select the oscillation phase and frequency, producing a 100-fold enhanced and sign-reversed effective thermal coefficient of frequency while also enabling low-noise, tunable subharmonic injection-locked operation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional resonant MEMS thermometry depends on the intrinsic temperature coefficient of frequency of mechanical resonances, which limits thermal transduction. The paper seeks a post-fabrication approach that enhances thermal response and simultaneously supports low-noise yet tunable mechanical oscillation.

Method: The authors embed a suspended thin-film lithium-niobate mechanical resonator in an opto-electro-mechanical feedback loop. Laser-cavity detuning controls the loop's round-trip phase and therefore the Barkhausen-selected oscillation frequency. They use temperature-dependent and open-loop phase measurements, independently measured closed-loop oscillation frequencies, and a detuning-dependent delay model to identify the origin of the enhanced response. They also investigate 2:1 subharmonic injection locking.

Result: The engineered effective TCF is +1.24×10^4 ppm/K, a 100-fold enhancement and sign reversal relative to the intrinsic TCF of −124 ppm/K. Measurements confirm that this giant response arises from feedback phase selection rather than the passive resonator. The architecture also supports 2:1 subharmonic injection locking with sub-6 Hz linewidths, reference-limited frequency stability, and tunable operation from 76.71 to 76.82 MHz.

Conclusion: Feedback-phase engineering is established as a general, post-fabrication route to giant thermal transduction and low-noise, tunable mechanical oscillation in thin-film lithium-niobate opto-electro-mechanical oscillators.

Abstract: Conventional resonant MEMS thermometry typically relies on the intrinsic temperature coefficient of frequency (TCF) of mechanical resonances. Here we show that embedding a suspended thin-film lithium-niobate mechanical resonator in an opto-electro-mechanical (OEM) feedback loop enhances the intrinsic coefficient by two orders of magnitude. By using laser--cavity detuning to control the loop's round-trip phase, and thus the Barkhausen-selected oscillation frequency, we engineer an effective TCF of $+1.24\times10^{4}$~ppm/K: a 100-fold enhancement and a sign reversal relative to its intrinsic TCF of $-124$~ppm/K. Temperature-dependent and open-loop phase measurements confirm that this giant response originates from feedback phase selection rather than from the passive resonator itself. This interpretation is further supported by the independently measured closed-loop oscillation frequencies and a detuning-dependent delay model. The same architecture also supports 2:1 subharmonic injection locking, yielding sub-6~Hz linewidths, reference-limited frequency stability, and tunable operation from $76.71$ to $76.82$~MHz. These results establish feedback-phase engineering as a general, post-fabrication route to both giant thermal transduction and low-noise yet tunable mechanical oscillation.

</details>


### [36] [Polarization-independent self-hybridized strong coupling by Brillouin zone folding induced quasi-bound states in the continuum in bulk WS2 metasurfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29204)
*Xiongwei Guo, Fu Liu, Jinhai Si, Xun Hou, Kuidong Wang*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates polarization-independent self-hybridized strong coupling between a Brillouin-zone-folding-induced quasi-bound state in the continuum and A excitons in a bulk WS2 metasurface, with a Rabi splitting of 165.9 meV that remains unchanged from linear to circular polarization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Strong coupling between high-refractive-index dielectric metasurfaces and transition-metal dichalcogenides typically requires a specific incident polarization to achieve maximal coupling because the optical modes are polarization-dependent. The authors aim to overcome this polarization dependence.

Method: A self-hybridized strong-coupling system is proposed in the same bulk WS2 metasurface, coupling the Brillouin zone folding induced quasi-bound states in the continuum mode with A excitons. The bulk WS2 metasurface thickness is varied to observe anticrossing behavior, and the incident-light polarization is changed from linear to circular.

Result: A typical anticrossing behavior is observed with a Rabi splitting of 165.9 meV. The Rabi splitting remains unchanged when the incident-light polarization is changed from linearly to circularly polarized, demonstrating polarization-independent self-hybridized strong coupling.

Conclusion: The work provides a method for realizing robust polarization-independent polaritons in bulk transition-metal dichalcogenide metasurfaces and offers insights for polaritonic devices operating with arbitrary polarizations.

Abstract: High-refractive-index dielectric metasurfaces supporting optical modes are ideal candidates for achieving strong coupling with transition-metal dichalcogenides (TMDCs) materials. However, optical illumination with a specific polarization is always required to estimate the maximal coupling strength in such coupled systems due to the polarization-dependent optical modes. Here, we propose a self-hybridized strong coupling between Brillouin zone folding induced quasi-bound states in the continuum (BZF-QBICs) mode and A excitons in the same bulk WS2 metasurface. By changing the thickness of the bulk WS2 metasurfaces, a typical anticrossing behavior with Rabi splitting of 165.9 meV is observed. Such Rabi splitting is unchanged when changing the polarization of the incident light from linearly to circularly polarizations, demonstrating the polarization-independent self-hybridized strong coupling. This work provides a novel method for realizing robust polarization-independent polaritons in bulk TMDCs metasurface and offers new insights for expanding the applications of polaritonic devices with arbitrary polarizations.

### [37] [2D MXene integrated optical polarizers based on TiO2 with high performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29121)
*David J. Moss*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: Demonstrates high-performance optical polarizers by integrating 2D MXene films onto silicon photonic devices and converting them in situ into 2D TiO2, achieving a record polarizer FOM, high power tolerance, broad bandwidth, high yield, and microring extinction ratios up to 12 dB.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Polarization control is essential for modern optical systems, but existing 2D-material-based polarizers struggle to simultaneously provide high figures of merit, robust power endurance, and scalable fabrication, limiting practical deployment beyond laboratories.

Method: 2D MXene films were integrated onto silicon photonic devices and converted in situ into 2D titanium dioxide (TiO2). The resulting 2D TiO2 was used to form hybrid polarizers and microring resonator polarizers, exploiting its improved anisotropic absorption and thermal/chemical stability relative to MXene.

Result: The hybrid polarizers achieved a record polarizer FOM of 17.5 among 2D-material-coated devices, stable operation under more than 2 W coupled average power, an operation bandwidth greater than 140 nm, and a fabrication yield of 98%. Microring resonator polarizers reached polarization extinction ratios up to 12 dB.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that these polarizers provide outstanding overall performance and strong large-scale manufacturing capability, representing a step toward industry-ready 2D-material optical polarizers. No laser–plasma interaction or intense-laser application is reported.

Abstract: Polarization control is essential for modern optical systems. Recently, two dimensional (2D) materials featuring highly anisotropic absorption across broad wavelength regions have emerged as a promising solution for realizing optical polarizers with operation bandwidths far exceeding those achievable using bulk materials. Despite this, existing 2D material based optical polarizers face critical limitations in simultaneously delivering high polarizer figures of merit (FOMs), robust power endurance, and scalable fabrication, hindering their practical deployment beyond laboratory. Here, we overcome this bottleneck by demonstrating optical polarizers through integrating 2D MXene films onto silicon photonic devices and subsequently converting them in situ into 2D titanium dioxide (TiO2). The 2D TiO2 exhibits significantly improved anisotropic absorption and thermal / chemical stability compared with MXene, enabling the hybrid polarizers to achieve a record high polarizer FOM of 17.5 (among 2D material-coated devices) and excellent power tolerance (stable under > 2 W coupled average power), together with a broad operation bandwidth (> 140 nm) and a high fabrication yield ( 98%). We also demonstrate microring resonator polarizers achieving high polarization extinction ratios up to 12 dB. With outstanding overall performance and strong capability for large-scale manufacturing, these polarizers represent a solid step towards industry ready implementations of 2D material based optical polarizers.

</details>


### [38] [Reconfigurable Microwave Photonic All-Pass Filters based on Optical Kerr Microcombs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29115)
*David J. Moss*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper proposes and experimentally demonstrates reconfigurable microwave photonic all-pass filters using a microcomb-driven transversal filter system, with programmable tap coefficients enabling first- and second-order filter responses and independent or joint tuning of characteristic parameters without hardware changes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Microwave photonic all-pass filters are important for phase engineering in high-bandwidth microwave signal processing because they manipulate phase without altering signal amplitudes, but conventional approaches struggle to provide high reconfigurability in filter order and characteristic parameters.

Method: The authors use a microcomb-driven transversal filter system based on optical Kerr microcombs to implement microwave photonic all-pass filters. They experimentally demonstrate first- and second-order APFs and tune filter characteristic parameters independently or jointly by programming tap coefficients without changing hardware.

Result: For the first-order APF, the maximum amplitude variation is 1.95 dB and the maximum group delay is 580 ps; for the second-order APF, the maximum amplitude variation is 2.01 dB and the maximum group delay is 1740 ps. Independent and joint tuning of APF characteristic parameters is also demonstrated through tap-coefficient programming.

Conclusion: The approach enables highly reconfigurable microwave photonic all-pass filters with sharp phase transitions, high group delays, and low loss, indicating potential for practical applications requiring dynamic and versatile phase control.

Abstract: Microwave photonic (MWP) all pass filters (APFs), which enable phase manipulation without altering signal amplitudes, are essential for phase engineering in high bandwidth microwave signal processing. However, achieving a high degree of reconfigurability in the filter order and characteristic parameters of APFs remains challenging for conventional methods. Here, we propose and experimentally demonstrate a new strategy for realizing MWP APFs based on a microcomb driven transversal filter system, enabling high reconfigurability, sharp phase transitions with high group delays, and low loss. We experimentally demonstrate both 1st and 2nd order APFs, achieving a maximum amplitude variation (MAV) of 1.95 dB and a maximum group delay (MGD) of 580 ps for the former, and a MAV of 2.01 dB and an MGD of 1740 ps for the latter. In addition, both independent and joint tuning of APF characteristic parameters are demonstrated by simply programming the tap coefficients without changing any hardware, enabling highly reconfigurable filter response for diverse phase engineering functionalities. These results highlight the potential of our approach for implementing MWP APFs with both high performance and reconfigurability for practical applications requiring dynamic and versatile phase control.

</details>


### [39] [Acoustic bound states in the continuum in a labyrinthine meta-atom](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29091)
*Aleksandra S. Zheleznova, Iuliia A. Timankova, ZiWei Ye, Yunmeng Wang, Jihao Tang, Mikhail V. Kuzmin, Nikolay S. Solodovchenko, Mihail I. Petrov, Andrey A. Bogdanov, Pavel S. Pankin, Yong Li*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper theoretically and experimentally demonstrates symmetry-protected acoustic bound states in the continuum in a labyrinthine meta-atom inside a waveguide, showing that symmetry breaking converts the BIC into a tunable radiative quasi-BIC.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The work aims to realize and control acoustic BICs using a labyrinthine meta-atom in a waveguide, with potential application to tunable absorbers, sensors, and modulators.

Method: A symmetry-protected BIC is analyzed in the lossless limit for a meta-atom on the waveguide axis; shifting it off-axis breaks symmetry and produces a radiative quasi-BIC. The concept is tested with a 3D-printed labyrinthine meta-atom in an impedance tube, compared with numerical simulations, and interpreted using two-mode temporal coupled-mode theory.

Result: On-axis placement supports a symmetry-protected BIC, while off-axis displacement yields a narrow resonant transmittance feature corresponding to a quasi-BIC. Measured spectra agree with simulations and confirm the BIC-to-quasi-BIC transition despite thermoviscous losses; resonance frequency, contrast, and linewidth are tunable via meta-atom position, with TCMT providing an analytical description.

Conclusion: Radiative leakage of the acoustic BIC can be tuned in the presence of absorption, making the proposed platform promising for tunable absorbers, sensors, and modulators.

Abstract: In this Letter, we theoretically and experimentally study acoustic bound states in the continuum (BICs) emerging in labyrinth meta-atom embedded in an acoustic waveguide. A symmetry-protected BIC is supported in the lossless limit when the meta-atom is positioned on the waveguide axis. Breaking the symmetry by shifting the meta-atom away from the center of the waveguide transforms the BIC into a radiative quasi-BIC, manifested by the emergence of a narrow resonant feature in the transmittance spectrum. The proposed concept is experimentally tested using a 3D-printed labyrinthine meta-atom placed inside an impedance tube. The measured transmittance spectra are consistent with numerical simulations and support the BIC-to-quasi-BIC transition induced by symmetry breaking in the presence of thermoviscous losses. The resonance frequency, contrast, and linewidth can be controlled by varying the meta-atom position. The observed behavior is interpreted within a two-mode temporal coupled-mode theory (TCMT), providing an analytical description of the resonant response. The ability to tune radiative leakage in the presence of absorption makes the proposed acoustic BIC platform promising for the development of tunable absorbers, sensors, and modulators.

</details>


### [40] [Uniguided and unicoupled resonances in an asymmetric acoustic metasurface](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29078)
*Andrey S. Konchakov, Daniil S. Buzin, Dmitrii N. Maksimov, Mingzhao Song, Y. Li, Mikhail I. Petrov, Chao Peng, Andrey A. Bogdanov, Pavel S. Pankin*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper theoretically analyzes an asymmetric acoustic metasurface whose oblique-incidence absorption asymmetry comes from paired uniguided and unicoupled resonances, enabling full transmission-phase control and one-sided perfect absorption with thermoviscous losses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to exploit uniguided and unicoupled resonances to achieve ultimate asymmetry in sound absorption under oblique incidence and to combine full phase control with perfect absorption in an acoustic metasurface.

Method: A theoretical study of an asymmetric acoustic metasurface is performed, analyzing a counter-propagating pair of uniguided (UGR) and unicoupled (UCR) resonances. The lossless regime and the effects of thermoviscous losses, including resonant intensity modulation and critical coupling, are considered.

Result: The UGR radiates to only one side, whereas the UCR radiates to both sides but can be excited from only one. In the lossless regime, the transmission phase winds by 2π across both UCR and UGR while transmittance remains flat at any prescribed nonzero level. Thermoviscous losses produce resonant intensity modulation and critical coupling, giving perfect sound absorption exclusively for incidence through the channel that excites the UCR.

Conclusion: Asymmetric metasurfaces combining UCR and UGR provide a route to full phase control and perfect sound absorption; specific experimental or fabrication details are not provided.

Abstract: We theoretically study an acoustic metasurface that exhibits ultimate asymmetry in absorption under oblique incidence. This behavior arises from a counter-propagating pair of uniguided (UGR) and unicoupled (UCR) resonances. The UGR radiates to only one side, while the UCR radiates to both sides but can be excited from only one. In the lossless regime, the transmission phase winds by $2π$ across both UCR and UGR while the transmittance remains flat at any prescribed nonzero level. Thermoviscous losses introduce resonant intensity modulation and enable critical coupling, yielding perfect sound absorption exclusively for incidence through the channel that excites the UCR. Therefore, asymmetric metasurfaces with UCR and UGR offer a route to full phase control and perfect sound absorption.

</details>


### [41] [Optical Motion Sensing for Piezoelectric-Resonator-Based Power Converters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29035)
*Liyang Jin, Zhiyang Cao, Jun Ji, Liyan Zhu, Linbo Shao*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper presents and experimentally validates an optical motion sensing approach that uses a laser, fiber optics, and a photodetector to monitor piezoelectric resonator displacement in real time and provide amplitude and phase information for closed-loop control of high-frequency piezoelectric power converters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Piezoelectric resonators are promising for miniaturized high-frequency power converters, but their optimal operating conditions are sensitive to temperature fluctuations and load variations, making robust closed-loop control essential. Electrical sensing of motional current may compromise the electrical isolation and EMI immunity provided by the mechanical nature of these resonators.

Method: The proposed system comprises a laser, fiber optics, and a photodetector and uses optical interference to monitor mechanical displacement in real time; the optical signal carries both amplitude and phase of the mechanical motion. The approach was experimentally characterized using a 45-kHz piezoelectric resonator driven by a GaN half bridge with input voltages up to 45 V, and closed-loop control was demonstrated for a 99-kHz isolated piezoelectric converter.

Result: The optical motion sensing approach was experimentally characterized and validated on a 45-kHz piezoelectric resonator driven by a GaN half bridge with input voltages up to 45 V. Closed-loop control using the proposed optical sensing was experimentally demonstrated for a 99-kHz isolated piezoelectric converter. Quantitative performance metrics are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: These initial results establish a viable pathway toward optically closed-loop control of piezoelectric power converters.

Abstract: Piezoelectric components present a promising alternative to conventional magnetics for miniaturized, high-frequency power converters. While high-Q piezoelectric resonators have demonstrated high efficiencies and large power densities in prototype high-frequency dc-dc converters, their optimal operating conditions are sensitive to temperature fluctuations and load variations. Therefore, robust closed-loop control is essential for practical deployment. Electrical approaches for sensing motional current may compromise the electrical isolation and electromagnetic interference (EMI) immunity inherently provided by the mechanical nature of these resonators. Here, we present an optical motion sensing approach for piezoelectric power converters. The optical sensing system comprises a laser, fiber optics, and a photodetector. The proposed approach uses optical interference to monitor the mechanical displacement in real time. The resulting optical signal carries both the amplitude and phase of the mechanical motion, which are necessary for closed-loop control. We experimentally characterize and validate this optical motion sensing approach using a 45-kHz piezoelectric resonator driven by a GaN half bridge with input voltages up to 45 V. We further experimentally demonstrate closed-loop control using the proposed optical motion sensing for a 99-kHz isolated piezoelectric converter. These initial results establish a viable pathway toward optically closed-loop control of piezoelectric power converters.

</details>


### [42] [Efficient phase-only wavefront shaping at 250 kHz for multimode fiber endomicroscopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29015)
*Pau Riera Rodríguez, Antonio M. Caravaca-Aguirre, Omer Tzang, Alexander Payne, Stephen Hamann, Rafael Piestun*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates a high-speed phase-only MEMS wavefront modulator with 8,192 elements, 7-bit phase control, and 250 kHz full-frame refresh for multimode-fiber endomicroscopy, achieving efficient light delivery and volumetric fluorescence imaging.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wavefront shaping through multimode fibers can enable diffraction-limited fluorescence imaging with hair-thin probes, but performance is limited by wavefront modulators that typically provide at most two of three desired properties: high refresh rate, high modal dimensionality, and high light efficiency.

Method: The authors use a tailored phase-only microelectromechanical modulator with a 32 x 256 array of 8,192 elements, 7-bit phase control, and 250 kHz full-frame refresh rate for wavefront control through a multimode fiber. They apply direct on-axis phase modulation, analyze the measured transmission with singular-value decomposition to identify effective degrees of freedom, and scan the focus at multiple depths.

Result: Direct on-axis phase modulation delivers 70+/-5% of incident light into the modulated field and eliminates off-axis encoding optical losses and hologram computation. Distal foci remain isotropic with high fidelity, and scanning at multiple depths enables volumetric fluorescence imaging.

Conclusion: The approach combines high refresh rate, high modal dimensionality, and high light efficiency, opening a new operating regime for adaptive in vivo multimode-fiber endoscopy.

Abstract: Wavefront shaping through multimode fibers (MMFs) enables diffraction-limited fluorescence imaging with hair-thin probes, at a speed and light budget bounded by the wavefront modulator. Existing modulators provide at most two of three key characteristics: high refresh rate, (high) modal dimensionality, and light efficiency. Here, we demonstrate wavefront control through an MMF using a tailored phase-only microelectromechanical modulator that combines all three, with 8,192 elements arranged in a 32 x 256 array, 7-bit phase control, and a 250 kHz full-frame refresh rate. Direct on-axis phase modulation delivers 70+/-5% of the incident light into the modulated field and eliminates both off-axis encoding optical losses and hologram computation. The generated foci at the distal end of the fiber remain isotropic with high fidelity. We use singular-value decomposition analysis of the measured transmission to identify the effective degrees of freedom of our system. By scanning the focus at multiple depths, we demonstrate volumetric fluorescence imaging. These results open a new operating regime for adaptive in vivo multimode-fiber endoscopy.

</details>


### [43] [Parallel Two-Photon Lithography: From Optical Nonlinearities to Energy Limits, Dose Control and Throughput Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29010)
*Michalis Stavrou, Panagiotis A. Loukakos, David Gray, Maria Farsari*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: This review analyzes parallel two-photon lithography, covering multi-focus and holographic approaches, an energy-deposition framework for voxel size and dose, throughput benchmarks above 10^8 voxels s^-1 and up to 1.7×10^9 voxels s^-1, and fidelity limits at high rates. It proposes minimum reporting standards and research priorities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Two-photon lithography can print three-dimensional polymer architectures with sub-diffraction features, but voxel-by-voxel writing limits throughput. The review motivates parallelization as a route beyond this serial-writing bottleneck.

Method: The paper reviews parallelization strategies including fixed multi-focus arrays, reconfigurable holography with spatial light modulators and digital micromirror devices, hybrid metalens arrays, shaped beams, temporally focused projection, and two-step absorption. It develops an energy-deposition framework based on measured nonlinear absorption coefficients to relate resist absorption order and coefficients to voxel size, energy per voxel, and attainable voxel rate, and applies it to quantify costs from non-uniform foci, coherent crosstalk, side-lobe losses, and weaker axial confinement.

Result: Published systems reach peak rates above 10^8 voxels s^-1 with scanned focus arrays and up to 1.7×10^9 voxels s^-1 with grayscale projection under a fine-voxel definition, with parallel features as small as 20–55 nm. At higher rates, dose uniformity, proximity effects, heat, stitching, and data bandwidth increasingly limit fidelity.

Conclusion: The review proposes a minimum reporting standard including energy per voxel and sustained part-level rates, and identifies priorities for the field: shared benchmark parts, predictive dose control, and resists designed for parallel writing.

Abstract: Two-photon lithography (TPL) prints three-dimensional polymer architectures with sub-diffraction features, but writing them voxel by voxel limits its throughput. This review examines how parallelization overcomes this limit with fixed multi-focus arrays, reconfigurable holography using spatial light modulators and digital micromirror devices, hybrid metalens arrays, shaped beams, temporally focused projection and two-step absorption. An energy-deposition framework built on measured nonlinear absorption coefficients shows how the absorption order and coefficients of a resist determine its voxel size, energy per voxel and attainable voxel rate. Applied to parallel writing, the framework quantifies the costs of non-uniform foci, coherent crosstalk between foci, side-lobe losses in shaped beams and weaker axial confinement in projection. Published systems reach peak rates above $10^{8}$ voxels s$^{-1}$ with scanned focus arrays and up to $1.7\times10^{9}$ voxels s$^{-1}$ with grayscale projection under a fine-voxel definition, and parallel features as small as 20-55 nm. As rates rise, however, dose uniformity, proximity effects, heat, stitching and data bandwidth increasingly limit fidelity. A minimum reporting standard that includes the energy per voxel and sustained part-level rates is therefore proposed, together with priorities for the field: shared benchmark parts, predictive dose control and resists designed for parallel writing.

</details>


### [44] [A Candidate twin-spine photon model in MacCullagh's medium](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29001)
*B. Hou, D. A. Sinclair*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper proposes a twin-spine photon in a rotationally elastic MacCullagh vacuum, deriving source-free Maxwell behavior from twist resistance and modeling photon propagation, birefringent torque, and electron emission via topological defects; no experimental validation is reported.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To model the vacuum as a low-energy elastic approximation to the deformable spacetime metric of General Relativity and obtain source-free Maxwell equations from a single-parameter twist-resistance action without separately postulating electric and magnetic fields, while providing a finite topological photon and an emission mechanism.

Method: The vacuum is treated as a rotationally elastic continuum using MacCullagh's single-parameter action. The paper analyzes transversely localized fields propagating rigidly at c, shows that line defects are required, and constructs the minimal twin-spine photon with two opposing displacement-current cores in linear and circular geometries. It considers birefringent-crystal differential wave speeds reshaping a shared spine trajectory, and models the electron as a bound topological defect with a π-twist phase condition; transverse acceleration shears its co-moving bow wave, the vacuum wave equation directs residual dipole strain into twin-spine geometry, and the phase condition supplies a quantizing boundary condition. An accompanying SymPy Executable Research Record separates postulated topology from derived mechanics.

Result: The minimal finite configuration is a twin-spine photon with two opposing displacement-current cores that propagates rigidly at c without loss or transverse stress and has angular momentum 0 or ±ℏ. In a birefringent crystal, differential wave speeds reshape the shared spine trajectory, producing a continuous macroscopic torque without invoking superposed phase-delayed plane waves. The emission model links transverse acceleration of a π-twist electron defect to twin-spine radiation and a quantizing boundary condition; quantitative emission outputs are not stated.

Conclusion: The twin-spine model offers a candidate topological, single-action account of source-free electromagnetism, photon spin, birefringent torque, and electron emission, but the abstract reports no experimental test or quantitative comparison; it explicitly separates postulated topology from derived mechanics.

Abstract: We model the vacuum as a rotationally elastic continuum, a low-energy electromagnetic approximation to the deformable spacetime metric of General Relativity. MacCullagh's single-parameter action yields the source-free Maxwell equations from resistance to twist, without separately postulating electric and magnetic fields. We show that a transversely localized field propagating rigidly at $c$ requires line defects; the minimal finite configuration is a twin-spine photon with two opposing displacement-current cores. It propagates without loss or transverse stress and admits linear and circular geometries with angular momentum $0$ or $\pm\hbar$. In a birefringent crystal, differential wave speeds reshape the shared spine trajectory, producing a continuous macroscopic torque without invoking superposed phase-delayed plane waves. For emission, we model the electron as a bound topological defect with a $π$-twist phase condition. Transverse acceleration shears its co-moving bow wave; the vacuum wave equation directs the residual dipole strain into twin-spine geometry, while the phase condition supplies a quantizing boundary condition. An accompanying SymPy Executable Research Record distinguishes postulated topology from derived mechanics.

</details>

Abstract: In this work, we introduce an inverse-design model called multiscale wave-coupled neural networks (MSWNets) inspired by physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) that integrate the deep-learning capability of fully-connected neural networks with rigorous Rayleigh-Sommerfeld diffraction theory. MSWNets are trained using spatial input and output functions describing the height profiles of diffractive structures. Importantly, a MSWNet is composed of multiple sub-networks that provide learning across multiple frequency components of the optimal design solution. First, we validate and analyze the inverse design capabilities of the MSWNets' architecture through training on the complete complex field propagated from the analytical design of an axilens, demonstrating accurate reconstruction. Finally, we employ MSWNets, trained to maximize focusing efficiency, for the inverse design of novel diffractive devices. In particular, we design and display the performance of several achromatic lenses and doublets across the mid-wave infrared region ($3-5\rm{\;μ} m$) with focal distance $f=500\rm{\;μm}$, demonstrating the potential of our inverse-design framework for the next-generation of broadband diffractive optical devices.

  <summary>Details</summary>

Abstract: Microchannel-plate (MCP) detectors offer high gain for extreme-ultraviolet (EUV) experiments, but spatial blur and nonlinear response hinder quantitative diffraction imaging. Here, we present an MCP-based EUV ptychography system that combines detector hardware with physics-simulation-trained computational restoration. A residual-learning neural network trained on simulated detector degradation restores diffraction patterns without experimentally acquired training pairs. This preprocessing substantially improves image quality compared with reconstructions from uncorrected data under identical reconstruction settings. Single-harmonic imaging at $57$~nm yields an effective edge-response width of $1.08~μ$m, approaching the nominal diffraction-limited scale of the imaging geometry. The approach further enables wavelength-multiplexed imaging at $44.4$ and $57$~nm. By coupling high-gain detection with computational compensation, this system enables high-resolution, multi-harmonic EUV ptychography and offers a cost-effective alternative to scientific-grade EUV CCD detectors

### [47] [Anisoplanatic Blind Deconvolution using Phase Diversity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28458)
*Shubhankar Jape, Seungman Choi, Peter Menart, Andrew Schramka, Pratyush Uppuluri, Vaneet Aggarwal, Zubin Jacob*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper introduces coded anisoplanatic blind deconvolution (CA-BD), which uses a pupil-plane spatial light modulator to apply known per-measurement phase codes, enabling recovery of an object and spatially varying turbulence phases from short acquisitions without a wavefront sensor or deformable mirror. Simulations show improved phase precision over direct imaging and tuned multiframe blind deconvolution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Atmospheric turbulence produces blur that varies across a wide field of view and evolves in time, so a single wavefront cannot describe it. Multiframe blind deconvolution is limited during fast imaging because measurements provide insufficient diversity.

Method: CA-BD applies a different known phase code to every measurement using a pupil-plane spatial light modulator, without a wavefront sensor or deformable mirror. A per-pixel single-layer estimator then recovers the object and spatially varying phases from the coded measurements.

Result: In simulation, Cramér-Rao bound analysis indicates that under anisoplanatism direct imaging needs about 41 times the photons of coded imaging for equal phase precision in strong turbulence. At the stronger of two turbulence strengths, CA-BD outperforms tuned multiframe blind deconvolution on average at every burst length, reducing phase mean squared error by 3.5x for stationary turbulence and 2-3x for time-varying turbulence. Measurements within a few coherence times benefit most from known coded SLM patterns.

Conclusion: Known coded pupil-plane phase diversity can provide sufficient diversity for anisoplanatic blind deconvolution within short acquisitions, outperforming tuned multiframe blind deconvolution in the reported simulations. Faster spatial light modulators are identified as key to realizing the full advantage in time-varying turbulence.

Abstract: Atmospheric turbulence distorts the wavefront of light from a distant scene and blurs its image. Over a field of view wider than the isoplanatic angle, the distortion varies with direction and evolves in time, so no single wavefront describes the blur. Multiframe blind deconvolution (MFBD) can recover the scene from measurements spread over a long acquisition, but falls short during fast imaging because of limited diversity across measurements. We present coded anisoplanatic blind deconvolution (CA-BD), in which a pupil-plane spatial light modulator (SLM) applies a different known phase code to every measurement, with no wavefront sensor or deformable mirror, so that the measurements carry diversity even within a short acquisition. A per-pixel single-layer estimator recovers the object and spatially varying phases from the coded measurements. In simulation, a Cramér-Rao bound analysis shows that under anisoplanatism direct imaging needs about 41 times the photons of coded imaging for equal phase precision in strong turbulence. At the stronger of two turbulence strengths, CA-BD outperforms tuned MFBD on average at every burst length. It reduces the phase mean squared error 3.5x for stationary turbulence and 2-3x for time-varying turbulence. Measurements taken within a few coherence times benefit most from the known coded SLM patterns, making faster SLMs the key to its full advantage in time-varying turbulence.

</details>

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper introduces a dual-optical-parametric-oscillator, variable-delay pump technique that reconstructs excited-state decay curves from delayed fluorescence spectra, enabling lifetime measurements for short-lived, low-yield rare-earth states; it is demonstrated on Er3+-doped ZBLAN and validated against conventional time-resolved fluorescence spectroscopy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional time-resolved laser fluorescence spectroscopy struggles to measure excited-state lifetimes of rare-earth ions when the states are very short lived or have low photon yield because non-radiative de-excitation dominates.
