The 4F9/2 lifetime agrees excellently with conventional time-resolved fluorescence spectroscopy. The approach is suited to lifetimes from the sub-microsecond to millisecond range; specific lifetime values are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: Variable excited-state dual-pump analysis provides a versatile platform for measuring excited-state lifetimes and investigating excited-state dynamics and energy-transfer processes in rare-earth-doped materials.

Abstract: This work presents a new technique for measuring excited-state lifetimes of rare-earth ions. This technique is particularly powerful for states that are very short lived or have a low photon yield because their de-excitation pathways are dominated by non-radiative processes. The technique uses dual optical parametric oscillators, whose pulses are separated by a variable delay. It reconstructs the excited-state decay curve from a series of delayed fluorescence intensity spectra rather than directly recording the fluorescence decay as done in conventional time-resolved laser fluorescence spectroscopy. The power of the technique is demonstrated by measuring the lifetime of the short-lived $^4I_{9/2}$ level in Er$^{3+}$-doped ZBLAN, which is well-known for its low photon yield. The technique enables accurate measurement of its lifetime and suggests a new depopulating mechanism involving the $^4I_{9/2}$ level in ZBLAN glass. Its accuracy is validated by measuring the lifetime of the $^4F_{9/2}$ state achieving excellent agreement with conventional time-resolved fluorescence spectroscopy measurements. The approach is well suited for measuring lifetimes ranging from the sub-microsecond to the millisecond range. It provides a versatile platform for investigating excited-state dynamics and energy-transfer processes in RE-doped materials.

</details>


### [49] [Inverse design of polarization-analyzing gratings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28364)
*Kexin Bian, Alexander R. Fiani, Lisa W. Li, Noah A. Rubin*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper introduces a differentiable topology optimization framework for inverse design of metasurface polarization-analyzing gratings, removing the paraxial restriction of the traditional Jones-calculus/Fourier-optics approach and improving performance especially at high angles, with preliminary experimental validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional design of metasurface polarization-analyzing gratings combines Jones calculus with Fourier optics, but its validity is limited to the paraxial regime, restricting performance and design capability, particularly at high angles.

Method: The authors develop a differentiable topology optimization framework for inverse design of metasurface polarization-analyzing gratings that is not constrained to the paraxial regime, and they compare resulting designs with the traditional approach and provide preliminary experimental validation.

Result: The proposed framework yields grating designs that outperform the traditional design approach, especially at high angles; preliminary experiments corroborate these results.

Conclusion: Differentiable topology optimization enables inverse design of metasurface polarization-analyzing gratings beyond the paraxial regime and can improve high-angle performance, supported by preliminary experimental validation.

Abstract: Metasurface polarization analyzing gratings have attracted increasing interest for polarization management and sensing in optical systems. Traditional strategies for the design of these gratings are based on a formalism combining the Jones calculus with Fourier optics. While this approach has achieved notable success in numerous applications, its validity is restricted to the paraxial regime. Here we introduce a differentiable topology optimization framework for these gratings which is free of this constraint. We show that this framework can yield designs which outperform the traditional approach, especially at high angles. Additionally, we provide preliminary experimental validation to corroborate these results.

</details>


### [50] [Cryogenic Luminescence Thermometry of Upconversion Micro- and Nanoparticles via Deep Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28311)
*Pablo José Coria Ledo, Lina Wölcken, Lucas R. Mendicino, Santiago Gliosca, M. Claudia Marchi, Marcos Illescas, Beatriz Barja, Christian Tomás Schmiegelow*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: A 1D convolutional neural network predicts temperature directly from single upconversion emission spectra of NaYF4:Yb3+,Er3+ micro- and nanoparticles, outperforming the conventional luminescence intensity ratio at cryogenic temperatures and under optical misalignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Luminescence thermometry of single or optically trapped particles suffers from weak, fluctuating signals and, for Er3+-based probes, from loss of luminescence intensity ratio sensitivity at cryogenic temperatures.

Method: The authors trained a one-dimensional convolutional neural network on single emission spectra of NaYF4:Yb3+,Er3+ micro- and nanoparticles from -150 °C to 105 °C and compared it with the standard luminescence intensity ratio under deliberately reproduced adverse conditions using particle ensembles, including optical misalignment and varying pump powers and wavelengths.

Result: From -150 °C to 30 °C, the 1D-CNN reduced the single-spectrum mean absolute error from 5.4 °C to 1.0 °C and maintained nearly constant temperature uncertainty at low temperatures, where Stark-component features remained temperature dependent while the luminescence intensity ratio lost sensitivity. Under optical misalignment, its error increased only from 0.58 °C to 0.88 °C, whereas the luminescence intensity ratio error rose from 1.4 °C to 3.5 °C. The approach also achieved 0.21 °C accuracy over a narrow temperature window with a single calibration valid across different pump powers and wavelengths.

Conclusion: The results establish 1D-CNNs as robust single-shot luminescence thermometers and represent a step toward fast thermometry of single and trapped particles.

Abstract: Luminescence thermometry with single or optically trapped particles is hampered by weak signals that fluctuate with alignment, collection efficiency, and excitation conditions and, for $\mathrm{Er}^{3+}$-based probes, by the loss of sensitivity of the luminescence intensity ratio (LIR) at cryogenic temperatures. Here we use a one-dimensional convolutional neural network (1D-CNN) to predict the temperature directly from single emission spectra of $\mathrm{NaYF}_4{:}\mathrm{Yb}^{3+},\mathrm{Er}^{3+}$ micro- and nanoparticles between $-150\,^\circ\mathrm{C}$ and $105\,^\circ\mathrm{C}$, and compare it with the standard LIR under adverse conditions deliberately reproduced with particle ensembles. From $-150\,^\circ\mathrm{C}$ to $30\,^\circ\mathrm{C}$, the 1D-CNN reduces the single-spectrum mean absolute error from $5.4\,^\circ\mathrm{C}$ to $1.0\,^\circ\mathrm{C}$ and keeps its temperature uncertainty nearly constant down to the lowest temperatures, where Stark-component features remain temperature dependent while the LIR loses its sensitivity. Under deliberate optical misalignment, its error increases only from $0.58\,^\circ\mathrm{C}$ to $0.88\,^\circ\mathrm{C}$, whereas that of the LIR rises from $1.4\,^\circ\mathrm{C}$ to $3.5\,^\circ\mathrm{C}$. The same approach reaches sub-degree accuracy ($0.21\,^\circ\mathrm{C}$) over a narrow temperature window and a single calibration valid across different pump powers and wavelengths. These results establish 1D-CNNs as robust single-shot thermometers and a step toward fast thermometry of single and trapped particles.

</details>


### [51] [Picojoule-level self-referencing with dual dispersive waves in thin-film lithium niobate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28304)
*Kiyoung Ko, Ian Christen, Kamila Kunes, Clayton Cheung, Chun-Ho Lee, Kiwon Kwon, Mihir Chaudhari, Ran Yin, Tushar Sanjay Karnik, Mengjie Yu*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: A monolithic thin-film lithium niobate waveguide combines Kerr spectral broadening and quasi-phase-matched second-harmonic generation to achieve picojoule-level f-2f self-referencing and low-energy carrier-envelope-offset stabilization at telecom wavelengths.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to reduce the pulse energy required for f-2f self-referencing and carrier-envelope-offset frequency stabilization of telecom-band pulses by using dispersion-engineered on-chip nonlinear optics.

Method: The authors use a monolithic thin-film lithium niobate waveguide with Kerr spectral broadening and quasi-phase-matched second-harmonic generation. Dispersion engineering generates dual dispersive waves near 1 and 2 µm symmetrically around the pump; mode-crossing dispersive-wave generation, a chirped poling period, and a poled section aligned with dispersive-wave onset are used to mitigate temporal walk-off.

Result: With 16.8 pJ on-chip pulse energy, the device produces an fceo beatnote with 32 dB SNR. The fceo of an Er-fiber laser is stabilized at 30 pJ, reported as the lowest pulse energy for telecom-wavelength fceo stabilization. The QPM section improves beatnote SNR by 43 dB and reduces required pulse energy fourfold compared with an identical unpoled waveguide.

Conclusion: Picojoule-level self-referencing and low-energy fceo stabilization are enabled in thin-film lithium niobate through combined Kerr broadening and QPM SHG, with dual dispersive waves and walk-off mitigation being key to performance.

Abstract: We demonstrate pulse-energy-efficient f-2f self-referencing of telecom-band pulses in a monolithic thin-film lithium niobate (TFLN) waveguide that combines Kerr spectral broadening with quasi-phase-matched (QPM) second-harmonic generation (SHG). Dispersion engineering generates dual dispersive waves (DWs) near 1 and 2 um, placed symmetrically around the pump to reduce the required spectral broadening. With an on-chip pulse energy of only 16.8 pJ, we detect a carrier-envelope-offset frequency (fceo) beatnote with a 32 dB signal-to-noise ratio (SNR), and we stabilize the fceo of an Er-fiber laser at 30 pJ, which is, to our knowledge, the lowest pulse energy reported for fceo stabilization at telecom wavelengths. Compared with an identical waveguide without poling, the QPM section enhances the beatnote SNR by 43 dB and reduces the required pulse energy fourfold. We further show that a mode-crossing DW, a chirped poling period, and a poled section aligned with the onset of DW generation to mitigate temporal walk-off are key to this performance.

</details>


### [52] [Observation of collective flatbands in hybrid Mie-grating THz metasurfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28246)
*Jie Ji, Thanh Xuan Hoang, Ayan Nussupbekov, Siyan Zhou, Kehan Wang, Yuri Kivshar, Jaime Gómez Rivas*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper proposes hybrid Mie-resonator/high-index-grating metasurfaces and experimentally observes quasi-flatbands with zero group velocity and strong energy localization in free-standing THz metasurfaces; grating-width tuning enhances the Q factor while preserving flatband dispersion and increases Purcell enhancement up to saturation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The work aims to create functional metasurfaces whose collective resonances form flatbands with zero group velocity and strong energy localization, using dielectric interconnections between Mie resonators as a geometric means to control multipolar hybridization and spectrally tune high-Q flatbands.

Method: Mie resonators are hybridized with high-index optical gratings in free-standing THz metasurfaces, where dielectric interconnections mediate multipolar hybridization. The authors use THz near-field measurements with dipole excitation, spatial mapping of the resonant electromagnetic response, continuous variation of grating width, and complementary Bloch-resolved Purcell calculations.

Result: Flatbands are experimentally observed. Near-field measurements uncover long-lived coherent oscillations associated with quasi-flat magnetic-dipolar resonant modes, and spatial mapping resolves the response across dielectric interconnections. Varying grating width tunes the hybrid magnetic-dipolar resonances, substantially enhances the Q factor while preserving quasi-flatband dispersion, and Purcell enhancement increases with Q before tending toward saturation, highlighting interplay between radiative confinement and local emitter-mode coupling. Exact Q values and material details are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: Engineering dielectric interconnections in periodic lattices is presented as a strategy for spectrally tuning high-Q flatbands in hybrid metastructures and tailoring localized electromagnetic interactions in functional optical metasurfaces.

Abstract: We propose a novel type of functional metasurfaces created by a hybridization of Mie resonators with high-index optical gratings. We demonstrate that such hybrid metasurfaces possess flatbands characterized by zero group velocity and strong energy localization. We experimentally observe the flatbands in free-standing terahertz (THz) metasurfaces, where dielectric interconnections between Mie resonators, forming the grating, mediate multipolar hybridization and provide a geometric control over collective resonances. Our THz near-field measurements with a dipole excitation uncover long-lived coherent oscillations associated with the quasi-flat magnetic-dipolar resonant modes, while a spatial mapping resolves the resonant electromagnetic response across the dielectric interconnections. We vary the grating width continuously to tune the hybrid magnetic-dipolar resonances and substantially enhance the quality factor (Q factor), while preserving the quasi-!atband dispersion. Complementary Bloch-resolved Purcell calculations reveal the substantial Purcell enhancement with increasing Q factor, followed by a trend towards saturation and highlighting the interplay between radiative confinement and local emitter-mode coupling. We believe our findings establish the engineering of dielectric interconnections in periodic lattices as a novel strategy for spectrally tuning high-Q flatbands in these metastructures, tailoring localized electromagnetic interactions in functional optical metasurfaces.

</details>


### [53] [Femtosecond laser-controlled production of functional titanium niobate layers from TiO$_{2}$/Nb$_{2}$O$_{5}$ powders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30549)
*Gonzalo Gomez-Munoz, Aarón Calvo-Villoslada, Inmaculada Álvarez-Serrano, María Luisa López, Paloma Fernández, Javier Solis, Belén Sotillo*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: A confined powder-bed femtosecond-laser technique was developed to form functional titanium niobate films directly from mixed TiO2 and Nb2O5 powders, bypassing prolonged high-temperature furnace synthesis. Laser pulse energy controls phase composition and morphology, and the formation mechanism appears related to laser-induced backward transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional titanium niobate synthesis requires prolonged high-temperature treatments. The work seeks an alternative, furnace-free route for manufacturing functional TNO oxide layers.

Method: A confined powder-bed femtosecond-laser-based technique was used to sinter TNO films from a TiO2/Nb2O5 powder mixture. The influence of laser-processing conditions on film formation, phase composition, and microstructure was systematically investigated, supported by comprehensive characterization of processed films.

Result: Different TNO phases and morphologies were obtained by controlling laser pulse energy. Characterization provided insight into film-formation mechanisms, which appear to share key features with laser-induced backward transfer processes. The abstract does not provide quantitative phase fractions, film properties, or specific application results.

Conclusion: The study establishes relationships between femtosecond-laser-processing conditions and TNO film formation, indicating that confined powder-bed femtosecond-laser processing is a potential alternative route for manufacturing functional oxide layers. Tentative applications are discussed, but specific applications are not identified in the abstract.

Abstract: Conventional synthesis methods of titanium niobates (TNO) require prolongated high-temperature treatments. To overcome this limitation, in this work, we have developed a confined powder-bed femtosecond (fs) laser-based technique to sinter TNO films starting from a mixture of TiO$_{2}$ and Nb$_{2}$O$_{5}$ powders, avoiding furnace-based approaches. The influence of laser-processing conditions on film formation, phase composition, and microstructure is systematically investigated, demonstrating that different TNO phases and morphologies can be obtained by controlling the laser pulse energy. A comprehensive characterization of the processed films has been performed, providing insights into the mechanisms governing their formation, which appear to share key features with laser-induced backward transfer (LIBT) processes. Our findings establish the relationship between laser-processing conditions and TNO film formation, highlighting the potential of confined powder-bed fs-laser processing as an alternative route for manufacturing functional oxide layers. Tentative applications of these layers are finally discussed.

</details>


### [54] [Stellar Temporal Intensity Interferometry with Adaptive Optics at the Palomar Observatory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30257)
*Darren Ming Zhi Koh, Xi Jie Yeo, Sam Halverson, Rebecca Oppenheimer, Gautam Vasisht, Christian Kurtsiefer, Peng Kian Tan*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates resolved stellar temporal photon bunching using ultra-narrowband filtering, high-speed single-photon counting, and the adaptive optics architecture at Palomar Observatory, reporting g^(2)(0)=1.3±0.1 from the red supergiant α Her and establishing stellar temporal intensity interferometry as an observational modality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spatial intensity interferometry has resurged in astronomy because it is immune to atmospheric phase turbulence, but its temporal counterpart, a cornerstone of quantum optics, has remained largely unexplored for astronomical sources. The work aims to show that high-visibility temporal photon bunching from stars is observationally accessible.

Method: The authors combine ultra-narrowband spectral filtering with high-speed single-photon counting detectors and leverage the adaptive optics architecture at the Palomar Observatory to observe stellar temporal photon bunching from the red supergiant α Her.

Result: The paper reports the first observation of resolved stellar temporal photon bunching, with a peak value of g^(2)(τ=0)=1.3±0.1 from α Her.

Conclusion: Stellar temporal intensity interferometry is established as a viable observational modality, bridging quantum optics with high-resolution stellar astrophysics.

Abstract: Spatial intensity interferometry has seen a major resurgence in astronomy due to its immunity to atmospheric phase turbulence, but its temporal counterpart - a cornerstone of quantum optics - has remained largely unexplored for astronomical sources. Here, we demonstrate that high-visibility temporal photon bunching signatures from stellar sources are now accessible by combining ultra-narrowband spectral filtering with high-speed single-photon counting detectors. Leveraging the adaptive optics architecture at the Palomar Observatory, we report the first observation of resolved stellar temporal photon bunching, with a peak of $g^{(2)}(τ=0) = 1.3 \pm 0.1$, from the red supergiant $α$ Her. This advance establishes stellar temporal intensity interferometry as a viable observational modality, bridging quantum optics with high-resolution stellar astrophysics.

</details>


### [55] [Integrable discretization of the Maxwell-Bloch equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30080)
*I. Kuk, I. Gabitov*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: An explicit, second-order integrable space-time discretization of the Maxwell-Bloch equations for a complex field in a resonant two-level medium preserves each Bloch-vector length and the exact field-medium energy balance, supports closed-form solitons and breathers, includes inhomogeneous broadening, and is analyzed as a numerical and scattering scheme.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional numerical schemes for the Maxwell-Bloch equations preserve the Bloch-vector length but not the integrable structure, so solitons are only approximate solutions. The authors seek a discretization that retains integrability and the associated conservation laws.

Method: The authors construct an integrable discretization in both time and space using a discrete Lax pair with the analytic structure of the continuous one. An auxiliary field variable is fixed by an isospectral normalization, yielding an explicit scheme based on a 2×2 similarity transformation of the density matrix per grid node. The lattice coincides with a known fully discrete integrable system, is used to derive closed-form solitons and breathers, and includes inhomogeneous broadening through ordered sublayers that preserve integrability. Scattering data evolution is analyzed on a finite time window and on the infinite line.

Result: The scheme conserves every Bloch-vector length and satisfies an exact energy balance between field and medium. The closed-form solitons and breathers are reflectionless potentials with energy 16Σ_j Im λ_j and converge to the McCall-Hahn soliton and Lamb's breather. On a finite time window, the scattering data evolve by a closed map, with the final medium state determined by data at one point of the spectral plane. On the infinite line, if the medium returns to its ground state behind the pulse, the transmission coefficient is conserved and the reflection coefficient evolves linearly with the multiplier of the linearized lattice. The method is explicit and second order, with cost per accuracy comparable to Cayley and exponential-midpoint schemes; an area-preserving input map with calibrated medium coupling makes it exact for a chosen soliton.

Conclusion: The lattice provides an integrable, conservative, explicit numerical scheme for the Maxwell-Bloch equations that preserves key structures, admits exact soliton and breather solutions, incorporates inhomogeneous broadening, and enables scattering analysis with accuracy competitive with standard schemes. No experimental validation or laser-plasma application is reported in the abstract.

Abstract: Numerical schemes for the Maxwell-Bloch equations keep the length of the Bloch vector but not the integrable structure, so that solitons are only approximate solutions. We write an integrable discretization, in both time and space, of these equations for a complex field in a resonant two-level medium in the physical variables and use it as a numerical scheme. The lattice follows from a discrete Lax pair with the analytic structure of the continuous one; an auxiliary field variable is fixed by an isospectral normalization, and the resulting scheme is explicit, a $2\times2$ similarity transformation of the density matrix per grid node. It conserves the length of every Bloch vector and satisfies an exact energy balance between field and medium. The lattice coincides with a known fully discrete integrable system, which we use to obtain closed-form solitons and breathers; they are reflectionless potentials with energy $16\sum_j\mathrm{Im}\,λ_j$ and converge to the McCall-Hahn soliton and Lamb's breather. Inhomogeneous broadening is included through ordered sublayers that keep integrability. On a finite time window the scattering data evolve by a closed map, with the final state of the medium given by the data at one point of the spectral plane. On the infinite line, if the medium returns to its ground state behind the pulse (which we show on the first row and observe numerically on later rows), the transmission coefficient is conserved and the reflection coefficient evolves linearly, with the multiplier of the linearized lattice. As a numerical method the lattice is explicit and second order, with a cost per accuracy comparable to that of Cayley and exponential-midpoint schemes, and an area-preserving input map with a calibrated medium coupling makes it exact for a chosen soliton.

</details>


### [56] [Improved two-way capacity upper bounds for amplitude-damping channels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29650)
*Stefano Pirandola*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper derives an improved upper bound, log2[1+(1-γ)γ^(γ/(1-γ))], on the two-way-assisted capacities of the qubit amplitude-damping channel with decay probability γ, covering quantum communication, entanglement distribution, private communication, and secret-key agreement. The bound strictly improves the PLOB relative-entropy bound, the analytic max-relative-entropy bound, and the optimized balanced squashed-entanglement bound for every 0<γ<1, and is extended to generalized amplitude damping including thermal excitation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for two-way-assisted capacities of amplitude-damping channels—the PLOB relative-entropy bound, the analytic max-relative-entropy bound, and the optimized balanced squashed-entanglement bound—are not tight, so improved upper bounds are needed to quantitatively limit adaptive communication through relaxing qubits. The work also aims to show how a sector-based simulation technique can remain useful for channels whose resource entanglement is not known exactly.

Method: Sector teleportation simulation is extended to qubits: phase rotations and permutations yield exact simulations within fixed-excitation subspaces while preserving the two-level input structure. The authors determine the exact, additive relative entropy of entanglement of the amplitude-damping resources and the exact regularized entanglement gain at fixed input population, then combine the amplitude-damping gain with unital and entanglement-breaking channels to obtain a closed expression. Bounds for generalized amplitude damping, including thermal excitation, are derived explicitly, and both results admit refinements under an average-excitation constraint.

Result: The new bound strictly improves the three established benchmarks for every 0<γ<1. At high damping, the known triple-rail protocol attains more than 99.54% of the bound's leading coefficient. For generalized amplitude damping, the closed expression recovers the zero-temperature result and vanishes throughout the entanglement-breaking region, improving established benchmarks over broad parameter ranges; the exact entanglement of the generalized resources remains open.

Conclusion: The derived bounds provide quantitative limits for adaptive communication through relaxing qubits and demonstrate that sector simulation can remain useful beyond channels whose resource entanglement is known exactly. The exact entanglement of the generalized amplitude-damping resources is left as an open question.

Abstract: We derive an improved upper bound on the two-way-assisted capacities of the qubit amplitude-damping channel with decay probability $γ$, given by $\log_2[1+(1-γ)γ^{γ/(1-γ)}]$. It applies to quantum communication, entanglement distribution, private communication, and secret-key agreement, and, for every $0<γ<1$, strictly improves the PLOB relative-entropy bound, the analytic max-relative-entropy bound, and the optimized balanced squashed-entanglement bound. At high damping, a known triple-rail protocol attains more than $99.54\%$ of its leading coefficient. Our method extends sector teleportation simulation to qubits: phase rotations and permutations give exact simulations within fixed-excitation subspaces while preserving the two-level input structure. We determine the exact, additive relative entropy of entanglement of the amplitude-damping resources and the exact regularized entanglement gain at fixed input population. We then derive explicit bounds for generalized amplitude damping, which includes thermal excitation. Combining the amplitude-damping gain with unital and entanglement-breaking channels gives a closed expression that recovers the zero-temperature result and vanishes throughout the entanglement-breaking region. It improves established benchmarks over broad parameter ranges, while the exact entanglement of the generalized resources remains open. Both results admit refinements under an average-excitation constraint. The bounds provide quantitative limits for adaptive communication through relaxing qubits and show how a sector simulation can remain useful beyond channels whose resource entanglement is known exactly.

</details>


### [57] [Observation of nonadiabatic quantum tunneling in a driven correlated insulator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28980)
*Mingyao Guo, Radu Andrei, Jaena Park, Je-Geun Park, Eugene Demler, David Hsieh*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: Time-resolved optical spectroscopy of the correlated van der Waals insulator NiPS3, with systematic tuning of drive field and frequency, reveals multiphoton-like resonances blue-shifted by ponderomotive effects within a tunneling-dominated absorption channel, indicating breakdown of adiabaticity at the Keldysh crossover; Hubbard-model simulations qualitatively reproduce the observations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many solid-state platforms for Floquet engineering, nonlinear transport, and high harmonic generation operate near the Keldysh crossover, but the microscopic pathways for photocarrier generation in this regime are poorly understood because neither perturbative multiphoton absorption nor adiabatic tunneling adequately describes the process.

Method: The authors performed time-resolved optical spectroscopy on NiPS3 while systematically tuning the drive electric field and frequency over a wide range. They also used Hubbard-model simulations based on a time-dependent Gaussian variational approach.

Result: In the crossover regime, the authors observed multiphoton-like resonances blue-shifted by ponderomotive effects within an otherwise tunneling-dominated absorption channel, providing a spectroscopic signature of adiabaticity breakdown. The Hubbard-model simulations qualitatively reproduced these features.

Conclusion: The results elucidate microscopic charge-excitation mechanisms in the Keldysh crossover regime and identify a key ingredient for controlling strong-field nonlinear dynamics in correlated materials.

Abstract: Many emerging solid-state platforms for Floquet engineering, nonlinear transport, and high harmonic generation operate near the Keldysh crossover, where optical fields are sufficiently strong and fast to reshape electronic states on sub-cycle timescales. Despite its importance, the microscopic pathways for photocarrier generation in this regime remain poorly understood, as neither perturbative multiphoton absorption nor adiabatic tunneling provides an adequate description. Here, we perform time-resolved optical spectroscopy on the correlated van der Waals insulator NiPS$_3$ while systematically tuning the drive electric field and frequency over a wide range. In the crossover regime, we observe multiphoton-like resonances that are blue-shifted by ponderomotive effects within an otherwise tunneling-dominated absorption channel - a clear spectroscopic signature of adiabaticity breakdown. Hubbard-model simulations based on a time-dependent Gaussian variational approach qualitatively reproduce these features. Together, our results elucidate the microscopic mechanisms of charge excitation in the Keldysh crossover regime and identify a key ingredient for controlling strong-field nonlinear dynamics in correlated materials.

</details>


### [58] [Handed Ultrafast Dynamics in Chiral Cyanine Nanotubes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28973)
*Indranil Ghosh, Keerthi Vaasan Mani, Caitlin C. Bellora, Gregory S. Engel*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: Circularly polarized two-dimensional electronic spectroscopy reveals helicity-dependent relaxation of longitudinal excitons in chiral J-aggregate cyanine nanotubes, with about 30% faster relaxation for RRXX than LLXX, while transverse excitons show no significant dependence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The abstract does not explicitly state a broader motivation; the study aims to detect and characterize helicity-dependent excited-state relaxation in a chiral exciton system.

Method: Unbundled, double-walled J-aggregate nanotubes of an amphiphilic cyanine dye were prepared using L-histidine as a chiral inducer. Circular dichroism quantified the net handedness, and two-dimensional electronic spectroscopy with circularly polarized pump pulses measured exciton relaxation dynamics.

Result: The chiral nanotubes showed a circular dichroism dissymmetry factor g_abs = 3.6 × 10^-2. Longitudinal exciton relaxation rates depended on pump helicity: 4.5 ps^-1 for ⟨RRXX⟩ versus 6 ps^-1 for ⟨LLXX⟩, a difference of about 30%, with the same behavior in inner and outer tubes. Transverse excitons showed no significant dependence.

Conclusion: Chirality and pump helicity bias exciton relaxation on femtosecond timescales for excitons delocalized along the helical structure.

Abstract: We detect helicity-dependent excited state relaxation in a chiral exciton system. Using L-histidine as a chiral inducer, we prepared unbundled, double-walled J-aggregrate nanotubes of an amphiphilic cyanine dye with a net handedness, which gives a large circular dichroism dissymmetry factor ($g_{abs} = 3.6 \times 10^{-2}$). With two-dimensional electronic spectroscopy and circularly polarized pump pulses, we find that the relaxation rates of longitudinal excitons depend on pump helicity (4.5 $ps^{-1}$ for $\langle \textit{RRXX} \rangle$ vs 6 $ps^{-1}$ for $\langle \textit{LLXX} \rangle$, a difference of about 30%) and is the same in the inner and outer tubes. Transverse excitons show no significant dependence. Chirality and pump helicity therefore bias exciton relaxation on the femtosecond timescale for excitons delocalized along the helical structure.

</details>


### [59] [photon number correlations in harmonics driven with classical and quantum light](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28498)
*Katarzyna M. Kowalczyk, Michael T. Weil, Kevin Murzyn, André Staudte, Jeff S. Lundeen, David M. Villeneuve, Giulio Vampa*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: The paper compares low-photon-number perturbative harmonic generation driven by classical light and bright squeezed vacuum to test whether strong harmonic correlations necessarily indicate genuinely quantum behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recent reports of nonclassical intensity correlations in harmonic generation raised the question of whether strong harmonic correlations necessarily imply genuinely quantum behavior.

Method: The authors compare harmonic generation driven by classical and bright squeezed vacuum sources at low photon number in the perturbative regime. They analyze auto- and cross-correlations in the harmonic spectrum using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and heralding measurements, and model the measured correlation-function scaling with mean harmonic flux using a click detector-response model.

Result: Bright squeezed vacuum produces strong auto- and cross-correlations in the harmonic spectrum, but the cross-correlations are fully classical and are explained by common fluctuations from the driving field. The measured correlation function shows visible scaling with mean harmonic flux, well explained by a click detector-response model.

Conclusion: Strong harmonic correlations do not necessarily imply genuinely quantum behavior; the observed cross-correlations can be classical and attributable to driving-field fluctuations.

Abstract: Recent reports of nonclassical intensity correlations in harmonic generation raised questions about whether strong harmonic correlations necessarily imply genuinely quantum behaviour. Here, we compare the low-photon-number perturbative harmonic generation driven by classical and bright squeezed vacuum sources. The latter generates strong auto- and cross-correlations in the harmonic spectrum. Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and heralding measurements show that the cross-correlations are fully classical and can be explained by the common fluctuations from the driving field. A visible scaling of the measured correlation function with mean harmonic flux is observed, which is well-explained by a click detector-response model.

</details>


### [60] [HEP-like 1550 nm wide-field pixelated optical receiver for inter-satellite laser links: a simulation-based proof of concept for Earth, lunar and Earth-Moon scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28071)
*Maurizio De Santis*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: A simulation study proposes a 1550 nm wide-field dome optical receiver with a ~1 Mpixel InGaAs pixel hybrid and event-driven, time-stamped readout for acquiring inter-satellite laser links, evaluating beacon detection, angle-of-arrival estimation, false alarms, lifetime, and Earth, lunar, and Earth–Moon scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Acquisition of inter-satellite laser links requires a wide-field receiver that can detect a pulsed beacon and supply angle-of-arrival information to a narrow-field data channel; the design is inspired by high-energy-physics pixel detectors.

Method: The paper simulates a dome receiver at 1550 nm using an approximately 1 Mpixel InGaAs pixel hybrid with event-driven, time-stamped readout. A pulsed beacon is detected by coincidence of m hits in a sliding block of pixels, and angle of arrival is estimated by clustering and centroiding before being handed to a narrow-field data channel. It considers a 550 km Earth constellation, lunar vicinity, and the Earth–Moon link, compares phase by phase with published scan-based acquisition, and examines cooling and radiation fluence.

Result: In the 550 km Earth constellation with a 5 mrad beacon and 10 mm aperture, detection takes 12 µs to 2 ms, or up to 116 ms with the 6–9 mm optics allowed, with whole-frame false-alarm rate below 0.01/s and angle-of-arrival error of 0.13–0.23 mrad. Cooling to about 233 K gives service life above 5 years for fluences up to about 2×10^9 p/cm² per year. Around the Moon and on the Earth–Moon link, it works only between high orbits. The search phase is 3–18 ms with 10 mm, shorter by about 10² to 10⁵ than scan-based acquisition; end to end, with assumed pointing and loop-closure times, it takes 0.16–1.5 s, comparable to a terminal with calibrated open-loop pointing but without requiring that pointing at the receiver, while the partner’s beacon must still be pointed at it to about 1.25 mrad for a 5 mrad beacon.

Conclusion: The simulation-based proof of concept supports the receiver concept for inter-satellite laser-link acquisition, particularly through a much shorter search phase. However, all reported values use idealised optics and a 1 Mpixel detector that does not exist today, and none is a measurement.

Abstract: I propose and evaluate by simulation a wide-field dome optical receiver (a field of view larger than a hemisphere) at 1550 nm for the acquisition of inter-satellite laser links. The detector is an InGaAs pixel hybrid of about 1 Mpixel with event-driven, time-stamped readout, inspired by the pixel detectors of high-energy physics. A pulsed beacon is detected by the coincidence of m hits in a sliding block of pixels, and the angle of arrival (AoA) is estimated by clustering and centroiding and handed to a narrow-field data channel. In a 550 km Earth constellation, with a 5 mrad beacon and a 10 mm aperture, detection takes 12 $μ$s to 2 ms (up to 116 ms with the 6-9 mm that the optics allow), with a whole-frame false-alarm rate below 0.01/s, and the AoA error is 0.13-0.23 mrad. Cooling to about 233 K gives a service life above 5 years for fluences up to about $2\times10^{9}$ p/cm$^2$ per year. The receiver is also studied around the Moon and on the Earth-Moon link, where it works only between high orbits. Compared phase by phase with published scan-based acquisition, the search phase (3-18 ms with 10 mm) is shorter by a factor of about $10^{2}$ to $10^{5}$; end to end, with assumed pointing and loop-closure times, it takes 0.16-1.5 s, comparable to a terminal with calibrated open-loop pointing but without requiring that pointing at the receiver; the partner's beacon must still be pointed at it to about 1.25 mrad (5 mrad beacon). All values are simulation results, obtained with idealised optics and a 1 Mpixel detector that does not exist today; none is a measurement.

</details>

### [61] [Mass-conservative and phase-consistent transport of phase-confined scalars in front tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30693)
*Ayman Mazloum, Gabriele Gennari, Fabian Denner, Berend van Wachem*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: A front-tracking scalar-transport method transports phase-averaged concentration, rebuilds cell-averaged concentration using the tracked front's phase fraction, and restores global mass by a uniform shift; it improves accuracy and prevents phase leakage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In front-tracking multiphase-flow simulations, phase-confined scalars are transported on a fixed Eulerian mesh independently of the Lagrangian front. This can make the scalar inconsistent with the interface: conservative high-order schemes may smear the scalar across the interface, leak it into the other phase, and distort near-interface concentrations despite conserving total mass.

Method: The method transports the phase-averaged concentration, which is smooth across the interface, rather than the cell-averaged concentration. The phase-averaged concentration is obtained by dividing the cell-averaged concentration by the phase fraction, extended linearly across the interface, and advected. The cell-averaged concentration is then rebuilt by multiplying the transported concentration by the front's volume-preserving phase fraction. Because this rebuild can alter total mass, a uniform shift of the phase-averaged concentration restores it.

Result: The method was compared with direct transport of the cell-averaged concentration using the same scheme in five three-dimensional cases: three drops (translation, reversible vortex, and localized source) and a rising bubble with and without dissolved surfactant. In the four cases without surfactant, it reduced the error against the exact solution by more than a factor of ten at the finest resolution and converged faster for the drops. In the surfactant-laden bubble, direct transport leaked 5.5–24% of the surfactant into the wake depending on resolution, whereas the proposed method kept it in the bubble.

Conclusion: The proposed transport method maintains consistency between phase-confined scalars and the tracked front while preserving global mass conservation, yielding substantially improved accuracy and preventing scalar leakage into the other phase.

Abstract: In front-tracking multiphase-flow simulations, the Lagrangian front defines the interface geometry and phase regions, while phase-confined scalars are transported independently on a fixed Eulerian mesh. This separation can make the scalar inconsistent with the tracked interface: even a conservative high-order scheme smears the scalar across the interface, leaks it into the other phase and distorts near-interface concentrations despite conserving total mass. We present a transport method that maintains consistency between the scalar and the tracked front while preserving global mass conservation. Instead of the cell-averaged concentration, the method transports the phase-averaged concentration, which is smooth across the interface. This concentration is obtained by dividing the cell-averaged concentration by the phase fraction, extended linearly across the interface and advected. The cell-averaged concentration is rebuilt by multiplying the transported concentration by the front's volume-preserving phase fraction. Because this rebuild can alter the total mass, a uniform shift of the phase-averaged concentration restores it. The method is compared with direct transport of the cell-averaged concentration by the same scheme in five three-dimensional cases: three drops (in translation, in a reversible vortex and with a localized source) and a rising bubble with and without dissolved surfactant. In the four cases without surfactant it reduces the error against the exact solution by more than a factor of ten at the finest resolution and converges faster for the drops. In the surfactant-laden bubble, direct transport leaks 5.5-24% of the surfactant into the wake, depending on resolution, whereas the proposed method keeps it in the bubble.

</details>


### [62] [Cyclic changes of frame for designing non-retracing verification cases for numerical interface tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30464)
*Dieter Bothe, Tomislav Marić*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper proposes non-retracing verification tests for numerical interface tracking by adding a cyclic change of frame to a forward–backward base deformation, so the frame returns to its initial pose while material trajectories differ. For geometric volume-of-fluid advection, discretely solenoidal face fluxes are constructed by circulating the transformed potential. In TwoPhaseFlow benchmarks, a full-turn frame increased final cell-average shape discrepancy in all 30 comparisons, with a median factor of 1.50, and reduced the fitted convergence exponent in all six benchmark-reconstruction comparisons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A forward–backward deformation test can supply an exact final interface, but retracing may allow errors from the two halves of the test to cancel, weakening its value for verification. Alternative tests are therefore needed that retain an exact final interface while avoiding simple retracing.

Method: A cyclic change of frame is superposed on the same base deformation. The frame returns to its starting pose, preserving the exact final phase and the deformation history up to rigid placement. A small returning translation can keep trajectories close to retraced ones, while a full rotation about a fixed centre or axis produces closed paths with nonzero winding for material labels away from that centre or axis. For incompressible base fields, the transformed velocity is divergence-free. Discretely solenoidal face fluxes for geometric volume-of-fluid advection are constructed by circulating the transformed potential. The approach is assessed with Three TwoPhaseFlow benchmarks using a shared time grid for each pair.

Result: A full-turn frame increased the final cell-average shape discrepancy in all 30 comparisons, with a median factor of 1.50. The fitted convergence exponent decreased in each of the six benchmark-reconstruction comparisons over the tested grids.

Conclusion: Cyclic frame changes can produce non-retracing verification cases that preserve the exact final phase and deformation history up to rigid placement. The reported benchmark results show that full-turn frame changes increase final shape discrepancy and reduce the fitted convergence exponent in the tested comparisons, indicating greater exposure of numerical error than retraced tests.

Abstract: A forward-backward deformation test supplies an exact final interface, but retracing can allow errors from the two halves to cancel. We construct alternative tests by superposing a cyclic change of frame on the same base deformation. The frame returns to its starting pose, preserving the exact final phase and the deformation history up to rigid placement. A small returning translation can keep trajectories close to the retraced ones. A full rotation about a fixed centre or axis gives closed paths with nonzero winding for material labels that stay off that centre or axis. For incompressible base fields the transformed velocity is divergence-free. We construct discretely solenoidal face fluxes for geometric volume-of-fluid advection by circulating the transformed potential. In three TwoPhaseFlow benchmarks, with a shared time grid for each pair, a full-turn frame increased the final cell-average shape discrepancy in all 30 comparisons, with a median factor of 1.50. The fitted convergence exponent decreased in each of the six benchmark-reconstruction comparisons over the tested grids.

</details>


### [63] [Topological analysis of permeability tensor in anisotropic porous media](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29731)
*Rafał Topolnicki, Paweł Dłotko, Maciej Matyka*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: Direction-aware topological descriptors, especially a throat-scale filtration, improve prediction of the full permeability tensor of anisotropic porous media from geometry, particularly the off-diagonal components that conventional descriptors capture poorly.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The permeability tensor of an anisotropic porous medium has small off-diagonal components that describe deflection of the macroscopic flux from the driving force and change sign under reflections. The authors ask whether direction-aware topological data analysis can predict the full three-dimensional permeability tensor directly from pore geometry, especially because non-topological baselines capture the off-diagonal components least well.

Method: The paper introduces two direction-aware topological descriptors for flow problems: a cone-based filtration adapted from earlier work on the elastic stiffness tensor, and a throat-scale filtration built on the distance transform of the pore space. Permeability tensors computed with the lattice Boltzmann method are compared against non-topological baselines comprising statistical microstructure descriptors and physics-based permeability estimators. The approach is also tested on real rock data.

Result: The topological descriptors are most informative for the off-diagonal permeability components. The throat-scale filtration alone outperforms every non-topological descriptor set, including the union of all statistical descriptors and physics-based estimators, and adding it to the baselines consistently improves prediction. The approach is confirmed on real rock data. Exact quantitative performance metrics and details of the rock dataset are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: Direction-aware topological data analysis, particularly the throat-scale filtration, is effective for predicting the full permeability tensor from porous-medium geometry and provides complementary information to statistical and physics-based descriptors, especially for off-diagonal components. The abstract reports confirmation on real rock data but does not provide numerical accuracy values or dataset specifications.

Abstract: The permeability of an anisotropic porous medium is a tensor whose off-diagonal components, which describe the deflection of the macroscopic flux away from the driving force, are small and change sign under reflections. We ask whether direction-aware topological data analysis can predict the full permeability tensor of three-dimensional porous media directly from their geometry. We introduce two direction-aware topological descriptors for flow problems: a cone-based filtration, adapted from our earlier work on the elastic stiffness tensor, and a throat-scale filtration built on the distance transform of the pore space. Using permeability tensors computed with the lattice Boltzmann method, we compare them with a comprehensive set of non-topological baselines, comprising statistical microstructure descriptors and physics-based permeability estimators. The topological descriptors are most informative for the off-diagonal components, which the baselines capture least well. There the throat-scale filtration alone outperforms every non-topological descriptor set, including the union of all statistical descriptors and physics-based estimators, and adding it to the baselines consistently improves the prediction. We also confirm the approach on real rock data.

</details>


### [64] [Coarse-Grained Description of Stochastic Neutron Transport in Highly Heterogeneous Reactor Media via Non-Local Feller Boundary Functionals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29680)
*V. V. Ryazanov*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper proposes a hierarchical coarse-graining framework for non-Markovian neutron transport in ultra-heterogeneous subcooled nucleate boiling reactor media, using Feller subordination and Complete Bernstein Functions to derive a non-local Fick law, establish statistical-manifold thermodynamic consistency, and construct a stability diagnostic for a WWER-1000 core in Mathcad Prime 8.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to model non-Markovian neutron transport within highly heterogeneous, multi-phase subcooled nucleate boiling layers of a nuclear reactor core and to evaluate operational safety margins under transient conditions, including Hopf bifurcations and spatial power oscillations.

Method: The method applies Feller subordination theory to continuous-time random walks and integrates microscopic fluid fluctuations and vapor bubble dynamics into macroscopic transport operators via Complete Bernstein Functions. A modified non-local Fick law is derived with a P1-Legendre polynomial expansion, mapping fractal porosity and phase boundaries into a stochastically renormalized effective diffusion coefficient. Thermodynamic consistency is grounded in Chentsov statistical manifolds; a generalized Lyapunov functional with a quadratic memory kernel and complex decomposition of the transfer function H(ω) are used. Analytical limits are parameterized for a WWER-1000 core and translated into an engineering computational sequence for Mathcad Prime 8.

Result: The framework yields a modified non-local Fick law with a stochastically renormalized effective diffusion coefficient, shows that granular Poisson-like shot noise produces a geometric-exponential family with constant negative information curvature, and provides analytical stability limits and a transfer-function decomposition. These are parameterized for a WWER-1000 core layout and converted into a computational sequence intended to diagnose Hopf bifurcations and spatial power oscillations. The abstract does not report quantitative validation results.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that a hierarchical, thermodynamically consistent coarse-grained description of stochastic neutron transport in highly heterogeneous reactor media can be formulated and translated into an engineering diagnostic procedure for transient stability. The abstract does not provide experimental or numerical validation.

Abstract: This paper presents a novel hierarchical coarse-graining framework to model non-Markovian neutron transport within the ultra-heterogeneous, multi-phase subcooled nucleate boiling layer of a nuclear reactor core. By applying Feller's subordination theory to continuous-time random walks, microscopic fluid fluctuations and vapor bubble dynamics are rigorously integrated into macroscopic transport operators through the apparatus of Complete Bernstein Functions (CBF). A modified non-local Fick's law is derived using a $P_1$-Legendre polynomial expansion, mapping the fractal porosity and structural phase boundaries into a stochastically renormalized effective diffusion coefficient. The thermodynamic consistency of the model is grounded within Chentsov's statistical manifolds, demonstrating that granular Poisson-like shot noise yields a geometric-exponential family of distributions characterized by a constant negative information curvature. To evaluate operational safety margins under transient conditions, a generalized Lyapunov functional with a quadratic memory kernel is constructed, and a complex decomposition of the transfer function $H(ω)$ is performed. The resulting analytical limits are parameterized for a WWER-1000 core layout and translated into an engineering computational sequence suitable for implementation in Mathcad Prime 8 to diagnose the onset of Hopf bifurcations and spatial power oscillations.

</details>


### [65] [A shock-fitting approach to high-order simulation of viscous hypersonic flows based on inviscid implicit shock tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29564)
*Huijing Dong, Alexander M. Perez Reyes, Matthew J. Zahr*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper proposes a shock-fitting framework for viscous hypersonic flows in which inviscid implicit shock tracking locates the bow shock, the post-shock domain is remeshed for the boundary layer, and compressible Navier–Stokes equations are solved with high-order discontinuous Galerkin; it is demonstrated on three high-Mach cases with good agreement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Computing viscous, shock-dominated flows at high Mach and Reynolds numbers is challenging, and fully coupled viscous shock tracking at such conditions demands extreme element compression and specialized formulations. The work aims to avoid resolving the shock structure in the viscous computation.

Method: High-order implicit shock tracking (HOIST) is applied to the Euler equations on a coarse, shock-agnostic mesh to obtain a shock-aligned mesh and shock geometry. Elements upstream of the shock are discarded, the post-shock domain is remeshed to resolve the boundary layer, and the compressible Navier–Stokes equations are solved with a high-order interior penalty discontinuous Galerkin method. The post-shock state is prescribed along the shock boundary using oblique shock relations.

Result: The framework is demonstrated on a Mach 10.6 axisymmetric blunt cone, a Mach 17.6 cylinder, and a Mach 5 sphere in three dimensions. Surface pressure and heat flux show good agreement with experimental measurements and reference computations.

Conclusion: The approach confines shock-tracking optimization to the coarse inviscid mesh and involves no mesh unknowns or additional nonlinear stabilization in the viscous computation. It avoids the extreme element compression and specialized formulations required by fully coupled viscous shock tracking at these conditions.

Abstract: This work presents a framework for computing viscous, shock-dominated flows at high Mach and Reynolds numbers in which the bow shock is located by inviscid implicit shock tracking and then treated as a boundary of the viscous computation. The high-order implicit shock tracking (HOIST) method is applied to the Euler equations on a coarse, shock-agnostic mesh to produce a shock-aligned mesh and the shock geometry; the elements upstream of the shock are discarded, the post-shock domain is remeshed to resolve the boundary layer, and the compressible Navier-Stokes equations are solved on it with a high-order interior penalty discontinuous Galerkin method, with the post-shock state prescribed along the shock boundary by the oblique shock relations. Because the shock structure is never resolved, the approach requires neither the extreme element compression nor the specialized formulations that fully coupled viscous shock tracking demands at these conditions; the tracking optimization is confined to the coarse inviscid mesh, and the viscous computation involves no mesh unknowns and no additional nonlinear stabilization. The framework is demonstrated on a Mach 10.6 axisymmetric blunt cone, a Mach 17.6 cylinder, and a Mach 5 sphere in three dimensions, with surface pressure and heat flux in good agreement with experimental measurements and reference computations.

</details>


### [66] [Scalable Modal Analysis of the Imperial Front Wing Flow Field](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29995)
*Parv Khurana, Benet Eiximeno, Alexandra Liosi, Spencer Sherwin, Athanasios Chatzopoulos, Julien Hoessler, Arnau Miró, Oriol Lehmkuhl, Ivette Rodriguez, J. Nathan Kutz*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper presents a scalable POD and Dynamic Mode Decomposition workflow in pyLOM for high-fidelity Formula 1 aerodynamics, applied to LES data of the Imperial Front Wing at Re = 2.2e5. It identifies four dominant vortical structures, extracts their frequencies, amplitudes and damping ratios, links selected Strouhal modes to specific flow features via point-probe spectra, and demonstrates modal compression from about 6 TB to 200 GB.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motorsport aerodynamic performance depends on understanding unsteady flow around complex geometries at high Reynolds numbers. Extracting dominant 3D coherent structures from high-fidelity simulations is difficult because of high state-space dimensionality, and standard POD does not provide interpretable temporal dynamics for the modes.

Method: The study uses a scalable POD and Dynamic Mode Decomposition method implemented in the open-source pyLOM framework. DMD is applied to large eddy simulation results of the Imperial Front Wing, a publicly available benchmark based on the McLaren MP4-17D, at Reynolds number Re = 2.2e5. The flow field is resolved with the spectral/hp element method via Nektar++, producing 49.72 million degrees of freedom at polynomial order p=4. DMD extracts frequencies, amplitudes and damping ratios, and point-probe spectra are used to associate modes with flow features.

Result: The simulation captures four dominant vortical structures: endplate, canard, main and footplate. Time-averaged force coefficients differ by 2.24% in C_L and 2.87% in C_D compared with reference data. DMD modes at chord-based Strouhal numbers of 5.1, 7.5 and 9.3 are associated through point-probe spectra with the main-element laminar separation bubble, canard vortex core pulsation and endplate vortex meander, respectively. The method reduces approximately 6 TB of snapshots to 200 GB of modal data.

Conclusion: Scalable POD and DMD in pyLOM can identify coherent structures and their temporal dynamics in high-fidelity Formula 1 aerodynamics simulations. The approach serves both as a model reduction technique and as a compression scheme for large snapshot datasets.

Abstract: The aerodynamic performance of motorsport vehicles relies on understanding the unsteady flow dynamics around complex geometries at high Reynolds numbers. Extracting the dominant 3D coherent structures from high-fidelity simulations is often intractable due to the high dimensionality of the state-space, and standard modal decompositions, such as proper orthogonal decomposition (POD), do not give interpretable meaning to the temporal dynamics of the modes. This study demonstrates a scalable POD and Dynamic Mode Decomposition (DMD) method, implemented in the open-source pyLOM framework, that can identify coherent structures and their temporal dynamics in high-fidelity simulations of Formula 1 aerodynamics, specifically the Imperial Front Wing (IFW), a publicly available benchmark based on the McLaren MP4-17D, at Reynolds number $Re = 2.2 \times 10^5$. DMD is applied to large eddy simulation (LES) results of the flow field that is resolved using spectral/hp element method via Nektar++, producing $49.72 \times 10^6$ degrees of freedom at polynomial order p=4. The simulation captures four dominant vortical structures (endplate, canard, main, and footplate), with time-averaged force coefficients showing 2.24% and 2.87% differences in $C_L$ and $C_D$ compared to the reference data. DMD extracts the frequencies, amplitudes and damping ratios of the coherent structures, and modes at chord-based Strouhal numbers of 5.1, 7.5 and 9.3 are associated, through point-probe spectra, with the main-element laminar separation bubble, canard vortex core pulsation and endplate vortex meander, respectively. The DMD allows for both a model reduction technique and a compression scheme, here reducing approximately 6 TB of snapshots to 200 GB of modal data.

</details>


### [67] [Flood forecasting using a physics-guided conditional denoising diffusion probabilistic model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31212)
*Omar Sallam, David Coe, Jeremy Feinstein, Hugo Torres, Sofia Sahagun Covarrubias, Eugene Yan*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: A conditional denoising diffusion probabilistic model is developed as a probabilistic surrogate for high-resolution regional flood forecasting, trained on shallow-water-equation simulations and optionally conditioned on coarse hydrodynamic outputs; it produces 20-member water-depth ensembles and achieves substantial speedups, with coarse 150 m conditioning giving the best overall skill despite ensemble under-dispersion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-resolution flood forecasting using full two-dimensional shallow water equation solvers is computationally expensive even on GPUs, motivating a faster probabilistic surrogate for regional-scale forecasting.

Method: The authors develop a conditional denoising diffusion probabilistic model (DDPM) that forecasts 30 m water-depth fields over a four-day horizon for the Des Plaines River watershed, Illinois. It is trained on SynxFlow SWE simulations of 113 historical storms observed across twelve Midwestern states from 2003 to 2024, each under three antecedent-wetness scenarios, and can optionally be conditioned on coarse 100 m or 150 m hydrodynamic simulations. Each forecast is a 20-member ensemble drawn from the same trained model because reverse diffusion is stochastic.

Result: Coarse conditioning reduced the four-day mean water-depth RMSE of the ensemble mean from 0.170 m to 0.054 m with 100 m conditioning and 0.048 m with 150 m conditioning. The 150 m conditioning also achieved the best critical success index (0.839), false alarm ratio, CRPS and Brier score at lower cost. Averaging members reduced error by 5-11% relative to a single realization, and the ensemble provided spatially explicit exceedance probabilities, although it was under-dispersive with spread-to-error ratios of 0.35-0.73. On a single GPU, a four-day forecast over 26 non-overlapping patches was 7.8x, 11.0x and 16.3x faster than the 30 m simulation for the 100 m, 150 m and unconditioned configurations, respectively; projected speedups for a 14-day forecast reached 12-49x for one member and 48-58x for 20 members.

Conclusion: Physics-guided diffusion models are promising probabilistic surrogates for regional-scale flood forecasting, although the evaluated ensembles are under-dispersive and finer hydrodynamic conditioning does not guarantee better skill.

Abstract: High-resolution flood forecasting with full two-dimensional shallow water equation (SWE) solvers remains computationally expensive, even on GPUs. We develop a conditional denoising diffusion probabilistic model (DDPM) that forecasts the evolution of 30 m water-depth fields over a four-day horizon for the Des Plaines River watershed, Illinois. The model is trained on SynxFlow SWE simulations of 113 historical storms observed across twelve Midwestern states (2003-2024), each under three antecedent-wetness scenarios, and can optionally be conditioned on coarse 100 m or 150 m hydrodynamic simulations. Because reverse diffusion is stochastic, every forecast is a 20-member ensemble drawn from the same trained model. Coarse conditioning reduces the four-day mean water-depth RMSE of the ensemble mean from 0.170 m to 0.054 m (100 m) and 0.048 m (150 m). The coarser 150 m conditioning also gives the best critical success index (0.839), false alarm ratio, CRPS and Brier score at lower cost, so finer conditioning does not guarantee better skill. Averaging members reduces error by 5-11% relative to a single realization, and the ensemble provides spatially explicit exceedance probabilities, although the ensembles are under-dispersive (spread-to-error ratios 0.35-0.73). On a single GPU, a four-day forecast over 26 non-overlapping patches is 7.8x, 11.0x and 16.3x faster than the 30 m simulation for the 100 m, 150 m and unconditioned configurations, and projected speedups for a 14-day forecast reach 12-49x for one member and 48-58x for 20 members. Physics-guided diffusion models are thus promising probabilistic surrogates for regional-scale flood forecasting.

</details>


### [68] [A centered finite-volume method with explicit pressure relaxation for weakly compressible single- and two-phase flows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31197)
*Shintaro Matsushita, Suhas S. Jain, Andrei G. Fedorov, Tetsuya Suekane*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: A non-dissipative centered finite-volume method for weakly compressible single- and two-phase flows couples kinetic-energy-preserving advection, conservative diffuse-interface regularization, and explicit pressure relaxation on collocated structured grids; harmonic face densities improve phase-mass positivity, and tests show very low kinetic-energy loss plus robust multiphase behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to provide low-dissipation, positivity-aware, energy-controlled transport for weakly compressible single- and two-phase flows, particularly when neighboring cells have different intrinsic densities and excessive phase-mass outflow can occur, while supporting future large-scale multiphase large-eddy simulation on GPUs with adaptive mesh refinement.

Method: The method uses a centered finite-volume formulation on structured collocated grids, coupling kinetic-energy-preserving advection with conservative diffuse-interface regularization and explicit pressure relaxation. Phase-mass fluxes are constructed with harmonic face densities to connect phase-volume and phase-mass transport without changing the advective kinetic-energy identity. A pressure-increment formulation retains the existing pressure gradient in the predictor, and compatible cell and face operators provide a conditional energy estimate for additional pressure iterations, with fully discrete budgets separating advection, predictor pressure work, and final correction.

Result: In inviscid homogeneous isotropic turbulence on 128^3 cells, the relative kinetic-energy change remains below 1.2×10^-7 in magnitude at t*=8 for both single-phase and density-ratio-1000 two-phase calculations, whereas matched upwind calculations retain only 3.60% and 30.72% of initial energies, respectively. Shear layers, cavities, rising bubbles, and dam breaks demonstrate applicability to incompressible-regime flows. In a three-dimensional dam break, harmonic averaging retains positive phase masses where arithmetic averaging produces negative values. With centered advection, a prescribed subgrid-scale stress supplies the principal dissipation.

Conclusion: The centered finite-volume method provides low-dissipation transport with phase-mass positivity benefits and controlled kinetic-energy behavior for weakly compressible single- and two-phase flows. Its local pressure algorithm and collocated finite-volume form support future large-scale multiphase large-eddy simulation on GPUs with adaptive mesh refinement.

Abstract: We present a non-dissipative centered finite-volume method for weakly compressible single- and two-phase flows. The method couples kinetic-energy-preserving advection to conservative diffuse-interface regularization and explicit pressure relaxation on structured collocated grids. To control excessive phase-mass outflow when neighboring cells have different intrinsic densities, we construct phase-mass fluxes using harmonic face densities. This connects phase-volume and phase-mass transport without altering the advective kinetic-energy identity, yielding a sufficient positivity condition and an inverse-density transport identity at finite velocity divergence. To retain the benefit of low-dissipation transport in the coupled calculation, a pressure-increment formulation incorporates the existing pressure gradient in the predictor. Compatible cell and face operators give a conditional energy estimate for additional pressure iterations, while fully discrete budgets separate advection, predictor pressure work, and the final correction. In inviscid homogeneous isotropic turbulence on $128^3$ cells, the relative kinetic-energy change remains below $1.2\times10^{-7}$ in magnitude at $t^*=8$ in both single-phase and density-ratio-$1000$ two-phase calculations. Matched upwind calculations retain only $3.60\%$ and $30.72\%$ of their initial energies, respectively. Shear layers, cavities, rising bubbles, and dam breaks demonstrate applicability to incompressible-regime flows. In a three-dimensional dam break, harmonic averaging retains positive phase masses where arithmetic averaging produces negative values. With centered advection, a prescribed subgrid-scale stress supplies the principal dissipation. The local pressure algorithm and collocated finite-volume form support future large-scale multiphase large-eddy simulation on GPUs with adaptive mesh refinement.

</details>


### [69] [Vortex rings shape the feeding-efficiency landscape of jellyfish](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31170)
*Harry Walden, Ashutosh Tripathi, Teo Lara, Brandon Weissbourd, Jörn Dunkel*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper models how jellyfish use self-generated vortex rings to balance prey capture against the energy cost of generating inertial flows, predicting an optimal feeding regime and explaining observed size and beat-frequency patterns across jellyfish taxa, with experimental support from Aurelia aurita.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The effects of inertial flows on the feeding efficiency of larger marine organisms are less understood than low-Reynolds-number microbial locomotion. The study examines the trade-off between prey capture rate and energy expended to generate inertial flows by surface deformations, using jellyfish as an archetypal system.

Method: The authors develop a biophysical feeding-efficiency landscape based on the trade-off between prey capture rate and energy expenditure from surface deformations. The model predicts an optimal feeding regime at the transition from Stokes pumping to inertial prey transport, mediated by a self-generated vortex ring, and is compared with experimental observations of the moon jellyfish Aurelia aurita.

Result: The model predicts an optimal feeding regime at the Stokes-pumping-to-inertial-transport transition, mediated by a self-generated vortex ring. These predictions provide a unifying explanation for size and beat frequencies reported across jellyfish species from different taxa, and vortex-ring-dominated transport is supported by experimental observations for Aurelia aurita.

Conclusion: Jellyfish feeding efficiency is shaped by a vortex-ring-mediated transition between Stokes pumping and inertial prey transport. More broadly, centimetric marine predators can exploit inertial flows for efficient prey capture, and the proposed landscape offers a unifying biophysical explanation of observed jellyfish sizes and beat frequencies.

Abstract: Fluid flows enable and limit the feeding and locomotion of sea creatures across a wide range of scales. Although much is known about how microorganisms navigate life at low Reynolds numbers, the effect of inertial flows on the feeding-efficiency of larger marine organisms remains less well understood. Here, using jellyfish as an archetypal example, we study the trade-off between prey capture rate and energy expended to generate inertial flows by surface deformations. We develop a feeding-efficiency landscape that predicts an optimal feeding regime at the transition from Stokes pumping to inertial prey transport, mediated by a self-generated vortex ring. These predictions provide a unifying biophysical explanation of size and beat frequencies reported for jellyfish species from different taxa, with vortex-ring-dominated transport supported by our experimental observations for the moon jellyfish \textit{Aurelia aurita}. More broadly, our results demonstrate how centimetric marine predators can exploit inertial flows for efficient prey capture.

</details>


### [70] [Modeling of reactive porosity waves as a fluid release process in a dehydrating slab](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31131)
*Liudmila Khakimova, Konstantin Huber, Yury Y. Podladchikov, Johannes C. Vrijmoed, Boris Antonenko, Timm John*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: A fully coupled two-dimensional hydro-mechanical-chemical model shows that compositional heterogeneities in dehydrating serpentinites spontaneously localize porosity and fluid flow into connected channel networks, providing a mechanism for efficient fluid extraction from subducting slabs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dehydration of subducting serpentinites releases aqueous fluids that must be efficiently extracted from the slab, motivating investigation of how reactive porosity waves focus fluid flow without prescribed channel geometry.

Method: The authors developed a fully coupled two-dimensional hydro-mechanical-chemical model of reactive porosity waves in deforming and dehydrating serpentinite, using Gibbs free-energy minimization for the MgO-FeO-SiO2-H2O system, including dissolved SiO2. Initial SiO2 and FeO heterogeneities were prescribed from multiscale compositional maps of serpentinites from the Mirdita ophiolite, Albania.

Result: Intrinsic chemical variations trigger spontaneous localization of porosity and fluid flow during dehydration. Channelization arises from coupled decompaction, fluid-pressure buildup, buoyancy-driven flow, and metasomatic reactions involving infiltration of SiO2-poor hydrous fluid and olivine formation. The resulting fluid-rich channels propagate upward and form connected networks resembling natural vein systems.

Conclusion: Reactive porosity waves in compositionally heterogeneous serpentinites provide a robust mechanism for focusing fluid flow and enhancing fluid extraction from dehydrating subducting slabs.

Abstract: Dehydration of subducting serpentinites releases aqueous fluids that must be efficiently extracted from the slab. We present a fully coupled two-dimensional hydro--mechanical--chemical model of reactive porosity waves in deforming and dehydrating serpentinite. Thermodynamic closure is provided by Gibbs free-energy minimization for the MgO--FeO--SiO$_2$--H$_2$O system, including dissolved SiO$_2$ in the aqueous phase. Natural multiscale compositional maps of serpentinites from the Mirdita ophiolite (Albania) are used to prescribe initial SiO$_2$ and FeO heterogeneities. The simulations show that these intrinsic chemical variations trigger spontaneous localization of porosity and fluid flow during dehydration, without prescribing channel geometry. Channelization results from the coupling of decompaction, fluid-pressure buildup, buoyancy-driven flow, and metasomatic reactions associated with infiltration of SiO$_2$-poor hydrous fluid and olivine formation. The resulting fluid-rich channels propagate upward and develop connected networks resembling natural vein systems. Our results demonstrate that reactive porosity waves in compositionally heterogeneous serpentinites provide a robust mechanism for focusing fluid flow and enhancing fluid extraction from dehydrating subducting slabs.

</details>
