### [71] [Hydrodynamic interactions of self-propelled tandem flappers with passive pitching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31028)
*Maximilian Schenk, Gonzalo Arranz, Oscar Flores, Manuel Moriche, Manuel Garcia-Villalba*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: Simulations of two in-line self-propelled 3D flappers with passive pitching show a stable tandem equilibrium whose spacing grows with heaving phase difference; equal-stiffness tandems generally do not improve efficiency, but softening the follower can yield modest 2–3% gains at larger phase shifts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To characterize the self-arrangement, power requirements, and propulsive efficiency of tandem self-propelled flappers with passive pitching, and to test whether independently tuning follower stiffness improves tandem performance relative to an isolated flapper.

Method: Two in-line, finite-span, self-propelled 3D flappers were simulated with sinusoidal heaving and a phase difference Δφ; pitching was passive, governed by fluid–structure interaction and resisted by a torsional spring. Identical spring stiffnesses were first set to the value maximizing isolated propulsive speed, then follower stiffness was independently varied through a stiffness ratio 0.8 ≤ κ̃ ≤ 1.2 while the leader remained at its isolated optimum.

Result: The pair reached a stable equilibrium for all phase shifts, with a common propulsive speed and equilibrium distance increasing approximately linearly with Δφ. The leader was largely unaffected and performed similarly to an isolated flapper, while the follower required more power in every case. At equal stiffness, this power penalty exceeded the small speed increase, so the tandem generally gave no efficiency benefit; comparable efficiency occurred only at an intermediate phase shift. The follower’s power penalty was associated with reduced pitching amplitude due to the leader’s wake, with nearly unchanged pitching phase. Reducing follower stiffness lowered its power requirement and increased tandem efficiency by an almost phase-independent amount without significantly affecting equilibrium distance or propulsive speed; for the softest follower, tandem efficiency exceeded that of an isolated flapper across all phase shifts, with gains of about 2–3% for Δφ ≥ 90°.

Conclusion: Equal-stiffness tandem flappers generally offer no efficiency advantage over an isolated flapper because the follower’s additional power requirement outweighs its small speed gain. Independently reducing follower stiffness can improve tandem efficiency modestly and nearly independently of phase, making the softest follower configuration more efficient than an isolated flapper across the full phase-shift range.

Abstract: We simulate two in-line, self-propelled 3D flappers of finite span, driven by sinusoidal heaving motions with a phase difference $Δφ$. Pitching is passive, governed by fluid--structure interaction and resisted by a torsional spring. We first consider identical spring stiffnesses, set to the value that maximises the propulsive speed of an isolated flapper. We characterise the self-arrangement of the pair. The pair settles into a stable equilibrium for all phase shifts, travelling at a common propulsive speed and maintaining an equilibrium distance that increases approximately linearly with $Δφ$. The leader is largely unaffected by the presence of the follower and performs similarly to an isolated flapper, whereas the follower requires more power in every case. This additional power outweighs the small increase in propulsive speed, so that the tandem generally provides no efficiency benefit over an isolated flapper, with comparable efficiency reached only at an intermediate phase shift. For equal stiffness, the power penalty of the follower is associated with a reduction of its pitching amplitude by the wake of the leader, while its pitching phase remains nearly unchanged relative to the isolated flapper. Keeping the leader at the stiffness that maximises its isolated propulsive speed, we investigate whether the tandem performance can be improved by independently tuning the follower through the stiffness ratio $\tildeκ$ in the range $0.8 \le \tildeκ \le 1.2$. Reducing the follower stiffness lowers its power requirement and increases the tandem efficiency by an almost phase-independent amount, without affecting the equilibrium distance or propulsive speed significantly. For the softest follower considered, the tandem is more efficient than an isolated flapper across the full range of phase shifts, with gains of about 2--3\% for $Δφ\ge 90^\circ$

</details>


### [72] [Density-ratio invariance of the optimal passive pitching of a self-propelled three-dimensional flapper](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30979)
*Maximilian Schenk, Manuel Garcia-Villalba, Manuel Moriche*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: A numerical study of a self-propelled finite-span 3D flapper with prescribed heaving and passive pitching shows that, for solid-to-fluid density ratios 1, 10, and 100, the maximum propulsive speed is nearly identical and the propulsive-speed curves collapse when spring stiffness is shifted by its density-ratio-dependent optimum. The optimum is governed by feathering rather than resonance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The abstract does not state an explicit broader motivation. Its stated aim is to examine how spring stiffness and solid-to-fluid density ratio affect the passive pitching and self-propelled propulsion of a finite-span three-dimensional flapper.

Method: The authors perform numerical simulations of a self-propelled 3D flapper of finite span that heaves sinusoidally at its centre of mass while pitching passively. They vary the spring stiffness for density ratios rho_p/rho_f = 1, 10, and 100 and analyze propulsion speed, pitching phase and amplitude, effective angle of attack, hydrodynamic forces, input power, propulsive efficiency, and resonant stiffness including added inertia.

Result: For all three density ratios, the propulsive speed reaches approximately the same maximum, but the stiffness at which this occurs increases with density ratio. After shifting stiffness by the respective optimum, the speed curves collapse onto one curve. At optimum, all flappers pitch with the same amplitude and lead heaving by 113 degrees, giving the same effective angle of attack and vertical hydrodynamic force. Stiffer springs feather too little, causing leading- and trailing-edge separation and forces mainly in the heaving direction; softer springs pitch excessively and late, with weaker hydrodynamic forces outweighing feathering benefits. Input power rises with density ratio because of flapper inertia, reducing distance per unit input work, while propulsive efficiency shows the opposite trend. The optimal spring is stiffer than the resonant spring for all density ratios by a nearly constant amount.

Conclusion: The optimal passive pitching is set by the feathering motion it produces rather than by resonance. A flapper with a higher density ratio requires a stiffer spring to attain the same optimal feathering state, and the optimal propulsive speed is approximately density-ratio invariant.

Abstract: We study numerically a 3D flapper of finite span that propels itself by a prescribed sinusoidal heaving motion of its centre of mass, while pitching passively. We vary the stiffness of the spring for three solid-to-fluid density ratios, $ρ_p/ρ_f=1$, 10 and 100. For all three cases, the propulsive speed reaches approximately the same maximum, while the stiffness at which it occurs increases with the density ratio. When the stiffness is shifted by the respective optimum, the propulsive speeds of the three density ratios collapse onto a single curve. At the optimum, the three flappers pitch with the same amplitude and lead the heaving motion by $113^\circ$, so that they experience the same effective angle of attack and the same vertical hydrodynamic force. The passive pitching acts as a feathering motion that turns the flapper towards the incoming flow. Stiffer springs feather too little, so the effective angle of attack remains large, the flow separates at the leading and trailing edges, and the hydrodynamic force acts mainly in the heaving direction. Softer springs result in excessive and delayed pitching of the flapper, and the benefits of feathering are outweighed by weaker hydrodynamic forces. The optimum balances these two limits. The input power increases with the density ratio because of the inertia of the flapper, so that the distance travelled per unit input work decreases, whereas the propulsive efficiency, which accounts for the kinetic energy of the flapper, shows the opposite trend. A comparison with the resonant stiffness of the flapper considering the added inertia of quiescent fluid, shows that the optimal spring is stiffer than the resonant one for all density ratios, by a nearly constant amount. The optimal spring is therefore not set by resonance but by the feathering motion it produces, and a flapper of higher density ratio only needs a stiffer spring to reach it.

</details>


### [73] [Compressibility effects on the flow-induced motions of bluff bodies: common features from transverse and rotary oscillations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30903)
*Sawant Omkar Deepak, Swagata Bhaumik*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: Two-dimensional direct numerical simulations compare compressibility effects on transverse vortex-induced vibration of a circular cylinder and vortex-induced rotary oscillation of an elliptic cylinder. Compressibility stabilizes the lock-in branch but amplifies response in the second desynchronized zone, where amplitudes follow a modified Prandtl-Glauert relationship.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To examine how compressibility affects flow-induced motions of bluff bodies and to identify common features between transverse and rotary oscillatory responses across desynchronized and lock-in regimes.

Method: The study uses two-dimensional direct numerical simulations of undamped transverse vortex-induced vibration of a circular cylinder at Re=150 and vortex-induced rotary oscillation of an elliptic cylinder with aspect ratio 0.5 at Re=200, for free-stream Mach numbers 0.1≤M∞≤0.6. Responses are analyzed across the first desynchronized zone, the synchronous lock-in branch, and the second desynchronized zone.

Result: Compressibility stabilizes oscillations in the synchronous branch, reducing response amplitudes, fluctuating aerodynamic coefficients, and the extent and intensity of hysteretic jumps. In contrast, oscillation amplitudes and aerodynamic coefficients increase with M∞ in the second desynchronized zone, and the amplitude variation follows the modified Prandtl-Glauert relationship for both transverse and rotary cases. This is attributed to the inviscid nature of compressibility in the second desynchronized regime, whereas enhanced viscous stabilization moderates the synchronous-branch response.

Conclusion: The simulations are at low Reynolds numbers, but the identified trends are consistent with fundamental compressibility mechanisms and may therefore persist at higher Reynolds numbers.

Abstract: Compressibility effects on flow-induced motions of bluff bodies are examined through two-dimensional direct numerical simulations of undamped transverse vortex-induced vibration (TVIV) of a circular cylinder ($Re=150$) and vortex-induced rotary oscillation (VIRO) of an elliptic cylinder of aspect ratio $0.5$ ($Re=200$) for free-stream Mach numbers $0.1\leq M_{\infty}\leq 0.6$. Response characteristics are analyzed across the first desynchronized zone ($DS\text{-}I$), the synchronous (lock-in) branch ($SB$), and the second desynchronized zone ($DS\text{-}II$). While compressibility effects are noted to stabilize the oscillations in $SB$ in terms of reductions in response amplitudes and fluctuating aerodynamic coefficients, as well as the extent and intensity of hysteretic jumps, in contrast, the oscillation amplitudes and aerodynamic coefficients increase with $M_{\infty}$ in $DS\text{-}II$, where the amplitude variation with $M_{\infty}$ is shown to follow the \textit{modified Prandtl-Glauert relationship} for both TVIV and VIRO cases. This indicates the role of the inviscid nature of compressibility in the $DS\text{-}II$ regime in increasing the oscillation response, as opposed to $SB$ where enhanced viscous stabilization with increasing $M_{\infty}$ leads to the moderation of response. Although the simulations are performed at low Reynolds numbers, the identified trends are consistent with fundamental compressibility mechanisms and may therefore persist at higher Reynolds numbers.

</details>


### [74] [Spontaneous Axis-Switching Swirling in Transonic Core-Annular Flow of Highly Differentiated Fluids](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30823)
*Wayne Strasser, Aleck Tilbrook, Daniel Wilson*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper computationally reports spontaneous axis-switching swirling (SASS) in a transonic core-annular flow of steam surrounded by a cold non-Newtonian fuel. Wave pile-up produces three-dimensional surface disturbances and slender lobes whose secondary instabilities generate self-sustaining oscillatory tangential velocities at the base-wave frequency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior studies examined instabilities in core-annular flow and atomization systems, but spontaneous axis-switching swirling modalities in core-annular flow had not been explored. The paper aims to demonstrate this new behavior for steam surrounded by a cold non-Newtonian fuel.

Method: The authors use computational simulation with a previously validated computational tool. They analyze wave pile-up, three-dimensional fuel-surface disturbances, radial modal deformation, oscillatory tangential velocities, frequency content, and numerical uncertainty, including performance on a coarse, limited mesh.

Result: Wave pile-up created three-dimensional fuel surface disturbances that developed into thin slender lobes with radial modal deformation. Further instabilities produced oscillatory tangential velocities that were a large fraction of the core steam velocity and orthogonal to the axially flowing base fuel wave. Because these velocities were not externally imposed by atomizer geometry or boundary conditions, they were concluded to be self-generating. Frequency analysis showed SASS tended to operate at the base wave frequency. SASS behavior was also achieved on a coarse, limited mesh at relatively low simulation times.

Conclusion: Spontaneous axis-switching swirling can arise self-generatingly in a transonic core-annular flow of steam and a cold non-Newtonian fuel, operating near the base wave frequency and being capturable with a coarse mesh at relatively low simulation times.

Abstract: Forcing fluids to interact at high speeds creates a multitude of instabilities. Prior studies considered the consequences of these instabilities for core-annular flow (CAF) or atomization systems. However, the existence of spontaneous axis-switching swirling (SASS) modalities in CAF have never been explored. We computationally demonstrate this new behavior for a CAF of steam surrounded by a cold non-Newtonian fuel. Wave pile-up created 3-D fuel surface disturbances which eventually became thin slender lobes with radial modal deformation. Those slender lobes were subject to further instabilities and resulted in oscillatory tangential velocities that were a large fraction of the core steam velocity and orthogonal to the axially flowing base fuel wave. Since these tangential velocities were not externally imposed by atomizer geometry or boundary conditions, it was concluded that they are self-generating. Frequency analysis revealed that SASS tended to operate at the base wave frequency. We additionally explored numerical uncertainty with our previously validated computational tool and found that SASS behavior can be achieved on a coarse, limited mesh at relatively low simulation times.

</details>


### [75] [Discrete adjoint method for the general synthetic iterative scheme for optimization of rarefied and continuum gas flows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30491)
*Jianan Zeng, Ruifeng Yuan, Wei Su*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: A discrete adjoint method for the general synthetic iterative scheme enables efficient gradient-based shape and boundary-flow-parameter optimization of gas flows spanning rarefied and continuum regimes, with sensitivities validated against finite differences and convergence comparable to the primal solver.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to enable efficient gradient-based optimization of gas flows across rarefied and continuum regimes by constructing an adjoint solver for the general synthetic iterative scheme without requiring a separate Chapman-Enskog or asymptotic analysis.

Method: The primal general synthetic iterative scheme is formulated as a fixed-point system of kinetic and macroscopic synthetic equations, and both residuals are included in a discrete Lagrangian. Stationarity of this Lagrangian yields a coupled mesoscopic-macroscopic adjoint system that inherits the primal acceleration structure. The adjoint system is solved by alternating kinetic and macroscopic updates analogous to the primal scheme, with inner subproblems handled using established discrete adjoint techniques for the discrete velocity method and Navier-Stokes equations.

Result: On the NACA0012 airfoil, adjoint sensitivities closely match central finite-difference estimates, and the adjoint solver converges within tens of iterations in rarefied and near-continuum flows, comparable to the primal general synthetic iterative scheme. Multi-objective shape and boundary-flow-parameter optimizations further validate the method: for a three-dimensional re-entry capsule, peak wall heat flux is reduced by 14.17%, 10.68%, and 6.54% with lift-to-drag gains of 10%, 20%, and 25%; for a fixed-geometry showerhead chamber, inlet-flow allocation reduces the coefficient of variation of wafer pressure from 1.49% to 0.06% at fixed total gas supply while maintaining mean pressure near its initial value.

Conclusion: The formulation provides a systematic and extensible framework for constructing multiscale adjoint solvers directly from the fixed-point representations of their primal counterparts.

Abstract: A discrete adjoint method is developed for the general synthetic iterative scheme (GSIS) to enable efficient gradient-based optimization of gas flows across rarefied and continuum regimes. The primal GSIS is expressed as a fixed-point system of kinetic and macroscopic synthetic equations, and both sets of residuals are incorporated into a discrete Lagrangian. Imposing stationarity of this Lagrangian then yields a coupled mesoscopic-macroscopic adjoint system that directly inherits the primal acceleration structure, without a separate Chapman-Enskog or asymptotic analysis. The resulting system is solved by alternating kinetic and macroscopic updates analogous to those in the primal GSIS, with the inner subproblems treated using established discrete adjoint techniques for the discrete velocity method and Navier-Stokes equations. Tests on the NACA0012 airfoil show that adjoint sensitivities closely agree with central finite-difference estimates, while the adjoint solver achieves convergence efficiency comparable to that of the primal GSIS, with both solvers converging within tens of iterations in rarefied and near-continuum flows. The method is further validated by multi-objective shape optimization and boundary-flow-parameter optimization. For a three-dimensional re-entry capsule, shape optimization reduces peak wall heat flux by 14.17%, 10.68%, and 6.54% while achieving respective lift-to-drag gains of 10%, 20%, and 25%. For a fixed-geometry showerhead chamber, optimization of inlet-flow allocation reduces the coefficient of variation of wafer pressure from 1.49% to 0.06% at fixed total gas supply, while maintaining the mean pressure near its initial value. Beyond these applications, the formulation provides a systematic and extensible framework for constructing multiscale adjoint solvers directly from the fixed-point representations of their primal counterparts.

</details>


### [76] [Oceanic Surface Waves from Wind-Drift-Layer Instability: Exponential Growth to Finite-Amplitude States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30456)
*Sayali N. Jadhav, S. Ravichandran, Ratul Dasgupta*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper links linear wind-drift-layer instability to finite-amplitude ocean surface waves, showing that a Holmboe-unstable mode grows exponentially and then evolves into sheared Stokes-like propagating waveforms consistent with an exact nonlinear steady-wave boundary-value problem.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Oceanic wind-wave genesis by shear-induced linear instability and the propagation and breaking of finite-amplitude waves are traditionally studied as decoupled problems; despite experimental evidence of a connection, a first-principles demonstration that finite-amplitude waves emerge from linear instability remains challenging.

Method: The authors study gravity-dominated surface waves from an inviscid, shear-driven Holmboe instability using a background uniform air current and a finite-thickness, constant-vorticity water drift layer over deep irrotational water in the high-Bond-number, low-Froude-number regime. They perform two-phase simulations of the incompressible Navier–Stokes equations initialized with the fastest-growing eigenmode, use spatio-temporal spectral analysis, and formulate an exact nonlinear boundary-value problem for steady-state propagating waves including background shear.

Result: The simulations show initial exponential growth in excellent agreement with linear theory. Before breaking, finite-amplitude propagating waveforms develop, with bound harmonics locked to the primary carrier wave and residual free-wave components. Wave profiles from the exact nonlinear boundary-value problem agree very well with time-averaged profiles from the nonlinear stage of the transient simulations.

Conclusion: An infinitesimal, linearly unstable wind-drift-layer mode can evolve into a nonlinear propagating waveform of approximately permanent form under background shear. Sheared Stokes-like waves can naturally emerge during nonlinear evolution of a wind-drift-induced linear instability, extending existing theory and complementing wind-wave observations.

Abstract: Oceanic wind-wave genesis via shear-induced linear instability and the propagation and breaking of finite-amplitude waves have traditionally been studied as decoupled problems. While experimental evidence indicates connection between the two regimes, a first principles demonstration that the latter can emerge from the former remains challenging. Here, we bridge this gap by investigating gravity-dominated surface waves arising from an inviscid, shear-driven Holmboe instability. The background state comprises a uniform air current and a constant-vorticity water drift layer of finite thickness overlying deep irrotational water. In the high-Bond-number and low-Froude-number regime, this configuration supports Holmboe-unstable modes beyond a critical Froude number. Two-phase simulations of the incompressible Navier-Stokes equations, initialised with the fastest-growing eigenmode, exhibit initial exponential growth in excellent agreement with linear theory. In the subsequent nonlinear stage preceding breaking, finite-amplitude propagating waveforms develop. Spatio-temporal spectral analysis reveals bound harmonics locked to the primary carrier wave, coexisting with residual free-wave components. This indicates that an infinitesimal, linearly unstable mode can evolve into a nonlinear propagating waveform of approximately permanent form under background shear. To characterise these waveforms theoretically, we formulate an exact nonlinear boundary-value problem for steady-state propagating waves including background shear. The resulting wave profiles show very good agreement with time-averaged profiles extracted from the transient simulations in the nonlinear stage. These findings extend the existing theory and complement the wind-wave observations, demonstrating that sheared Stokes-like waves, can naturally emerge during the nonlinear evolution of a wind-drift-induced linear instability.

</details>


### [77] [Spatial dimensionality affects the convective thawing of permafrost](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30306)
*M. Magnani, S. Musacchio, G. Boffetta*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: Two- and three-dimensional simulations show that groundwater convection thaws permafrost and transports heat more slowly in 2D than in 3D, with convective plumes shaping the ice-water interface; accurate modeling requires 3D subsurface heat transport.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Permafrost thaw is a major consequence of climate change and is studied mainly with numerical models, but groundwater convection is underrepresented and the effect of spatial dimensionality on simulated thaw is unclear.

Method: The authors compared 2D and 3D numerical simulations of permafrost thaw and heat transport in the active layer using realistic parameter sets that enable groundwater convection.

Result: Permafrost thawing rate and heat transport were systematically slower in 2D than in 3D simulations. The ice-water interface developed spatial patterns aligned with convective structures, with rising cold plumes and descending warm plumes shaping its morphology.

Conclusion: Convective flow structures are coupled to the evolving geometry of the permafrost boundary, and three-dimensional representations of subsurface heat transport are important for accurately capturing permafrost thaw dynamics in land-surface models.

Abstract: Permafrost thaw is one of the major consequences of ongoing climate change, with potentially far-reaching implications for high-latitude and high-altitude environments. Because permafrost degradation is a subsurface phenomenon, its dynamics and future evolution are primarily investigated through numerical models. Yet, the role of groundwater convection in permafrost thawing is an underrepresented process. Here, we explore how the spatial dimensionality affects the simulation of permafrost thaw and heat transport within the thawed layer, known as the active layer. Using two- and three-dimensional (2D and 3D) simulations, with realistic parameter sets that enable convection, we show that permafrost thawing rate and heat transport are systematically slower in 2D than in 3D simulations. The ice-water interface develops spatial patterns that coincides with the underlying convective structures, with rising cold plumes and descending warm plumes shaping its morphology. These findings reveal a coupling between convective flow structures and the evolving geometry of the permafrost boundary, and highlight the importance of three-dimensional representations of subsurface heat transport for accurately capturing permafrost thaw dynamics in land-surface models.

</details>


### [78] [Flow-induced vibration of an array of circular cylinders in a square enclosure due to thermal buoyancy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30091)
*Amit Pramod Joshi, Nishit Gupta, Unnat Agarwal, Vaibhav Joshi*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: This study numerically investigates natural-convection-driven flow-induced vibrations of heated circular cylinder arrays in a square enclosure and their influence on heat transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper examines flow-induced oscillations of heated cylinder arrays in a square enclosure driven solely by thermal buoyancy, together with the resulting effects on heat-transfer characteristics.

Method: A coupled thermal-fluid-structure-interaction formulation based on a body-fitted arbitrary Lagrangian-Eulerian framework is implemented using variational finite element methodology at Prandtl number 7 and Rayleigh number 10^7 based on the enclosure side length.

Result: The stationary 3×3 array exhibits asymmetry and a non-dimensional wall-gap plume oscillation frequency of 0.183 on the left-column cylinders. When elastically mounted, these cylinders synchronize and lock in at a reduced velocity of 15.7, while the remaining cylinders experience broadband buffeting that grows monotonically with reduced velocity and exceeds the synchronized response. The 4×4 array is symmetric with no synchronization and shows forced buffeting for all cylinders, dominated by slow plume forcing for inner-column cylinders. Heat transfer changes negligibly for the 3×3 array but shows measurable enhancement for the 4×4 array.

Conclusion: Buoyancy-driven flow-induced vibration and heat transfer depend strongly on array configuration: the 3×3 array exhibits asymmetry and synchronization, whereas the 4×4 array remains symmetric without synchronization and achieves measurable heat-transfer enhancement.

Abstract: Flow-induced oscillations of heated cylinder arrays in a square enclosure driven solely by natural convection are investigated in the current study at Prandtl number of 7 and Raleigh number of $10^7$ (based on side of square enclosure). This is accomplished by a coupled thermal-fluid-structure-interaction formulation based on body-fitted arbitrary Lagrangian-Eulerian framework, implemented using variational finite element methodology. Study on stationary array shows asymmetry in the $3\times 3$ configuration, along with a non-dimensional wall-gap plume oscillation frequency of 0.183 on the left column cylinders. When elastically mounted, these cylinders synchronize and lock-in with the frequency at reduced velocity of 15.7. The remaining cylinders undergo broadband buffeting that grows monotonically with reduced velocity and exceeds the synchronized response. The $4\times 4$ array shows symmetric behavior with no synchronization. The response is observed to be forced buffeting for all the cylinders, and dominated by the slow plume forcing for the inner column cylinders. The motion of the cylinders also influence the heat transfer characteristics. The $3\times 3$ array shows negligible change in heat transfer for the whole array, while measurable enhancement is observed for $4\times 4$ array.

</details>


### [79] [A Steady Theory for Highly Unsteady Thin Airfoil Motions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30074)
*Scott A. Bollt*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper proposes a generalized steady thin airfoil theory based on a frame where absolute vorticity is steady, constructs a phase-space foliation for unsteady thin-airfoil motions, reinterprets classical stall and unsteady-flow results, and links quasi-steady and Theodorsen theories to a planar approximation of the foliation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional steady thin airfoil theory is built from steady flow and is insufficient for highly unsteady airfoil motions. The authors seek a generalized steady formulation grounded in flows that are steady in absolute vorticity within some reference frame, aiming to clarify unsteady stall, classical results, and prior experiments.

Method: The authors specialize a general steady-absolute-vorticity idea to construct a foliation of a phase space for thin airfoil motions, with phase-space coordinates given by the airfoil's total rotation rate, speed, and angle of attack. Staying on a leaf of the foliation maintains zero absolute vorticity in the fluid indefinitely. They relate the formulation to unsteady stall conditions, classical results, quasi-steady flow theory, Theodorsen unsteady flow theory via a planar approximation, and the experiments of McGowan et al.

Result: Within this class of flows, one preeminent unsteady stall condition is shown to be equivalent to the traditional steady stall condition. Various classical results can be reinterpreted as steady in the absolute vorticity field, and insight is provided into the surprising experimental results of McGowan et al. Parts of existing quasi-steady and Theodorsen unsteady flow theory can be built from a planar approximation of the foliation. Pure unsteady effects amount to a transversal of the leaves and occupy just one dimension. No quantitative experimental or computational results are provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: The proposed theory provides a framework in which quasi-steady extension is trivial and an unsteady extension appears feasible. Because pure unsteady effects reduce to a one-dimensional traversal of the foliation leaves, the work suggests future theoretical and experimental prospects, though the abstract does not report validation or quantitative performance.

Abstract: A generalized steady thin airfoil theory is built not from steady flow, but from flows in which there exists a frame of reference where the absolute vorticity field is steady. We specialize this general idea to construct a foliation of a domain of phase space for thin airfoil motions in which staying on a leaf of the foliation maintains zero absolute vorticity in the fluid indefinitely. The phase space is described by the airfoil's total rotation rate, speed, and angle of attack. It is found using this definition that one of the preeminent unsteady stall conditions is equivalent to the traditional steady stall condition for this class of flows, various other classical results can be re-interpreted as steady in the absolute vorticity field, and insight is also provided into the surprising experimental results of McGowan et al. (AIAA J. 49, 2011). We show parts of existing quasi-steady flow and Theodorsen unsteady flow theory can be built from a planar approximation of our foliation, making the quasi-steady extension of our theory trivial, and suggesting how an unsteady extension can be created as well. Furthermore, pure unsteady effects in the new theory amount to a transversal of the leaves, occupying just one dimension, inviting interesting prospects for future experimental and theoretical work.

</details>


### [80] [Collision of a sphere and a prolate spheroid settling in a quiescent ambient](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29733)
*Arnab Choudhury, Anubhab Roy*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper computes collision rates between a settling sphere and a prolate spheroid at low Reynolds number, combining geometric collision kernels with hydrodynamic interactions from a slender-body pair-trajectory model, then correcting near-contact behavior using boundary-element results and lubrication theory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Collision models for nonspherical particles settling in a quiescent fluid require collision kernels and efficiencies that account for orientation, hydrodynamic interactions, and near-contact lubrication, which are not captured by simple geometric cross-sections.

Method: The ideal collision kernel is derived from the projected cross-section enlarged by the sphere radius, in closed form for aligned spheroids and by one-dimensional quadrature for randomly oriented spheroids. Hydrodynamic interactions are modeled with first-reflection slender-body pair trajectories, considering strongly aligned weathervaning spheroids and freely rotating feathering/gliding spheroids. Boundary-element calculations assess near-contact gap closure, and a lubrication film correction is applied.

Result: Alignment increases the ideal kernel by at most a factor of 4/pi but extinguishes it on the extinction locus where an isolated broadside spheroid settles as fast as the sphere. Weathervaning lowers collision efficiency; feathering lowers it far from the extinction locus but gliding raises it near the locus. At finite alignment rates, collision rates can lie well above both the strong-alignment result and the ideal isotropic kernel, and collisions persist just above the extinction locus where strongly aligned spheroids are blocked. The boundary-element solution shows the slender-body model overestimates near-contact gap closure, while lubrication largely corrects this and reduces efficiencies from interception values.

Conclusion: The study provides simple fits for ideal kernels, collision efficiencies, and the near-contact correction for use in collision models. Specific quantitative parameter ranges and fitted coefficients are not provided in the abstract.

Abstract: The collision rate of a prolate spheroid and a sphere settling through a quiescent fluid at small Reynolds number is computed for randomly oriented spheroids and for spheroids turned broadside by weak fluid inertia. Without hydrodynamic interactions, the collision cross-section is the projection of the spheroid enlarged by the radius of the sphere. The ideal kernel then follows in closed form for aligned spheroids and from a one-dimensional quadrature for isotropic ones. Alignment raises the ideal kernel by at most a factor of 4/π, but extinguishes it on the extinction locus, where an isolated broadside spheroid settles as fast as the sphere. Hydrodynamic interactions are computed from pair trajectories in a first-reflection slender-body model, in which the interaction velocity is proportional to the difference of the buoyant weights and only the flow of the sphere rotates the spheroid. A strongly aligned spheroid weathervanes, whereas a freely rotating one feathers and glides. Weathervaning lowers the collision efficiency, as does feathering far from the extinction locus, but gliding raises it near the locus. There, the collision rate of spheroids aligned at a finite rate lies far above both the strong-alignment result and the ideal isotropic kernel. Just above the extinction locus, where strongly aligned spheroids are blocked, collisions persist at all the finite alignment rates computed. A boundary-element solution shows that the model overestimates the rate at which the gap closes near contact. A lubrication film largely corrects this and reduces the collision efficiencies from their interception values. Simple fits for the ideal kernels, the efficiencies and the near-contact correction are provided for use in collision models.

</details>


### [81] [Experimental study of the Reynolds number effect on the mean flow and coherent structures in a 180-degree bent pipe flow using PIV](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29694)
*Eman Bagheri, Christoph Heigl, Felix Czwielong, Stefan Becker, Philipp Schlatter*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: Planar PIV measurements in a 180° pipe bend with curvature γ = 0.2 for bulk Reynolds numbers ReD = 10,000–60,000 show Reynolds-number-dependent flattening of mean velocity profiles and formation of a downstream counter-rotating vortex pair, while coherent structures retain their spatial features and nearly unchanged Strouhal numbers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To study how Reynolds number affects the mean flow and coherent structures in a 180° bent pipe flow at γ = 0.2, and to obtain reliable PIV measurements despite refraction at the pipe wall.

Method: Planar particle image velocimetry (PIV) was performed in the pipe symmetry plane using water as the working fluid for ReD = 10,000–60,000. Refraction at the outer pipe interface was removed by refractive-index matching with a water–glycerol–sodium iodide mixture, and inner-interface refraction was corrected by reverse ray tracing. Measurements were validated against direct numerical simulations at ReD = 10,000 and 20,000 and hot-wire data at 60,000; planar velocity fields were also modal-decomposed.

Result: As ReD increases, mean velocity profiles in and downstream of the bend become flatter. A second vortex pair, rotating opposite to the Dean vortices, forms further downstream between bend angles of 116° and 126°. Modal decomposition shows that coherent structures keep their spatial features at all Reynolds numbers, with Strouhal numbers remaining nearly unchanged and showing no monotonic trend.

Conclusion: Reynolds number modifies the mean flow and leads to a downstream secondary vortex pair, but the coherent structures’ spatial features and Strouhal-number characteristics remain largely similar across ReD = 10,000–60,000.

Abstract: In this work, we study the effect of Reynolds number on the flow in a 180° pipe bend with a curvature of γ = 0.2. We perform planar particle image velocimetry (PIV) in the symmetry plane of the pipe for bulk Reynolds numbers ReD from 10 000 to 60 000 with water as the working fluid. To remove the refraction at the pipe wall, a hybrid method is employed. A mixture of water, glycerol and sodium iodide is used to match the refractive index of the pipe at its outer interface. The refraction at the inner interface is corrected using reverse ray tracing. The measurements are validated against direct numerical simulations at ReD = 10 000 and 20 000 and against hot-wire data from the literature at 60 000. As the Reynolds number increases, the mean velocity profiles in and downstream of the bend become flatter. Furthermore, a second vortex pair, with the opposite direction of rotation to the Dean vortices, forms further downstream, between bend angles of 116° and 126°. A modal decomposition of the planar velocity fields shows that the coherent structures keep their spatial features at all Reynolds numbers. Their Strouhal numbers remain nearly unchanged and show no monotonic trend.

</details>


### [82] [Stable eigenmodes of the Large-scale circulation in Turbulent Convection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29636)
*Yin Wang, Pik-Yin Lai, Penger Tong*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper develops a stochastic theory of the large-scale circulation in turbulent Rayleigh-Bénard convection, derives coupled equations for its angular velocity and dipolar temperature field, identifies stable eigenmodes, and validates their Rayleigh-number scalings in thin-disk-cell experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Turbulent Rayleigh-Bénard convection is a canonical buoyancy-driven turbulent system whose system-size large-scale circulation couples coherent motion to turbulent fluctuations; the paper aims to explain the dynamical origin of its quasi-periodic oscillations and fluctuation-driven cessations and reversals.

Method: Starting from the Boussinesq Navier-Stokes and temperature equations, the authors derive three coupled stochastic equations for the large-scale-circulation angular velocity and associated dipolar temperature field. They analyze deterministic fixed states, linearize about the stable finite-large-scale-circulation states, and compare predictions with experiments in a thin-disk cell.

Result: The deterministic dynamics contain an unstable no-large-scale-circulation state and two symmetry-related stable finite-large-scale-circulation states. Linearization yields one purely relaxational eigenmode and a pair of damped oscillatory eigenmodes with frequency set by the large-scale-circulation turnover rate; turbulent fluctuations continually excite the oscillatory modes. Experiments directly resolve both eigenmodes and show the predicted Rayleigh-number scalings, and the stochastic dynamics also provide a route to rare cessations and reversals.

Conclusion: The theory-experiment framework provides a tractable account of how coherent circulation states, intrinsic modes, and fluctuation-driven transitions emerge in turbulent convection, with broader relevance to geophysical and astrophysical flows.

Abstract: Turbulent Rayleigh-Bénard convection (RBC) is a canonical system for studying buoyancy-driven turbulence. Its defining feature is a system-size large-scale circulation (LSC), whose dynamics reflect the coupling between coherent motion and turbulent fluctuations. Starting from the Boussinesq Navier-Stokes and temperature equations, we derive three coupled stochastic equations for the LSC angular velocity and its associated dipolar temperature field. The deterministic dynamics contain an unstable no-LSC set and two symmetry-related stable finite-LSC states. Linearization about either finite-LSC state yields one purely relaxational eigenmode and a pair of damped oscillatory eigenmodes whose frequency is set by the LSC turnover rate. Turbulent fluctuations continually excite the latter, providing a dynamical origin for the observed quasi-periodic oscillations. Experiments in a thin-disk cell directly resolve both eigenmodes and show their predicted Rayleigh-number scalings. The same stochastic dynamics also provide a route to rare cessations and reversals. This theory-experiment framework provides a tractable example of how coherent circulation states, intrinsic modes, and fluctuation-driven transitions emerge in turbulent convection, with broader relevance to geophysical and astrophysical flows.

</details>


### [83] [Dynamic Renewal for Hydrodynamic Coupling in Ciliary Mucus Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29378)
*Jeroen Bechtold, Valentijn van Zwieten, Iris Verkuil, Meike F. Bos, Georg Rempfer, Mae Nesenberend, Pieter Michels, Florian K. R. Gaeremynck, Bart Stam, Debabrata Panja, Joost de Graaf*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper develops an immersed-boundary and Fourier-space Stokes simulation method with a dynamic-renewal algorithm to test whether a mucus strand's hydrodynamic disturbance affects its ciliary-driven reorientation; it finds only a limited reduction in reorientation rate, supporting the free-draining approximation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous modeling of single MUC5B-rich mucus strand reorientation used a free-draining coupling for cilia-induced flow, leaving unresolved whether the strand's own disturbance of the surrounding liquid assists or frustrates turning.

Method: The authors combine an immersed-boundary coupling with a Fourier-space Stokes solver and a bespoke dynamic-renewal algorithm that exposes the strand to new ciliary domain without enlarging the simulation volume.

Result: Hydrodynamic interactions produce only a limited decrease in the rate of strand reorientation, supporting use of the free-draining approximation; no quantitative values are provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: The methodology helps overcome a multiscale challenge of mucociliary clearance and provides a foundation for future studies of collective strand dynamics.

Abstract: Mucociliary clearance in large mammalian airways involves millimetric MUC5B-rich mucus strands that emerge from submucosal glands approximately aligned with the direction of transport. These strands are observed downstream in cross-flow networks. Recently, Bos et al. [PNAS Nexus 2, pgad388 (2023)] modeled single strand reorientation using a free-draining coupling for the flow induced by cilia. This leaves unresolved whether the strand's own disturbance of the surrounding liquid assists or frustrates the turning. Here, we develop the methodology to address this question. An immersed-boundary coupling is combined with a Fourier-space Stokes solver and a bespoke dynamic-renewal algorithm that exposes the strand to new ciliary domain without enlarging the simulation volume. This allows us to overcome one of the multiscale challenges posed by mucociliary clearance. We find that hydrodynamic interactions result in only a limited decrease in the rate of reorientation, which supports the use of the free-draining approximation. Future work can build on the methodological foundation introduced here to study collective strand dynamics.

</details>


### [84] [Transient Impact Force and Spreading Dynamics of Droplets Impacting Spherical Surfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29371)
*Mengqi Ye, Tianyou Wang, Zhizhao Che*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: A volume-of-fluid numerical study of droplets impacting spherical surfaces, quantifying how wettability, surface curvature, Weber number, and Ohnesorge number govern spreading, rebound, deposition, contact time, and transient impact-force peaks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Droplet impacts on spherical surfaces occur in spray cooling, anti-icing, and spray deposition. Target curvature alters the droplet spreading path and impact force, producing more complex kinematic and transient force dynamics that require systematic characterization.

Method: The authors use a numerical model based on the volume-of-fluid approach to simulate droplet spreading, retraction, rebound, and deposition on spherical surfaces. Parametric analyses examine wall wettability, spherical curvature, Weber number, and Ohnesorge number.

Result: Complete and partial rebound exhibit two impact-force peaks, while the second peak disappears in complete deposition. Decreasing wettability reduces maximum spreading length and contact time and substantially changes the second force peak; for complete rebound, contact time follows a specified contact-angle relation. Increasing dimensionless curvature lowers the critical contact angle for complete rebound. The dimensionless first force peak decreases with Weber number and approaches a constant according to a proposed relation. Increasing Ohnesorge number reduces maximum spreading length, prolongs contact time, and slightly increases the first force peak.

Conclusion: The study characterizes droplet impact dynamics and transient forces on spherical surfaces and provides parametric relationships linking wettability, curvature, Weber number, and Ohnesorge number to spreading, rebound, contact time, and impact-force peaks. The abstract does not report experimental validation or specific application-level performance outcomes.

Abstract: Droplet impacts on spherical surfaces are encountered in processes such as spray cooling, anti-icing, and spray deposition. The curvature of the impact target changes the spreading path of the droplet and the impact force, leading to more complex kinematic and transient force dynamics. This study employs a numerical model utilizing the volume of fluid approach to investigate the spreading, retraction, rebound and deposition characteristics of droplets impinging on spherical surfaces. The roles of wall wettability, spherical curvature, Weber number, and Ohnesorge number on droplet spreading and wall impact force are analyzed. The results show that the wall impact force exhibits two peaks in the complete rebound and partial rebound modes, whereas the second peak disappears in the complete deposition mode. Further parametric analysis shows that increasing the contact angle, i.e., decreasing wettability, reduces both the maximum spreading length and the contact time, and significantly changes the second peak of the impact force. Under complete rebound mode, the relationship between the contact time and the contact angle can be expressed as ${{{t}_{\text{c}}}}/{{{t}_{σ}}}\;={{C}_{1}}\left[ {{\left( 1-\cos {{θ}_{\text{eq}}} \right)}^{{-1}/{2}\;}}-{1}/{\sqrt{2}} \right]+{{C}_{2}}$, where $C_1$ and $C_2$ are constants. Within the investigated curvature range, increasing the dimensionless curvature reduces the critical contact angle required for complete rebound. The dimensionless first peak of the impact force decreases with increasing the Weber number and finally approaches a constant, following a relationship of $F_{\text{t}1}^{*}={{β}_{1}}+{{β}_{2}}W{{e}^{-1}}$, where ${{β}_{1}}$ and ${{β}_{2}}$ are constants. Increasing the Ohnesorge number reduces the maximum spreading length, prolongs the contact time, and slightly increases the first peak of impact force.

</details>


### [85] [Generation and characterization of unsteady inflows in a fan-vane wind tunnel](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29228)
*Yutong Liu, Gang Hu, Hongwei Zhang*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: Pseudorandom and periodic vane-angle forcing in a 768-fan fan-vane wind tunnel is used to broaden attainable turbulence intensity and integral length scale, with amplitude and command-spectrum shape controlling low-frequency velocity variance, correlation time, and integral scales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wind-tunnel experiments often require inflows with specific combinations of turbulence intensity and integral length scale, and the attainable range of these inflows needs to be broadened.

Method: Fifteen servo-driven vanes downstream of a 768-fan array received the same angle command while all fans operated at a constant duty cycle. Periodic and pseudorandom vane excitation were used, with variation of angular amplitude and command-spectrum shape. Hot-wire measurements covered twenty heights and selected spanwise and downstream positions, including regional analysis of a representative pseudorandom case.

Result: At 60% fan duty, increasing vane angular amplitude raised profile-mean turbulence intensity from 7.3% to 31.8% under periodic forcing and from 6.2% to 23.0% under pseudorandom forcing. With a fixed normalized pseudorandom command spectrum, larger amplitudes increased the low-frequency share of velocity variance, correlation times, and integral scales. At 15 degrees root-mean-square angular amplitude, changing the command spectrum produced profile-mean integral length scales of 0.88-1.67 m at similar turbulence intensities. Principal velocity-spectrum features persisted between 2 and 4 m downstream while integral scales continued to change. Both forcing types showed middle-region minima in turbulence intensity and integral scale, linked in a representative pseudorandom case to weaker low-frequency fluctuations and shorter correlation times.

Conclusion: Vane inputs can be selected according to the turbulence-intensity and integral-scale requirements of the intended test region, and pseudorandom vane excitation provides a basis for expanding attainable wind-tunnel inflow conditions.

Abstract: Wind-tunnel experiments often require inflows with specific combinations of turbulence intensity and integral length scale because both can influence the measured response. To broaden the range of attainable inflows, this study uses pseudorandom vane excitation to generate large-scale turbulence and examines how both quantities vary with the amplitude and spectral shape of the vane-angle command. In each test, the 15 servo-driven vanes downstream of a 768-fan array received the same angle command, and all fans operated at a common, constant duty cycle. Hot-wire measurements covered twenty heights and selected spanwise and downstream positions. At a fan-control duty cycle of 60%, increasing vane angular amplitude raised profile-mean turbulence intensity from 7.3% to 31.8% under periodic forcing and from 6.2% to 23.0% under pseudorandom forcing. With the normalized pseudorandom command spectrum held fixed, larger angular amplitudes increased the low-frequency share of velocity variance, accompanied by longer correlation times and larger integral scales. At a root-mean-square angular amplitude of 15 degrees, changing the command spectrum yielded profile-mean integral length scales of 0.88-1.67 m at similar turbulence intensities. The principal features of the velocity spectra persisted between 2 and 4 m downstream, while integral scales continued to change. Both forcing types exhibited middle-region minima in turbulence intensity and integral scale. Regional analysis of a representative pseudorandom case linked these minima to weaker low-frequency fluctuations and shorter correlation times. These findings provide a basis for selecting vane inputs according to the turbulence-intensity and integral-scale requirements of the intended test region.

</details>


### [86] [OpenGLSA: A high-order matrix-free solver for global linear stability analysis of compressible flows on structured grids](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29145)
*Rui-Qing Yang, Bao-Feng Ma*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: OpenGLSA is an open-source, MPI-parallel, high-order finite-difference solver for global linear stability analysis of compressible flows on structured curvilinear grids, supporting BiGlobal and TriGlobal analyses with matrix-free eigensolution and validated on several canonical flow cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the need for an efficient and tractable solver for memory-intensive global linear stability analysis of compressible flows, including complex geometries and fully three-dimensional base flows, while reducing memory requirements.

Method: OpenGLSA uses a high-order finite-difference method on structured curvilinear grids, MPI parallelisation, Fourier mode expansions for BiGlobal analysis of planar and axisymmetric two-dimensional base flows, and TriGlobal analysis for fully three-dimensional base flows. Base flows are computed with Selective Frequency Damping and WENO reconstruction for shock capturing. The linearised equations use a linearise-then-discretise approach, with eigenmodes computed by matrix-free time stepping coupled with the implicitly restarted Arnoldi method.

Result: The solvers are validated against published results for low-Mach-number flows past a circular cylinder and a sphere, hypersonic flow over a compression ramp, and square and cubic lid-driven cavity flows. The matrix-free strategy is reported to significantly reduce memory requirements and improve efficiency and tractability. No quantitative performance or accuracy metrics are provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: OpenGLSA provides an open-source, high-order, matrix-free capability for global linear stability analysis of compressible flows on structured grids, with validation across canonical two- and three-dimensional cases.

Abstract: We present OpenGLSA, an open-source code for the global linear stability analysis of compressible flows. It is based on a high-order finite-difference method on structured curvilinear grids and parallelised via the Message Passing Interface (MPI). The code supports linear stability analysis around planar and axisymmetric two-dimensional base flows with three-dimensional perturbations represented by Fourier mode expansions (BiGlobal), and on fully three-dimensional base flows (TriGlobal). Curvilinear coordinate grids are employed to facilitate flow analysis around complex geometries. Base flow computations employ the Selective Frequency Damping (SFD) method combined with weighted essentially non-oscillatory (WENO) reconstruction for shock capturing. A linearise-then-discretise approach is adopted for the linearised equations, and eigenmodes are computed using matrix-free time stepping coupled with the implicitly restarted Arnoldi method. This strategy significantly reduces memory requirements, thereby enhancing both the efficiency and tractability of memory-intensive compressible flow analyses. The solvers are validated against published results for low-Mach-number flows past a circular cylinder and a sphere, hypersonic flow over a compression ramp, and square and cubic lid-driven cavity flows.

</details>


### [87] [Fractal dimension of velocity-driven mixing: Reanalysis of Cao et al. (Phys. Rev. A 45, 3841 (1992)) and the universality of the Marstrand co-dimension rule](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29053)
*Rod W. Douglass, Steven R. Dunbar*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper reanalyzes Cao et al.'s reported fractal dimensions for velocity-driven mixing using grid-converged volume-of-fluid simulations, modern box-counting, and molecular dynamics. It finds that Cao's MD result largely measured thermal scatter rather than interface geometry; after filtering thermal noise, MD and continuum results converge to a common late-time dimension. It also derives linear-regime dimension formulas and examines the Marstrand co-dimension rule in 2D and 3D.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To revisit Cao et al. (1992), which compared box-counting fractal dimensions of mixing interfaces from continuum and molecular dynamics simulations at zero Atwood number, and to test the universality of the Marstrand co-dimension rule across models and dimensions.

Method: The authors use grid-converged geometric volume-of-fluid (VOF) simulations and modern box-counting algorithms, independent LAMMPS molecular dynamics simulations, and median binning at 2 sigma to filter thermal scatter by reducing the midpoint cloud to a curve. They also derive analytical formulas for the linear-regime box-counting dimension and perform a three-dimensional grid convergence study.

Result: The grid-converged continuum dimension depends on the initial-condition realization, D = 1.13-1.38. Cao's MD dimension measures thermal scatter of atomic positions rather than interface geometry, since a flat interface at t=0 gives D ~ 1.36; after thermal filtering, the MD trajectory converges to the same late-time D ~ 1.43 as the continuum. The linear-regime dimension is exactly D = 2 - beta for A > 0 and D = 5/2 - beta for A = 0, where beta is the spectral exponent of the initial perturbation; for white noise (beta = 0) neither formula applies, and Cao's early-time D ~ 2 is a boundary artifact. A 3D grid convergence study gives D_3D = 2.361 +/- 0.002 at 400x400x200, and Marstrand's co-dimension rule D_3D = D_2D + 1 shows a 7% discrepancy reflecting initial-condition-realization dependence at A = 0.

Conclusion: Cross-model universality is recovered when thermal scatter is filtered from the MD midpoint cloud, yielding agreement between molecular dynamics and continuum late-time fractal dimensions. Cao et al.'s MD-derived dimension is interpreted as an artifact of the box-counting window lying below the interface-structure scale. The Marstrand co-dimension rule is only approximately satisfied in the zero-Atwood-number case, with a 7% discrepancy tied to initial-condition realization dependence, unlike the buoyancy-driven case where the discrepancy decreases with resolution.

Abstract: Cao et al. [Phys. Rev. A 45, 3841 (1992)] compared box-counting fractal dimensions of interfaces from continuum (SOLA-VOF) and molecular dynamics (MD) simulations of zero-Atwood-number mixing driven by random velocity perturbations. They reported D ~ 1.1-1.4 across physical models. We revisit their results with grid-converged geometric VOF simulations and modern box-counting algorithms. The grid-converged continuum dimension depends on the initial-condition realization (D = 1.13-1.38). Independent LAMMPS simulations show that Cao's MD dimension measures thermal scatter of atomic positions, not interface geometry, because their box-counting window lies below the scale of the interface structure: a flat interface at t=0 gives the same D ~ 1.36. When the thermal scatter is filtered by reducing the midpoint cloud to a curve (median binning at 2 sigma), the MD trajectory converges to the same late-time D ~ 1.43 as the continuum, recovering cross-model universality. We prove that the linear-regime box-counting dimension is exactly D = 2 - beta (A > 0) and D = 5/2 - beta (A = 0), where beta is the spectral exponent of the initial perturbation. For white noise (beta = 0) neither formula applies; Cao's early-time D ~ 2 is a boundary artifact. A three-dimensional grid convergence study gives D_3D = 2.361 +/- 0.002 at 400x400x200. Marstrand's co-dimension rule D_3D = D_2D + 1 shows a 7% discrepancy reflecting IC-realization dependence at A = 0, unlike the buoyancy-driven case where the discrepancy falls with resolution.

</details>


### [88] [An Implicit Discrete Adjoint Unified Gas-Kinetic Scheme for Sensitivity Analysis and Shape Optimization in Multiscale Gas Flows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28923)
*Yuze Zhu, Hangkong Wu, Kun Xu*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper develops a matrix-free implicit discrete adjoint method for the steady unified gas-kinetic scheme (UGKS), enabling gradient-based sensitivity analysis and constrained shape optimization in continuum, transitional, and free-molecular multiscale gas flows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate and efficient gradient-based sensitivity evaluation is challenging for performance optimization in multiscale flows with complex geometries, especially when flow physics spans continuum to free-molecular regimes.

Method: The authors exactly linearize and transpose the converged fixed-point operator of the steady implicit UGKS. They use a hybrid differentiation strategy: local automatic differentiation of nonlinear kinetic kernels, combined with analytically derived tangent and transpose operators for coupled macroscopic–microscopic implicit updates and mass-conservation projection. The formulation avoids global Jacobian assembly and end-to-end reverse-mode AD memory overhead while maintaining discrete consistency for flow and geometric dependencies.

Result: Verification across continuum, transitional, and free-molecular regimes shows machine-precision stepwise duality with the tangent-linear solver and close agreement with central finite differences. A six-parameter shape-sensitivity analysis achieves 7.54X and 3.88X speedups for one adjoint solve over tangent-linear and central finite-difference evaluations, respectively. Constrained shape optimizations of a periodic Knudsen pump and a Mach-2 NACA0012 airfoil converge within ten design updates, improving normalized mass flow by up to 29.68% and reducing aerodynamic drag by 7.46%.

Conclusion: The proposed implicit discrete adjoint UGKS provides an exact, memory-efficient, and computationally efficient framework for sensitivity analysis and shape optimization across internal and external multiscale gas flows.

Abstract: Accurate and efficient gradient-based sensitivity evaluation remains challenging for performance optimization in multiscale flows with complex geometries. To address this challenge, this work develops a matrix-free discrete adjoint method for the steady implicit unified gas-kinetic scheme (UGKS) through the exact linearization and transposition of its converged fixed-point operator. The method employs a hybrid differentiation strategy that combines local automatic differentiation (AD) of complex nonlinear kinetic kernels with analytically derived tangent and transpose operators for the coupled macroscopic--microscopic implicit updates and mass-conservation projection. This formulation ensures exact discrete consistency across both flow and geometric dependencies while avoiding global Jacobian assembly and the memory overhead of end-to-end reverse-mode AD. Verification tests across continuum, transitional, and free-molecular regimes confirm machine-precision stepwise duality with the corresponding tangent-linear solver and close agreement with central finite differences. In a six-parameter shape-sensitivity analysis, a single adjoint solve achieves speedups of 7.54X and 3.88X over tangent-linear and central finite-difference evaluations, respectively. Finally, constrained shape optimizations of a periodic Knudsen pump and a Mach-2 NACA0012 airfoil converge within ten design updates, achieving up to 29.68% improvement in normalized mass flow and 7.46% reduction in aerodynamic drag across internal and external multiscale flows.

</details>


### [89] [Degenerate symmetric formulation and principal subsystems for chemically reacting multi-temperature fluid mixtures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28845)
*Dieter Bothe, Tommaso Ruggeri*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper constructs a degenerate symmetric first-order formulation and principal subsystems for chemically reacting multi-temperature fluid mixtures with independent constituent velocities and temperatures, viscosity, and heat conduction. It identifies a potential-generated Euler-flux subclass via vanishing Gibbs–Duhem one-forms, analyzes Hessian and dissipation structure, extends Boillat–Ruggeri principal subsystems to degenerate systems, and develops a thermodynamic Class-III-to-Class-II reduction through constrained entropy maximization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The abstract does not explicitly state a broader motivation. Its stated aim is to formulate chemically reacting multi-temperature mixtures with independent constituent velocities and temperatures, viscosity, and heat conduction, and to extend principal-subsystem and reduction constructions to degenerate systems.

Method: Starting from an entropy-admissible Class-III theory, the authors characterize when prescribed constituent Euler fluxes are generated by scalar potentials in conservative entropy variables. They use the main-field multipliers for conservative balances and zero-storage constitutive equations, examine spatial and temporal Hessians, eliminate auxiliary variables for separated transport laws, extend the algebraic Boillat–Ruggeri principal-subsystem construction to degenerate systems, impose commuting restrictions, and use constrained entropy maximization and relative-entropy splitting.

Result: Under the stated hypotheses, the necessary and sufficient condition is vanishing of the separate Gibbs–Duhem one-forms, implying locally additive entropy although the parent theory does not require it. The spatial Hessians are symmetric; the temporal Hessian is positive semidefinite and singular; and for separated transport laws the positive-semidefinite dissipative principal symbol factorizes. The construction yields a dissipative common-temperature system and a nested hierarchy of grouped-temperature Euler subsystems, with classical characteristic interlacing in the Euler hierarchy. Constrained entropy maximization defines an energy-equivalent common temperature and gives a thermodynamic Class-III-to-Class-II reduction for additive and nonadditive parent entropies; for a thermally reconstructed reference state, the full relative entropy splits exactly into the thermal projection deficit and the retained-variable relative entropy. A Class-II entropy-production identity is derived.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that a degenerate symmetric formulation and principal subsystems can be constructed for the considered chemically reacting multi-temperature mixtures, with a potential-generated Euler-flux subclass characterized by vanishing Gibbs–Duhem one-forms. It distinguishes thermodynamic projection from principal restrictions and dynamical relaxation limits, and provides a Class-III-to-Class-II thermodynamic reduction. No quantitative or experimental validation is reported in the abstract.

Abstract: We construct a degenerate symmetric first-order formulation and principal subsystems for chemically reacting multi-temperature fluid mixtures with independent constituent velocities and temperatures, viscosity, and heat conduction. Starting from the entropy-admissible Class-III theory of arXiv:2610.37637, we characterize when its prescribed constituent Euler fluxes are generated by scalar potentials in the conservative entropy variables. Under the stated hypotheses the necessary and sufficient condition is that the separate Gibbs--Duhem one-forms $ω_i$ vanish. This implies locally additive entropy, but it is not required by the general parent theory. In this subclass, the complete main field contains multipliers for both conservative balances and zero-storage constitutive equations. The spatial Hessians are symmetric, and the temporal Hessian is positive semidefinite and singular. For separated transport laws, eliminating auxiliary variables factorizes the positive-semidefinite dissipative principal symbol. We extend the algebraic Boillat--Ruggeri principal-subsystem construction to degenerate systems. Commuting restrictions yield a dissipative common-temperature system and a nested hierarchy of grouped-temperature Euler subsystems. Classical characteristic interlacing applies within the nested Euler hierarchy, whose temporal Hessians are positive definite. Separately, constrained entropy maximization defines an energy-equivalent common temperature and provides a thermodynamic Class-III-to-Class-II reduction for additive and nonadditive parent entropies. For a thermally reconstructed reference state, the full relative entropy splits exactly into the thermal projection deficit and the relative entropy of the retained variables. We derive the Class-II entropy-production identity and distinguish the thermodynamic projection from principal restrictions and dynamical relaxation limits.

</details>


### [90] [AI Meets Turbulence Modeling: Balancing Causation, Correlation, and User Intent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28794)
*Sharath S. Girimaji*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper proposes an operational framework for choosing data-driven turbulence modeling strategies according to flow non-equilibrium, the balance between physical causation and statistical correlation, and user intent. Using the Mori-Zwanzig formalism, it separates turbulent stress into a local response and a non-local memory integral, then maps equilibrium RANS local closures, RAAI-guided causal scale resolution for unseen geometries, and NIROM/POD correlational synthesis for known parameterized coherent flows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Turbulence states vary widely in modeling difficulty, so no single AI or data-driven approach suits all flows. The authors aim to classify data-driven turbulence modeling by balancing physical causation and statistical correlation through non-equilibrium flow memory and user intent.

Method: The authors use the Mori-Zwanzig projection formalism to decompose unclosed turbulent stress into a local response term and a spatio-temporally non-local memory integral. Near statistical equilibrium, they consider intrusive local constitutive relations in Reynolds-Averaged Navier-Stokes equations, while noting limitations from pressure-velocity coupling and eddy dimensionality. For non-equilibrium turbulence, they bifurcate modeling by intent: Rules-Augmented AI (RAAI) with first-principles causal scale resolution for predicting unseen geometries, or Non-Intrusive Reduced-Order Modeling (NIROM) combined with uncentered Proper Orthogonal Decomposition (POD) across multi-realization ensembles for rapid design in a known parameterized coherent flow class.

Result: The paper presents a conceptual operational framework rather than quantitative validation. It argues that near equilibrium local closures can remain acceptable engineering approximations despite not being strictly valid, that non-local memory in non-equilibrium flows invalidates local total-stress closures for unseen geometries, and that NIROM with uncentered POD enables zero-solver synthesis of stationary statistics and unsteady coherent evolution from governing dimensionless parameters in known parameterized flow classes.

Conclusion: The framework aligns physical causation, statistical correlation, and user intent across flow regimes, providing guidance for selecting data-driven turbulence modeling approaches. No experimental or numerical performance results are provided in the abstract.

Abstract: Turbulence states vary widely in modeling difficulty, requiring distinct paradigms; thus, no single AI or data-driven approach suits all flows. We propose an operational framework classifying data-driven turbulence modeling by balancing physical causation and statistical correlation via non-equilibrium flow memory and user intent. Using the Mori--Zwanzig projection formalism, we decompose unclosed turbulent stress into a local response ($τ_{ij}^L$) and a spatio-temporally non-local memory integral ($τ_{ij}^{NL}$). Near statistical equilibrium, rapid relaxation suppresses advective memory, letting local correlations capture physical causation via intrusive $τ_{ij}^L$ constitutive relations in Reynolds-Averaged Navier--Stokes (RANS) equations. Because the elliptic Poisson equation for fluctuating pressure couples stress evolution to eddy dimensionality beyond single-point velocity-gradient componentiality, local closures are not strictly valid even at equilibrium, yet remain acceptable engineering approximations. In non-equilibrium turbulence, modeling bifurcates by user intent. For predicting unseen geometries, large coherent structures sustain non-local memory ($τ_{ij}^{NL}$), invalidating local total-stress closures and requiring first-principles causal scale resolution guided by Rules-Augmented AI (RAAI), with resolution set by a recent non-equilibrium theory. Conversely, for rapid design in a known, parameterized coherent flow class, a purely correlational strategy suffices: combining Non-Intrusive Reduced-Order Modeling (NIROM) with uncentered Proper Orthogonal Decomposition (POD) across multi-realization ensembles yields zero-solver synthesis of stationary statistics and unsteady coherent evolution from governing dimensionless parameters. This framework aligns physical causation, statistical correlation, and user intent across flow regimes.

</details>


### [91] [Transient coupling of Marangoni spreading and interfacial waves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28784)
*Abhinandan Mohanty, Palas Kumar Farsoiya*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper analyzes the linear Cauchy-Poisson problem for an axisymmetric interface between two viscous fluids with independently prescribed initial surfactant and displacement distributions, using Hankel and Laplace transforms and direct numerical simulations. It shows that Marangoni-driven surfactant redistribution couples to interface deformation only through inertia: in Stokes flow the interface is not displaced, whereas with inertia the Marangoni flow excites capillary-gravity waves that subsequently transport surfactant and produce weakly damped oscillations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The study addresses how an initially non-uniform surfactant distribution generates waves on a deformable interface and how those waves then govern the redistribution of the surfactant.

Method: The authors solve the linear Cauchy-Poisson problem for an axisymmetric interface between two viscous fluids, released from rest with independently prescribed surfactant and interface-displacement distributions, using Hankel and Laplace transforms. They also perform direct numerical simulations with sharp geometric surfactant transport to verify the predictions.

Result: In Stokes flow, the pressure and viscous normal stress of the Marangoni flow cancel in the normal traction for any viscosity ratio, so surfactant relaxes without displacing the interface and the coupling is inertial. With inertia, the Marangoni flow initiates a capillary-gravity wave whose leading-order polarity for thin boundary layers is set by the sign of ρ_l μ_l - ρ_u μ_u; the surfactant is carried by the wave and its concentration performs weakly damped oscillations at the wave frequency after Marangoni relaxation of a flat surface has ended. The oscillation amplitude is given in closed form at small viscosity, its maximum does not coincide with that of familiar resonant damping, and surfactant and interface shape decouple exactly only when the two fluids have equal densities and viscosities. A localized displacement radiates a wave packet that the surfactant damps, and a localized surfactant redistribution initiates a wave on an initially flat interface. Direct numerical simulations verify these predictions.

Conclusion: The coupling between surfactant redistribution and interface deformation is inertial rather than viscous.
