Marangoni spreading can excite capillary-gravity waves that transport surfactant and produce weakly damped oscillations, with exact decoupling only for fluids of equal density and viscosity; the analytical predictions are supported by direct numerical simulations.

Abstract: How does an initially non-uniform distribution of surfactant generate waves on a de- formable interface, and how do those waves then govern the redistribution of the surfactant? We answer this question for the linear Cauchy-Poisson problem of an axisymmetric interface between two viscous fluids, released from rest with independently prescribed distributions of surfactant and of interface displacement, by Hankel and Laplace transforms. In Stokes flow the pressure and the viscous normal stress of the Marangoni flow cancel in the nor- mal traction on the interface, for any viscosity ratio, so that the surfactant relaxes without displacing the interface: the coupling between surfactant redistribution and interface de- formation is inertial. With inertia, the Marangoni flow initiates a capillary-gravity wave whose polarity, at leading order for thin boundary layers, is set by the sign of $ρ_l μ_l - ρ_u μ_u $, and the surfactant is then carried by the wave, so that its concentration performs weakly damped oscillations at the wave frequency after the Marangoni relaxation of a flat surface has ended. The amplitude of these oscillations is given in closed form at small viscosity; its maximum does not coincide with that of the familiar resonant damping, and the surfactant and the interface shape decouple exactly only when the two fluids have equal densities and viscosities. A localized displacement radiates a wave packet that the surfactant damps, and a localized surfactant redistribution initiates a wave on an initially flat interface. Direct numerical simulations with sharp geometric surfactant transport verify these predictions.

</details>


### [92] [How Brine Pockets Evolve and Release in Ice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28699)
*Xinyu Tang, Feng Wang, Yihong Du, Jin Hu, Yuki Wakata, Chao Sun*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The study tracks individual brine pockets in saline ice to show how bulk thermomigration elongates them and how proximity to the ice-water interface and liquid connectivity control their release, with a quasi-steady diffusion-resistance model explaining release times.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Liquid inclusions migrate through solids by melting at one end and freezing at the other, but how a macroscopic phase boundary regulates their deformation and release is poorly understood.

Method: Individual brine pockets in saline ice were tracked through bulk migration, elongation, and release at the ice-water interface, resolving the motion of both ends. A quasi-steady diffusion-resistance model was used to analyze half-length release time versus pocket length at connection.

Result: In the bulk, temperature-dependent thermomigration gradually elongates the pockets. Near the ice-water interface, the front slows, whereas liquid connection is followed by accelerated rear freezing and rapid shortening. These opposing responses arise from distinct changes in thermal-solutal transport: boundary proximity suppresses local thermal-gradient enhancement and slows front-end melting, while liquid connection enables salt exchange with the surrounding liquid and accelerates rear-end freezing. The model captures the dependence of half-length release time on pocket length at connection and identifies an effective external exchange length consistent with a salt-balance estimate.

Conclusion: Thermal proximity and liquid connectivity are distinct controls on inclusion evolution and release, providing a framework for understanding liquid redistribution in multicomponent solids.

Abstract: Liquid inclusions migrate through solids by melting at one end and freezing at the other, but how a macroscopic phase boundary regulates their deformation and release remains poorly understood. Here, we track individual brine pockets in saline ice through bulk migration, elongation, and release at the ice-water interface, resolving the motion of both ends. In the bulk, temperature-dependent thermomigration gradually elongates the pockets. Near the ice-water interface, the front slows, whereas liquid connection is followed by accelerated rear freezing and rapid shortening. These opposing responses arise from distinct changes in thermal-solutal transport: proximity to the boundary suppresses the local thermal-gradient enhancement and slows front-end melting, while liquid connection enables salt exchange with the surrounding liquid and accelerates rear-end freezing. A quasi-steady diffusion-resistance model captures the dependence of the half-length release time on the pocket length at connection and identifies an effective external exchange length consistent with a salt-balance estimate. Our results identify thermal proximity and liquid connectivity as distinct controls on inclusion evolution and release, providing a framework for understanding liquid redistribution in multicomponent solids.

</details>


### [93] [A Viscosity-Decoupled Uzawa Lattice Boltzmann Method for Incompressible Flows Across Reynolds Numbers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28609)
*Yuan Yu, Yaolong Yu, Yanhui Ding*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: A viscosity-decoupled Uzawa lattice Boltzmann method fixes collision relaxation at unity and introduces viscosity through a conservative post-streaming momentum-flux lift, achieving second-order accuracy across Reynolds numbers from 10^-4 to 10^6, large speedups over OpenFOAM, and improved enstrophy accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional lattice Boltzmann methods tie viscosity to collision relaxation, causing diffusive stiffness at low Reynolds numbers, non-hydrodynamic instabilities at high Reynolds numbers, and passband breakdown in variable-relaxation dual-time schemes; continuum incompressible solvers instead require global pressure-Poisson solves every step. The paper aims to decouple viscosity from collision relaxation while avoiding these limitations.

Method: The proposed Viscosity-Decoupled Uzawa Lattice Boltzmann Method (VDU-LBM) uses D2Q9 and D3Q19 discretizations, permanently fixes collision at unit relaxation under smooth harmonic scaling, and clears pre-collision non-conserved kinetic memory in one sweep. Constant, heterogeneous, and Smagorinsky eddy viscosities are imposed through a parameter-free post-streaming bond-symmetric momentum-flux lift that conserves discrete momentum pairwise without perturbing continuity. Variable-step BDF1/BDF2 marching is accelerated by a matrix-free local polynomial Uzawa smoother on the (d+1) conserved moments, combined with link-wise Bouzidi curved walls and shifted ghost open boundaries.

Result: Across Reynolds numbers from 10^-4 to 10^6, VDU-LBM achieves smooth low-Mach second-order space-time accuracy, accelerates unsteady DFG 2D-2 shedding per cycle by 14.27x to 15.85x over matched 20-core OpenFOAM-v2412, and lowers the 256^3 Re=1600 resolved enstrophy error by 2.25x versus hybrid recursive regularized LBM. On an NVIDIA A100 GPU, the fused D3Q19 solver reaches 80.9% to 85.5% of measured copy bandwidth in multi-pass port traffic and reduces bounded-relaxation diffusive step cost at high viscosity contrasts.

Conclusion: VDU-LBM decouples viscosity from collision relaxation, supports constant, heterogeneous, and Smagorinsky eddy viscosities through a momentum-conserving lift, and demonstrates broad-Reynolds-number second-order accuracy and substantial computational performance gains. The abstract does not provide additional limitations or broader validation beyond the reported cases.

Abstract: Conventional lattice Boltzmann methods (LBMs) tie viscosity to collision relaxation, which induces diffusive stiffness at low Reynolds numbers, non-hydrodynamic instabilities at high Reynolds numbers, and passband breakdown in variable-relaxation dual-time schemes, whereas continuum incompressible solvers require global pressure-Poisson solves every step. We propose a Viscosity-Decoupled Uzawa Lattice Boltzmann Method (VDU-LBM) in D2Q9 and D3Q19 that permanently fixes collision at unit relaxation under smooth harmonic scaling and clears pre-collision non-conserved kinetic memory in one sweep. Constant, heterogeneous, and Smagorinsky eddy viscosities enter through a parameter-free post-streaming bond-symmetric momentum-flux lift that conserves discrete momentum pairwise without perturbing continuity. Variable-step BDF1/BDF2 marching is accelerated by a matrix-free local polynomial Uzawa smoother on the $(d+1)$ conserved moments, paired with link-wise Bouzidi curved walls and shifted ghost open boundaries. Across $\mathrm{Re}\in[10^{-4},10^6]$, VDU-LBM achieves smooth low-Mach second-order space--time accuracy, accelerates unsteady DFG 2D-2 shedding per cycle by $14.27\times\text{--}15.85\times$ over matched 20-core OpenFOAM-v2412, and lowers $256^3$ $\mathrm{Re}=1600$ resolved enstrophy error by $2.25\times$ versus hybrid recursive regularized LBM. On an NVIDIA A100 GPU, the fused D3Q19 solver attains $80.9\%\text{--}85.5\%$ of measured copy bandwidth in multi-pass port traffic and cuts bounded-relaxation diffusive step cost at high viscosity contrasts.

</details>


### [94] [Numerical Study of Flow and Cavitation in Single-Liquid Injectors with Sharp-Edged and Chamfered Orifice Inlets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28527)
*Shreyash Gulati, Sven R. Walsberg, Timothy W. Nearhood, Jeffrey D. Moore*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: A CFD study of water flow and cavitation in single-liquid injectors compared sharp-edged and chamfered orifice inlets over pressure drops of 0.07–0.59 MPa and length-to-diameter ratios of 2, 5, and 10. The simulations were validated against experimental volumetric flow rates and showed distinct cavitation and hydraulic-flip behavior depending on inlet geometry, pressure drop, and length-to-diameter ratio.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to numerically investigate water flow and cavitation in single-liquid injectors with sharp-edged versus chamfered orifice inlets, accounting for different pressure drops and orifice length-to-diameter ratios, and to assess CFD predictions against existing experimental data.

Method: The investigators used Computational Fluid Dynamics with the k-ω Shear Stress Transport turbulence model, the Zwart-Gerber-Belamri cavitation model, and the Volume of Fluid method. CFD-predicted volumetric flow rates were validated against experimental data for sharp-edged and chamfered orifice inlets. Pressure drops from 0.07 MPa to 0.59 MPa and orifice length-to-diameter ratios of 2, 5, and 10 were considered.

Result: Strong cavitation occurred for sharp-edged orifice inlets with a length-to-diameter ratio of 5 and pressure drop greater than 0.2 MPa. A hydraulic flip occurred for the sharp-edged inlet with length-to-diameter ratio 5 and pressure drop 0.59 MPa, consistent with prior experiments. Cavitation inception occurred for chamfered inlets with length-to-diameter ratio 5 and pressure drops greater than 0.2 MPa. For sharp-edged inlets with length-to-diameter ratios of 2 and 10, strong cavitation was observed inside the orifice.

Conclusion: The study concludes that orifice inlet geometry, pressure drop, and length-to-diameter ratio strongly influence cavitation and hydraulic-flip behavior in single-liquid injectors, and that the CFD predictions are consistent with existing experimental volumetric flow-rate data.

Abstract: In the current work, the investigators used Computational Fluid Dynamics (CFD) to investigate water flow and cavitation in single-liquid injectors with sharp-edged and chamfered orifice inlets. The k-ω Shear Stress Transport (SST) turbulence model was used along with the Zwart-Gerber-Belamri (ZGB) cavitation model and the Volume of Fluid (VOF) method. The CFD predictions for the volumetric flow rate were validated against existing experimental data for the sharp-edged and chamfered orifice inlets. Pressure drops ranging between 0.07 MPa and 0.59 MPa were considered along with orifice length-to-diameter ratios of 2, 5, and 10. Strong cavitation was observed for the sharp-edged orifice inlet cases with a length-to-diameter ratio of 5 and a pressure drop larger than 0.2 MPa. A hydraulic flip was observed for the sharp-edged orifice inlet with a length-to-diameter ratio of 5 and a pressure drop of 0.59 MPa, which was consistent with the findings from the previous experimental study. Cavitation inception was observed for the chamfered orifice inlet cases with a length-to-diameter ratio of 5 and pressure drops larger than 0.2 MPa. For the sharp-edged orifice inlet with orifice length-to-diameter ratios of 2 and 10, a strong cavitation was observed inside the orifice.

</details>


### [95] [Distinguishing photothermal and photoelectrokinetic contributions to flow in light-activated microchannel architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28481)
*Sudharshan Anandan, Yuhang Fang, Lily Farmer, Maria Irineu, Shanmukhi Sripada, Tyler Hughes, Jared Nathan, Sathish Rajendran, Steven Wereley, Amy Marconnet, Thomas Beechem, Jeffrey L. Moran, David M. Warsinger*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: Depth-resolved particle tracking and infrared thermometry around a UVA-illuminated TiO2-Au Janus membrane show that recirculating flows are dominated by horizontal Hadley convection caused by light absorption in the gold film, not by electrokinetic effects; in thin channels, simulations indicate convection weakens and surface-driven osmotic flows may dominate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Light-driven flows near Janus surfaces could power microfluidic devices without external hardware, but their mechanism is often attributed to photoinduced electro-osmosis or diffusio-osmosis even though illumination also heats the surface and may drive natural convection.

Method: The authors mapped flow and temperature around a UVA-illuminated TiO2-Au Janus membrane using depth-resolved particle tracking and infrared thermometry, compared the measured velocity profile with wall-corrected Hadley theory, tested the effect of adding 100 mM NaCl to suppress electrokinetic flows, and used simulations to examine thin-channel behavior.

Result: Recirculating rolls formed on both sides with no measurable net flow across the membrane. Their cubic, reversing velocity profile identified horizontal Hadley convection driven by a lateral temperature gradient from light absorption in the gold film. The profile agreed with wall-corrected Hadley theory using one fitted parameter. Adding 100 mM NaCl left the profile unchanged, indicating convection dominates. Simulations showed convection falls below 1 μm/s in thin channels, where surface-driven osmotic flows could dominate.

Conclusion: Device geometry and surface chemistry determine whether convection or surface-driven osmotic flows govern light-driven microfluidic flows.

Abstract: Light-driven flows near Janus surfaces could power microfluidic devices without external hardware. These flows are often attributed to photoinduced electro- or diffusio-osmosis, but illumination also heats the surface, potentially driving natural convection. Here, we map flow and temperature around a UVA-illuminated TiO$_2$-Au Janus membrane using depth-resolved particle tracking and infrared thermometry. Recirculating rolls form on both sides, with no measurable net flow across the membrane. Their cubic, reversing velocity profile identifies horizontal (Hadley) convection, driven by a lateral temperature gradient as the gold film absorbs light. The measured profile agrees with wall-corrected Hadley theory using one fitted parameter. Adding 100 mM NaCl to suppress electrokinetic flows leaves the profile unchanged, indicating that convection dominates. Simulations show that convection falls below 1 $μ$m/s in thin channels, where surface-driven osmotic flows could dominate. Thus, device geometry and surface chemistry determine which mechanism governs light-driven microfluidic flows.

</details>


### [96] [Nonpremixed Flames Near the Leading Edge of Parabolic Cylinders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28437)
*Vinicius M. Sauer, Jose M. Martinez*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: A second-order matched-asymptotic analysis extends the classical stagnation-point boundary-layer description of nonpremixed flames near porous parabolic cylinders by including surface curvature and boundary-layer displacement, with validation against laminar Navier-Stokes solutions and quantification of effects on flame stand-off, scalar dissipation, wall shear, and wall heat flux.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The classical first-order stagnation-point boundary-layer description neglects surface curvature and boundary-layer displacement, so it may be inaccurate for nonpremixed flames in the forward stagnation region of porous cylinders.

Method: The authors use the method of matched asymptotic expansions to second order for a porous parabolic cylinder. The flame is modeled as a detached sheet with unity Lewis numbers, and the formulation accounts for thermal expansion and displacement of the entire reacting layer. Predictions are compared with laminar Navier-Stokes solutions of the same idealized problem.

Result: The expansion is verified against laminar Navier-Stokes solutions, with two-term errors decreasing approximately as the square of the expansion parameter in resolved cases. At Reynolds numbers typical of laboratory-scale burners, curvature and displacement move the flame away from the wall and reduce scalar dissipation rate, wall shear, and wall heat flux, while flame temperature changes little. These effects increase with injection, displacement contributes more over a longer porous section, and second-order terms considerably reduce first-order errors in flame stand-off distance and scalar dissipation rate.

Conclusion: Second-order curvature and displacement effects materially improve the description of nonpremixed flames near the leading edge of porous parabolic cylinders and are relevant at laboratory-scale burner Reynolds numbers, especially for flame stand-off distance and scalar dissipation rate.

Abstract: Nonpremixed flames in the forward stagnation region of porous cylinders are classically described by the first-order stagnation-point boundary layer, which neglects the effects of surface curvature and boundary-layer displacement. In the present work, the description is extended to second order for a porous parabolic cylinder using the method of matched asymptotic expansions. The flame is assumed to be a sheet detached from the porous wall with unity Lewis numbers, and the model accounts for thermal expansion and displacement of the entire reacting layer. The expansion is verified against laminar Navier-Stokes solutions of the same idealized problem, and in the resolved cases the two-term errors decrease approximately as the square of the expansion parameter. At Reynolds numbers typical of laboratory-scale burners, curvature and displacement move the flame away from the wall and reduce the scalar dissipation rate, wall shear, and wall heat flux, whereas the flame temperature changes little. These effects increase with injection, and displacement contributes more over a longer porous section. The second-order terms considerably reduce the errors of the first-order theory in the flame stand-off distance and the scalar dissipation rate.

</details>


### [97] [State of Understanding of High-speed Combustion: Can the Detonation Structure in Fuel-Air Mixtures Be Predicted?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28138)
*Alexei Y. Poludnenko, Sai Sandeep Dammati, Abeetath Ghosh*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: This paper assesses whether state-of-the-art chemical-kinetics and multidimensional computational models can predict freely propagating detonation properties across eleven fuel-air mixtures. It finds large discrepancies between predicted and experimental detonation cell sizes, especially at atmospheric and lean conditions, and concludes that these cannot be attributed solely to numerical resolution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-speed reacting flows are important for detonation-based propulsion and astrophysical phenomena, but understanding of high-speed combustion remains limited. The authors therefore examine how well modern physicochemical and computational models predict freely propagating detonation properties.

Method: The authors systematically survey eleven fuel-air mixtures, including hydrogen, methane, acetylene, ethylene, JP-10, and n-dodecane jet fuels, over ranges of upstream pressure, temperature, and equivalence ratio. They analyze detonation properties using prior numerical studies and new multidimensional, high-fidelity simulations with multi-step FFCM/HyChem reaction mechanisms. They compare predicted and experimental cell sizes, examine the thermodynamic state space and ignition-delay temperature sensitivity, assess constraints from direct ignition-delay measurements, and evaluate evidence for vibrational non-equilibrium.

Result: Simulations reproduce experimental cell sizes for H2/air at sub-atmospheric pressures or elevated upstream temperatures, but at atmospheric conditions predicted cell sizes disagree by factors of 3-6 at stoichiometric composition and far more in lean mixtures. Hydrocarbon fuels show comparable or larger discrepancies. The authors argue that these disagreements cannot be ascribed solely to computational factors such as numerical resolution, even at the continuum limit. Current evidence for a proposed role of vibrational non-equilibrium is inconclusive.

Conclusion: Modern models have substantial limitations in predicting detonation structure in fuel-air mixtures. Improved predictive capability requires better-constrained chemical kinetics and further understanding of the relevant physics; several key open questions remain.

Abstract: (Abridged) High-speed reacting flows are central to many natural and practical systems, from detonation-based propulsion to various astrophysical phenomena. Here we discuss the limitations in our understanding of high-speed combustion by asking how well modern state-of-the-art physicochemical and computational models can predict the properties of a freely propagating detonation. We present a systematic survey of eleven fuel/air mixtures, from hydrogen, methane, acetylene, and ethylene to JP-10 and n-dodecane jet fuels, at a range of upstream pressures, temperatures, and equivalence ratios. Detonation properties are analyzed using prior numerical studies in the literature, as well as the multi-dimensional, high-fidelity simulations performed in this work, which employ modern multi-step kinetics based on the FFCM/HyChem families of reaction mechanisms. We show that simulations reproduce experimental cell sizes in H$_2$/air at sub-atmospheric pressures or elevated upstream temperatures, yet at the atmospheric conditions, disagreement in the predicted cell sizes is a factor of 3 - 6 at stoichiometric composition and much more severe in lean mixtures. Hydrocarbon fuels show comparable or even larger discrepancies. We argue that such disagreement cannot be ascribed solely to the computational factors, in particular, numerical resolution even at the continuum limit. We analyze the thermodynamic state space occupied by detonations in various mixtures, describe the predicted chemical properties in various regions of the state space, in particular ignition delays and their temperature sensitivity, and consider how well modern chemical kinetics mechanisms are constrained at these conditions by the direct ignition delay measurements. We further assess the proposed role of vibrational non-equilibrium and demonstrate that the current evidence is inconclusive. Finally, we summarize key open questions.

</details>


### [98] [Fifth-order KdV solitary waves in a nonlinear electrical transmission line](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31074)
*Loic Fache, Filip Novkoski, Eric Falcon*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper experimentally observes solitary waves governed by a fifth-order dispersion Korteweg–de Vries equation in a nonlinear electrical transmission line engineered to suppress cubic dispersion, and characterizes their dispersion, oscillatory tails, scaling relations, collisions, and dissipation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to achieve controlled experimental observation of solitary waves governed by the fifth-order dispersion KdV equation, specifically by eliminating cubic dispersion in a nonlinear electrical transmission line.

Method: A nonlinear electrical transmission line is constructed with mutual inductive coupling between nearest-neighbor inductors to achieve vanishing cubic dispersion. The resulting solitary waves are characterized through direct spatiotemporal measurements of the dispersion relation, oscillatory tail profiles, amplitude-width scaling, and amplitude-velocity relation, together with collision experiments between co- or counter-propagating solitary waves and quantification of dissipation effects.

Result: The experiments observe fifth-order KdV solitary waves and find good agreement with semi-analytical predictions for the measured dispersion relation, oscillatory tails, amplitude-width scaling, and amplitude-velocity relation. Collisions between two solitary waves show that their self-similar profile persists after interaction, up to a time shift, and dissipation effects are quantified.

Conclusion: The nonlinear electrical transmission line provides an experimental realization of fifth-order KdV solitary waves, validating semi-analytical predictions and showing that their self-similar profiles remain robust through collisions, with dissipation effects characterized.

Abstract: We report the controlled experimental observation of solitary waves governed by the fifth-order dispersion Korteweg-de Vries (KdV) equation. To achieve vanishing cubic dispersion, we use a mutual inductive coupling between nearest-neighbor inductors within a nonlinear electrical transmission line. We then experimentally characterize the resulting solitary waves through direct spatiotemporal measurements of the dispersion relation, its oscillatory tail profiles, amplitude-width scaling, and amplitude-velocity relation, finding good agreement with semi-analytical predictions. Collision experiments between two (co- or counter-propagating) solitary waves also show that their self-similar profile persists after interaction, up to a time shift. Dissipation effects are also quantified.

</details>


### [99] [A Fitch-Based Wind Farm Parameterization for CM1: Implementation, Validation, and Impacts on Convection Initiation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30806)
*Jianxiang Li, Zhiyong Meng*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: A Fitch-based wind farm parameterization was implemented in CM1, validated against WRF, and used to show that large turbines can initiate convection tens of kilometers downstream through wake-generated warming, secondary circulations, and a gravity wave.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The abstract does not explicitly state the broader motivation; its stated aim is to add a Fitch-based wind farm parameterization to Cloud Model 1 (CM1), verify its consistency with WRF, and assess wind-farm impacts on boundary layer response and convection initiation.

Method: The authors implemented a Fitch-based wind farm parameterization in CM1, representing turbines as elevated momentum sinks and turbulent kinetic energy (TKE) sources within the rotor-swept layer, with computational-efficiency and parallel-execution optimizations. They compared results directly with WRF, ran diurnal simulations across atmospheric stability regimes, and performed idealized simulations favorable for convection to diagnose downstream convection initiation and its mechanisms.

Result: The CM1 implementation closely agreed with WRF in wind-speed deficit magnitude and spatial structure and in TKE enhancement. Diurnal simulations captured the known stability dependence of boundary layer responses. Under stable conditions, coherent near-surface wind-speed and temperature patterns extended well beyond the farm. In convection-favorable idealized simulations, large turbines promoted convection initiation tens of kilometers downstream and earlier than without a farm. Turbulent entrainment of warmer air across the boundary layer top dominated an elongated downstream warm band, with a smaller contribution from enhanced surface sensible heat flux; secondary circulations across the warm band provided sustained low-level lifting, while a wind-farm-generated gravity wave supplied the initial vertical perturbation that intensified downstream and developed into a localized deep convective updraft.

Conclusion: The Fitch-based wind farm parameterization in CM1 is consistent with WRF and captures stability-dependent boundary layer responses. The simulations indicate that wind farms can influence convection initiation through turbine-induced warming, secondary circulations, and gravity-wave perturbations, producing earlier downstream deep convection under favorable conditions.

Abstract: We implement a Fitch-based wind farm parameterization in Cloud Model 1 (CM1), representing turbines as elevated momentum sinks and turbulent kinetic energy (TKE) sources within the rotor-swept layer, with optimizations for computational efficiency and parallel execution. Direct comparison with the Weather Research and Forecasting (WRF) model shows close agreement in the magnitude and spatial structure of wind-speed deficits and TKE enhancement. Diurnal simulations also capture the known dependence of boundary layer responses on atmospheric stability, further supporting the consistency of the implementation. Under stable conditions, the wind farm produces coherent spatial patterns in near-surface wind speed and temperature that extend well beyond the farm. In idealized simulations under conditions favorable for convection, large turbines promote convection initiation tens of kilometers downstream, earlier than in the no-farm simulation. Analysis identifies turbulent entrainment of warmer air across the boundary layer top as the dominant source of an elongated downstream warm band, with a smaller contribution from enhanced surface sensible heat flux. Secondary circulations develop across this warm band and provide sustained low-level lifting. A gravity wave generated by the wind farm supplies the initial vertical perturbation, which intensifies as it is advected downstream within an ascending branch of these circulations and develops into a localized deep convective updraft.

</details>


### [100] [An Annular Spiral Construction for Anomalous Dissipation in the Forced Navier-Stokes Equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29458)
*Changhong Li*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper constructs smooth solutions of the forced three-dimensional Navier-Stokes equations using an annular spiral flow and shows anomalous dissipation with a uniform positive lower bound as viscosity tends to zero.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Kolmogorov's K41 theory predicts that turbulent kinetic energy dissipation can remain positive as viscosity vanishes. Bruè and De Lellis constructed such anomalous dissipation using quasi-self-similar mixing; this paper investigates whether anomalous dissipation can occur for a flow with spiral geometry in an annulus.

Method: A radial segment in the horizontal plane is wound into a two-turn spiral and subjected to alternating shears so that, after each pair of shears, its image shares a positive-length subarc with the original spiral. Vertical velocity is compared with sine profiles whose angular period halves after each pair. After the horizontal flow stops, diffusion damps the final profile. The planar construction is embedded in a (2+1/2)-dimensional framework to obtain smooth three-dimensional solutions with the same smooth initial datum.

Result: For a sequence of viscosities tending to zero, the constructed solutions have dissipation over a fixed subannulus and one vertical period with a uniform positive lower bound. Their forces converge in C^α uniformly in time for every 0<α<1.

Conclusion: The annular spiral construction yields solutions of the forced three-dimensional Navier-Stokes equations that exhibit anomalous dissipation.

Abstract: Kolmogorov's 1941 theory of turbulence (K41) predicts that the rate of kinetic energy dissipation in a turbulent flow can remain positive as viscosity tends to zero. In 2023, Bruè and De Lellis used quasi-self-similar mixing to construct solutions of the forced three-dimensional Navier-Stokes equations exhibiting anomalous dissipation. In this paper, we show that anomalous dissipation can still occur for a flow with spiral geometry in an annulus. In the horizontal plane, we wind a radial segment into a two-turn spiral and then apply alternating shears. After each pair of shears, the image of the segment shares a subarc of positive length with the original spiral. We compare the vertical velocity with sine profiles whose angular period halves with each pair. After the horizontal flow stops, diffusion damps the final profile. By embedding this planar construction into the $(2+\frac{1}{2})$-dimensional framework, we obtain smooth solutions of the three-dimensional equations with the same smooth initial datum. For a sequence of viscosities tending to zero, their dissipation over a fixed subannulus and one vertical period has a uniform positive lower bound, and their forces converge in $C^α$ uniformly in time for every $0<α<1$. We use this spiral construction to produce solutions of the forced three-dimensional Navier-Stokes equations that exhibit anomalous dissipation.

</details>


### [101] [A scalar arterial input function does not uniquely determine the perfusion residue: Theory and phantom MRI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28378)
*Yuyang Gan, Dominick Romano, Maneesh John, Renjiu Hu, Qihao Zhang, Benjamin Weppner, Alexey V. Dimov, Pascal Spincemaille, Yi Wang*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: A cross-sectionally averaged arterial input function (AIF) is shown, theoretically and in a straight-tube MRI phantom, not to uniquely determine the perfusion residue function because inlet tracer concentration and velocity distributions affect tissue curves.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To relate tracer-kinetic residue functions to blood velocity and cross-sectional tracer distributions at the arterial inlet, and to test whether a scalar cross-sectionally averaged AIF uniquely determines the residue and whether residue-AIF models are consistent with fluid mechanics.

Method: The residue function referenced to the averaged inlet AIF was analytically derived for steady laminar flow without radial diffusion. In a straight tube with parabolic flow, two nonnegative inlet profiles sharing one measured AIF were compared for gamma-variate and rectangular arterial spin labeling inputs. A straight-tube phantom was imaged at 3 T during gadolinium bolus passage, with uniform fills as controls.

Result: Phantom enhancement during bolus arrival was greater near the lumen center than near the wall at all 11 stations and for all three boluses, while uniform gadolinium fills showed no such pattern. The two inlet profiles produced tissue curves differing by up to 23.3% of the uniform-inlet peak and 0.30 s in time to peak for the gamma-variate input, and by 33.3% for ASL labels shorter than the fastest transit time. Their residues differed in initial amplitude and normalized temporal shape.

Conclusion: Identical AIFs can produce different tissue concentration curves depending on the spatial distributions of inlet tracer concentration and arterial flow velocity. Consequently, residue-AIF tracer kinetic models may not be consistent with fluid mechanics or interpretable as tissue flow.

Abstract: Purpose: To relate the residue function of tracer kinetic models to blood velocity and cross-sectional tracer distributions at the arterial inlet, and show that a cross-sectionally averaged arterial input function (AIF) does not uniquely determine the residue.
  Theory and Methods: For steady laminar flow without radial diffusion, the residue function referenced to the cross-sectionally averaged inlet AIF was analytically derived from the velocity and inlet tracer distributions. In a straight tube with parabolic flow, two nonnegative inlet profiles sharing one measured AIF were compared for gamma-variate and rectangular arterial spin labeling (ASL) inputs. A straight-tube phantom was imaged at 3 T during gadolinium bolus passage, with uniform fills as controls.
  Results: In the phantom, enhancement during bolus arrival was greater near the lumen center than near the wall at all 11 stations and for all three boluses, whereas uniform gadolinium fills showed no such pattern. In the tube model, the two profiles produced tissue curves differing by up to 23.3% of the uniform-inlet peak and 0.30 s in time to peak for the gamma-variate input, and by 33.3% for ASL labels shorter than the fastest transit time. Their residues differed in initial amplitude and normalized temporal shape.
  Conclusion: Identical AIFs can produce different tissue concentration curves, depending on the spatial distributions of inlet tracer concentration and arterial flow velocity. Consequently, the residue-AIF tracer kinetic models may not be consistent with fluid mechanics or interpreted as tissue flow.

</details>


### [102] [Failure Modes and Data-Efficient Remedies for Transformer-Based Prediction of Viscoplastic Flows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28267)
*Maedeh Saberi, Hugo Franca, Maziyar Jalaal, Safa Jamali*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: This paper systematically analyzes failure modes of the RheOFormer transformer neural operator for steady Bingham channel flow and shows that logarithmic velocity inputs, gradient-aware losses, and minimal strategic data augmentation improve accuracy and data efficiency for viscoplastic flow prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transformer-based neural operators such as RheOFormer have shown promise for non-Newtonian flow surrogates, but their applicability to viscoplastic flows and their ability to recover realistic flows remain poorly understood because of yield surfaces, discontinuous gradients, and strongly nonlinear yielded-unyielded transitions.

Method: The authors conduct the first systematic investigation of RheOFormer on steady Bingham channel flow, isolating a simple geometry and constitutive model to examine how dataset structure, numerical value range, and input representation affect interpolation and extrapolation. After identifying failure regimes, they introduce targeted input-representation and loss-formulation enhancements, including logarithmic velocity representations and gradient-aware losses, and augment training with a few strategically selected cases spanning missing physical regimes. They also test whether a single partially developed snapshot can support steady-state prediction.

Result: The study identifies data regimes where RheOFormer remains accurate and where errors emerge, particularly for plug-dominated flows and conditions outside the velocity scales or flow structures represented in training. Logarithmic velocity representations and gradient-aware losses substantially improve accuracy in challenging low-gradient plug regions where the baseline fails, and extrapolation errors are reduced by adding only a few strategically selected training cases. A single partially developed snapshot suffices for accurate steady-state prediction, and the model outperforms simply copying its input throughout flow development.

Conclusion: Appropriate field representation and parameter-space coverage are important for data-efficient neural-operator prediction of viscoplastic flows; targeted representation, loss, and dataset-design remedies can address key failure modes of transformer-based surrogates.

Abstract: Fast and accurate prediction of viscoplastic flows remains a challenge in computational rheology due to yield surfaces, discontinuous gradients, and strongly nonlinear transitions between yielded and unyielded regions. Transformer-based neural operators such as RheOFormer have recently shown strong potential for surrogate modeling of non-Newtonian flows. However, their applicability to viscoplastic systems and their efficacy in recovering realistic flows remain poorly understood. In this work, we present the first systematic investigation of RheOFormer applied to steady Bingham channel flow. By isolating the problem to a simple geometry and constitutive model, we examine how dataset structure, numerical value range, and input representation govern the model's ability to interpolate and extrapolate within the parameter space. The data regimes in which RheOFormer remains accurate and those in which errors emerge are identified, particularly for plug-dominated flows and for conditions outside the velocity scales or flow structures represented during training. Upon identifying these failure modes, targeted enhancements to the input representation and loss formulation are introduced to better predict low-gradient plug regions. Logarithmic velocity representations and gradient-aware loss formulations substantially improve accuracy in challenging regimes where the baseline formulation fails. In addition, extrapolation errors are reduced by augmenting the training set with only a few strategically selected cases spanning the missing physical regimes. Finally, a single partially developed snapshot suffices for an accurate steady-state prediction, and the model outperforms simply copying its input throughout flow development. These results highlight the importance of appropriate field representation and parameter-space coverage for data-efficient neural-operator prediction of viscoplastic flows.

</details>


### [103] [Shallow may be better: Physics-Constrained Lightweight Networks for High-Throughput Particle Image Velocimetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28088)
*Claudio Mucignat, Kamila Zdybal, Ivan Lunati*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper argues that PIV networks need not inherit deep general-purpose optical-flow encoders because tracer displacement is physically bounded. It derives an explicit upper bound on required spatial support, proposes the 589,000-parameter LIMA3 pyramidal estimator, and shows accuracy comparable to established learned and classical methods with much lower memory and higher throughput, including embedded and megapixel operation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Convolutional optical-flow networks commonly use progressively deeper pyramidal encoders on the assumption that larger receptive fields improve motion estimation. For particle image velocimetry, however, inter-frame tracer-particle displacement is bounded by acquisition geometry, so network depth can be derived from the physically admissible displacement rather than adopted from general-purpose architectures.

Method: The authors perform a layer-by-layer analysis of spatial-support growth, derive an explicit upper bound for PIV, and show that deeper architectures exceed the required support. Based on this analysis, they develop LIMA3, a lightweight pyramidal displacement estimator with 589,000 trainable parameters.

Result: Across synthetic and experimental benchmarks, LIMA3 matches the accuracy of established learned and classical methods with at least an order of magnitude less memory and substantially higher throughput. It retains the physically expected coarsening of spatial resolution as tracer information is lost. Linear memory scaling enables megapixel processing within embedded memory budgets, reaching over 900 image pairs per second at 640x480 pixels on an RTX 5090 and 99 image pairs per second on an NVIDIA Jetson AGX Orin.

Conclusion: The spatial-support analysis provides a transferable design principle for compact optical-flow architectures and bounded-window attention. It offers an alternative to compressing oversized general-purpose networks and enables efficient operation alongside image acquisition.

Abstract: Convolutional networks for optical flow typically adopt progressively deeper pyramidal encoders under the assumption that larger receptive fields improve motion estimation. For particle image velocimetry (PIV), however, tracer-particle displacement between frames is bounded by the acquisition geometry, allowing network depth to be derived from the physically admissible displacement rather than inherited from general-purpose architectures. Using a layer-by-layer analysis of spatial-support growth, we derive its explicit upper bound and show that deeper architectures substantially exceed the support required for PIV, compromising training stability, spatial resolution under particle loss, and inference efficiency. Based on this analysis, we develop LIMA3, a lightweight pyramidal displacement estimator with 589,000 trainable parameters. Across synthetic and experimental benchmarks, LIMA3 matches the accuracy of established learned and classical methods with at least an order of magnitude less memory and substantially higher throughput, while retaining the physically expected coarsening of spatial resolution as tracer information is lost. Its linear memory scaling enables megapixel processing within embedded memory budgets, such that LIMA3 can reach over 900 image pairs per second at $640\times480$~pixel on an RTX 5090 and 99 image pairs per second on an NVIDIA Jetson AGX Orin. The spatial-support analysis provides a transferable design principle for compact optical-flow architectures and bounded-window attention, offering an alternative to compressing oversized general-purpose networks and enabling efficient operation alongside image acquisition.

</details>



<div id="physics.atom-ph"></div>

# physics.atom-ph [[Back]](#toc)


### [104] [QED theory of scalar hyperfine interactions in HD$^+$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30481)
*Tymon Kilich, Mateusz Pańtak, Krzysztof Pachucki*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The paper computes the dominant electron–proton and electron–deuteron spin–spin hyperfine couplings in HD+ with relativistic and leading radiative QED corrections, achieving substantially improved accuracy and comparing the results with precision measurements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: HD+ supports determinations of proton-to-electron and deuteron-to-electron mass ratios, tests of quantum electrodynamics, and searches for new interactions, all of which require accurate hyperfine structure. Previous theoretical predictions disagreed with several precise measurements.

Method: The two dominant hyperfine interactions—couplings of the electron spin to the proton and deuteron spins—are calculated including complete nonrecoil electronic relativistic and leading radiative corrections of relative orders α^2 and α^3, respectively, within the Born-Oppenheimer approximation. Nuclear-structure effects are suppressed by using measured hyperfine splittings of atomic hydrogen and deuterium.

Result: Couplings are obtained for 40 rovibrational states and are about 18 times more accurate than previous values. They agree with a recent Penning-trap measurement in the ground state within a combined uncertainty of 65 Hz, or 7 parts in 10^8, and with a recent measurement in a rotationally excited state. A discrepancy of 7.4(9) kHz persists for a vibrational overtone transition.

Conclusion: The improved hyperfine couplings provide better theoretical input for future determinations of the proton and deuteron masses from HD+ spectroscopy.

Abstract: HD$^+$ is the simplest heteronuclear molecular ion. It has been used to determine the proton-to-electron and deuteron-to-electron mass ratios, to test quantum electrodynamics, and to search for new interactions. All these applications rely on an accurate knowledge of its hyperfine structure, for which theoretical predictions have disagreed with several precise measurements. Here we calculate the two dominant hyperfine interactions, namely the couplings of the electron spin to the proton and deuteron spins, including the complete nonrecoil electronic relativistic and leading radiative corrections of relative orders $α^2$ and $α^3$, respectively, evaluated in the Born-Oppenheimer approximation. Nuclear-structure effects are suppressed by using the measured hyperfine splittings of atomic hydrogen and deuterium. The resulting couplings for 40 rovibrational states are about 18 times more accurate than the previous ones. They agree with the recent Penning-trap measurement in the ground state within the combined uncertainty of 65 Hz, i.e., 7 parts in $10^8$, and with a recent measurement in a rotationally excited state, whereas a discrepancy of $7.4(9)$ kHz persists for a vibrational overtone transition. Our results provide improved theoretical input for future determinations of the proton and deuteron masses from HD$^+$ spectroscopy.

</details>


### [105] [Confidence-Aware Discriminative Readout of Neutral-Atom Arrays](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30421)
*Jaeick Bae, Kangheun Kim, Yunheung Song, Junseo Ha, Jeong Ho Han, Jaewook Ahn, Hyunsoo Ha, Soonwon Choi, Jongchul Mun*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates confidence-aware readout of 171Yb atoms in optical-tweezer arrays using a logistic-regression classifier trained on photon-count and spatial fluorescence features. It achieves 99.83% readout fidelity at 10 ms, comparable to 99.82% with conventional thresholding at 20 ms, and converts low-confidence outcomes into erasures to reduce residual errors and false positives, with Monte Carlo simulations predicting improved defect-free rearrangement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fluorescence imaging is a limiting step for rapid experimental cycling, large-scale neutral-atom array operation, and quantum-error-correction protocols. Conventional hard binary site outcomes discard measurement reliability information, and false positives can create defects in downstream operations such as atom rearrangement.

Method: The authors use a discriminative logistic-regression classifier that outputs a measurement reliability score from features based on both the total photon count and the spatial pattern of fluorescence at each atom site. Low-confidence outcomes can be converted into measurement-level erasures. Performance is assessed through readout fidelity, imaging time, false-positive suppression, and Monte Carlo rearrangement simulations.

Result: The classifier achieves the same readout fidelity with approximately half the imaging time: 99.83% fidelity at 10 ms versus 99.82% for conventional photon-count thresholding at 20 ms. Confidence-based erasure conversion yields fewer residual unflagged errors at a fixed erasure budget, strong false-positive suppression, and improved predicted defect-free assembly in Monte Carlo rearrangement simulations.

Conclusion: Confidence-aware readout using a discriminative classifier is presented as a practical paradigm for fast and scalable neutral-atom quantum computing.

Abstract: Fast and reliable detection is a central requirement for neutral atom quantum computers, yet fluorescence imaging remains a limiting step for rapid experimental cycling, large-scale array operation, and quantum-error-correction protocols. In this work, we demonstrate confidence-aware readout of $^{171}\mathrm{Yb}$ atoms in optical tweezers using a discriminative classifier. Rather than forcing each atom-site image directly into a hard binary outcome, the proposed readout produces a measurement reliability score. This score is produced by a logistic-regression classifier trained on features derived from both the total photon count and the spatial pattern of fluorescence at each site. The resulting classifier achieves the same readout fidelity with approximately half the imaging time. At an exposure of 10 ms, it achieves $99.83\%$ readout fidelity, comparable to the $99.82\%$ achieved by conventional photon-count thresholding at 20 ms. Importantly, the classifier score enables low-confidence outcomes to be converted into measurement-level erasures, leaving fewer residual unflagged errors at a fixed erasure budget. This improvement is further accompanied by strong false-positive suppression, which is important for downstream operations such as atom rearrangement where false positives can create target defects. Monte Carlo rearrangement simulations show that the reduced false-positive rate and confidence-based erasure conversion improve predicted defect-free assembly performance. This work establishes confidence-aware readout using a discriminative classifier as a practical paradigm for fast and scalable neutral-atom quantum computing.

</details>


### [106] [Low Vibration Closed-Cycle Flow Cryostat for Ion-Cavity Experiments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29785)
*Andrew Laugharn*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: A custom 4 K closed-cycle helium-flow cryostat integrates a Paul trap and Fabry-Pérot cavity, achieves low vibration and UHV-compatible ion storage, and demonstrates preliminary ion-cavity mode interaction for trapped-ion quantum interconnect applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cryogenic operation below 10 K can suppress trap-electrode and dielectric heating of trapped ions and cryopump residual gases to ultra-high vacuum without bakeout. These benefits are important for cavity-integrated ion traps, where nearby dielectric surfaces can increase motional heating and rapid design-build-test iterations benefit from eliminating bakeout cycles. The work aims to study ion-trap optical cavity structures intended as a photonic interconnect between remote, compute-capable trapped 171Yb+ ion quantum processors for long-baseline entanglement distribution beyond 10 km.

Method: The authors developed a custom 4 K cryostat cooled by a ColdEdge Technologies Stinger closed-cycle 4He flow cryogenic system. They integrated an Alpine Quantum Technologies Paul trap and a Fabry-Pérot optical cavity at the 4 K stage. Cooling capacities of the 40 K and 4 K stages were measured, vibrations of the 4 K stage relative to the optical table were measured with a Michelson interferometer, and UHV was inferred from hydrogen-Yb+ ion collision rates. They also assessed ion storage, positional reproducibility after thermal cycling, system uptime, in-vacuum access, and spatial overlap and interaction between a trapped 174Yb+ ion and the cavity mode on the 1345 nm transition.

Result: The cryostat cooling capacities were 5 W at 45 K and 0.5 W at 6.1 K. Vibrations of the 4 K stage relative to the optical table were less than 12 nm RMS in three dimensions. UHV was inferred from hydrogen-Yb+ collision studies. Ion storage times exceeded 5 days without laser cooling, and trapped-ion positions were reproducible within approximately 10 μm after repeated thermal cycles to room temperature. The system uptime was 72 days and provided convenient access to the 4 K stage for in-vacuum maintenance and modifications. Spatial overlap and interaction between a trapped 174Yb+ ion and the optical cavity mode on the 1345 nm transition were demonstrated, providing preliminary experimental validation of the integrated cryogenic ion-cavity structure.

Conclusion: The presented platform supports cryogenic cavity-integrated trapped-ion experiments with low vibration, UHV-compatible operation, long ion storage, and practical in-vacuum access. It provides preliminary experimental validation of an integrated cryogenic ion-cavity structure intended for photonic interconnects between remote trapped-ion quantum processors. Long-baseline entanglement distribution itself is a stated goal rather than a demonstrated result in the abstract.

Abstract: Cryogenic operation below 10 K suppresses trap-electrode and dielectric heating of trapped ions while cryopumping residual gases to ultra-high vacuum (UHV) without requiring a bakeout. These advantages are particularly important for cavity-integrated ion traps, where nearby dielectric surfaces can increase motional heating and rapid design-build-test iterations benefit from eliminating bakeout cycles. We have developed a custom 4 K cryostat cooled by a ColdEdge Technologies (CET) Stinger closed-cycle $^{4}\text{He}$ flow cryogenic system to study various ion trap optical cavity structures designed to serve as a photonic interconnect between remote, compute-capable trapped $^{171}\text{Yb}^{+}$ ion quantum processors, enabling long baseline entanglement distribution $(>10\text{ km})$. We integrate an Alpine Quantum Technologies (AQT) Paul trap and Fabry-Pérot optical cavity at the 4 K stage. The cooling capacities of the cryostat 40 K and 4 K stages were measured to be 5 W at 45 K and 0.5 W at 6.1 K. Vibrations of the 4 K stage relative to the optical table are less than $12\text{ nm}_{\text{RMS}}$ in three dimensions, as measured using a Michelson interferometer. An ultra-high vacuum (UHV) is inferred by studying the rate of hydrogen-$\mathrm{Yb}^{+}$ ion collisions. Ion storage times exceeding 5 days without laser cooling have been reported and the position of the trapped ions are reproducible within $\sim10\text{ }μ\text{m}$ following repeated thermal cycles to room temperature. The uptime of this system is 72 days and offers convenient access to the 4 K stage for in-vacuum maintenance and system modifications. Using this platform, we demonstrate spatial overlap and interaction between a trapped $^{174}\text{Yb}^{+}$ ion and the optical cavity mode on the 1345 nm transition, establishing preliminary experimental validation of the integrated cryogenic ion-cavity structure.

</details>


### [107] [Exponential advantage in quantum observation with one extra qubit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30876)
*Zhenyu Du, Xiongfeng Ma, Vlatko Vedral, Jinzhao Sun*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: Adding one ancilla qubit to a quantum machine enables an exponential reduction in the number of evolutions needed to estimate many multi-time correlators of quantum many-body dynamics, with proven spatial and temporal optimality and numerical validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum technologies promise to transform physical observation, but achieving large advantages with minimal hardware overhead remains a central goal; the paper pursues such an advantage for probing quantum many-body dynamics.

Method: The authors propose an algorithm that augments a quantum machine with one extra qubit and uses it to coherently interfere dynamical trajectories, allowing simultaneous estimation of exponentially many multi-time correlators with exponentially fewer evolutions than ancilla-free schemes. They analyze the advantage under unrestricted coherence time and arbitrary adaptive control, prove optimality in space and time, and numerically demonstrate prediction of higher-order probe signals in a many-body system.

Result: The protocol provides an exponential advantage in probing quantum many-body dynamics, estimating exponentially many multi-time correlators with exponentially fewer evolutions, even when the ancilla-free machine has unrestricted coherence time and arbitrary adaptive control and even for a single correlator. Extending coherence time is proven to exponentially enhance observational power, establishing optimality in space and time. Numerical demonstrations predict higher-order probe signals with orders-of-magnitude fewer experimental runs than previous methods. Specific platforms and quantitative error metrics are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: A single extra qubit can deliver an exponential and provably optimal observational advantage for quantum many-body dynamics, indicating broad utility across quantum learning, sensing, and simulation.

Abstract: While quantum technologies promise to revolutionise how we observe the physical world, achieving substantial advantages with minimal overhead remains a central pursuit. Here, we show that augmenting a quantum machine with just one extra qubit yields an exponential advantage in probing quantum many-body dynamics. We propose an algorithm that uses this qubit to coherently interfere dynamical trajectories, simultaneously estimating exponentially many multi-time correlators with exponentially fewer evolutions than any ancilla-free scheme. Remarkably, this advantage holds even when the ancilla-free machine has unrestricted coherence time and arbitrary adaptive control, and persists even for a single correlator. We further prove that extending coherence time exponentially enhances observational power, establishing our protocol's optimality in both space and time. Finally, we numerically demonstrate our approach by predicting higher-order probe signals in a many-body system with orders-of-magnitude fewer experimental runs than previous methods. These results highlight the broad utility of our framework across quantum learning, sensing, and simulation.

</details>


### [108] [Computational Complexity and Energy Landscapes of Coulomb Crystals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30657)
*Paco Navarro, Michael Emmerich*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The paper analyzes computational complexity and energy landscapes of trapped-ion Coulomb crystals and Thomson-sphere charge configurations, reporting advice-based complexity bounds, approximately exponential growth in simulated-annealing search metrics, and NP-hardness for site-selection variants under bare Coulomb repulsion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Simulated annealing is widely used to search for ground states of trapped-ion Coulomb crystals and charges on a sphere, so the paper aims to clarify the computational difficulty of these problems and the role of input encoding and energy-landscape structure.

Method: It derives nonuniform complexity bounds for decision and approximation problems, performs simulated-annealing simulations for up to 100 ions, extends an inverse-square reduction to the Coulomb interaction by deriving its angular law including the quartic field term and sharpening two estimates, constructs quadrupole arrays whose common monopole term cancels from energy differences between legal configurations, and uses barrier trees plus finite-precision laboratory-parameter tests.

Result: With unary ion count and a binary energy threshold, the fixed-trap decision problem is in P/poly, and NP-hardness would collapse the polynomial hierarchy; the same holds on the unit sphere. With unary ion count and logarithmic precision, approximate configuration search on the sphere and in a fixed positive-definite harmonic trap admits a solution function in FP/poly. In simulations, the number of distinct minima and time to the best-known minimum grow approximately exponentially. Choosing occupied prescribed sites to minimize Coulomb energy is NP-hard in two and three dimensions, with a finite-precision promise version NP-complete. The quadrupole construction exhibits large barriers between nearly degenerate minima, trapping simulated annealing above the ground state in the cube example, while the encoding survives numerically on a half-filled square lattice at laboratory parameters with individually tunable well depths.

Conclusion: The advice bounds provide no uniform search algorithm. For identical ions with bare Coulomb repulsion, the hardness is carried by site positions alone. Barrier structure explains why simulated annealing can become trapped, and laboratory-scale implementation may require stiff wells a few to tens of micrometers apart, as in electrode trap arrays for Doppler-cooled strontium ions.

Abstract: Simulated annealing is widely used to search for ground states of trapped-ion Coulomb crystals and of charges on a sphere (the Thomson problem). Elementary nonuniform bounds clarify the role of the input encoding. For identical ions in a fixed trap, unary ion count and a binary energy threshold define a decision problem in P/poly; NP-hardness would imply a collapse of the polynomial hierarchy. The same holds on the unit sphere. With unary ion count and logarithmic precision parameter, approximate configuration search on the sphere and in a fixed positive-definite harmonic trap admits a solution function in FP/poly. These advice bounds provide no uniform search algorithm. In simulations of up to 100 ions, the number of distinct minima found and the time to reach the best-known minimum both grow approximately exponentially. When ions are pinned to prescribed candidate sites, choosing which sites to occupy to minimize the Coulomb energy is NP-hard in two and three dimensions; a finite-precision promise version is NP-complete. All ions are identical, the interaction is bare Coulomb repulsion, and the hardness is carried by the site positions alone. We extend a previous inverse-square reduction to the Coulomb interaction by deriving its angular law, including the quartic field term, and sharpening two estimates. The construction is an array of quadrupoles whose common monopole term cancels from energy differences between legal configurations. Its energy landscapes exhibit large barriers between nearly degenerate minima. Barrier trees resolve this structure, and simulated annealing becomes trapped above the ground state in the cube example. At laboratory parameters, with individually tunable well depths, the encoding survives numerically on a half-filled square lattice. For Doppler-cooled strontium ions, this requires stiff wells a few to tens of micrometers apart, as in electrode trap arrays.

</details>


### [109] [Where does a molecule begin? Threshold masses of three-particle Coulomb systems and a predictive theory of their Born-Oppenheimer offsets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28300)
*Roberto Peverati*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The paper tests the limits of the Born-Oppenheimer approximation in three-particle Coulomb systems {X+,X+,e-} as the nuclear-to-electron mass ratio M varies. Using variational calculations and the exact BO approximation, it computes threshold masses for 23 excited levels below M=100 and develops a parameter-free theory that predicts nearly constant BO offsets. The results locate the onset of molecular structure: the ground state changes gradually over a 2.3-decade crossover, while the first rotational and vibrational levels bind at M=4.208 and M=9.526, respectively, versus BO predictions of 5.54 and 11.08.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Born-Oppenheimer approximation, which treats nuclei as infinitely heavy, is foundational to molecular structure and chemistry. The authors aim to test its boundaries by varying nuclear masses and examining how molecular structure emerges in three-particle Coulomb systems.

Method: The authors study the three-particle family {X+,X+,e-} as a function of M=m_X/m_e using deterministic variational calculations and the exact Born-Oppenheimer approximation. They compute threshold masses of all 23 excited levels bound below M=100 with certified upper bounds and develop a parameter-free theory to explain the BO offsets, testing predictions frozen before exact calculations.

Result: The BO approximation places all 23 excited-level thresholds too late by a nearly constant amount, 1.2 to 1.6 in M (0.23 to 0.54 with the adiabatic correction). The parameter-free theory passes 18 of 18 symmetric-level predictions with typical miss 0.003; for unequal nuclear masses none of 7 levels falls within the band, though the theory captures over 90% of the offset. The ground state changes character gradually over a crossover 2.3 decades wide in M; the first rotational level binds at M=4.208 in the ortho-type nuclear-spin species and the first vibrational level at M=9.526, while BO places them at 5.54 and 11.08.

Conclusion: The BO picture orders the spectrum correctly at finite mass but shifts it by an amount that can be computed. Molecular structure emerges gradually with increasing nuclear mass, and threshold masses provide sharp landmarks for when rotational and vibrational molecular levels begin.

Abstract: The Born-Oppenheimer (BO) approximation serves as the foundational framework for the contemporary concept of molecular structure, which is central to chemistry and the molecular sciences. Its principal assumption is the treatment of nuclei as infinitely heavy particles. In this work, we test the boundaries of the BO approximation by examining systems with variable nuclear masses to investigate the emergence of molecular structure. We examine the three-particle family {X^+,X^+,e^-} as a function of the mass ratio M = m_X/m_e, using deterministic variational calculations and the exact BO approximation. We accurately compute the threshold masses of all 23 excited levels bound below M = 100 with certified upper bounds. The BO approximation places every one of them too late by a nearly constant amount, 1.2 to 1.6 (0.23 to 0.54 with the adiabatic correction). We explain this constancy with a parameter-free theory, and test it with predictions frozen before the exact calculations: 18 of 18 symmetric levels pass (typical miss 0.003). For unequal nuclear masses none of 7 levels falls within the band, but the theory still captures over 90% of the offset. Where does a molecule begin? The ground state changes character gradually, over a crossover 2.3 decades wide in M. The sharp landmarks are the threshold masses: the first rotational level binds at M = 4.208, in the ortho-type nuclear-spin species, and the first vibrational level at M = 9.526; BO places them at 5.54 and 11.08. Thus, the BO picture orders the spectrum correctly at finite mass and shifts it by an amount that can be computed.

</details>

# physics.comp-ph [[Back]](#toc)


### [110] [Digital Models of Widely-Used Powder Characterisation Tools and their Application in DEM Calibration Part III: Packing and Flow Testers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29117)
*B. D. Jenkins, A. L. Nicusan, D. Werner, J. P. K. Seville, A. Neveu, G. Lumay, F. Francqui, C. R. K. Windows-Yule*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops and validates open-source digital models of the GranuFlow orifice flow tester and GranuPack tapped density analyser for discrete element method (DEM) calibration, with a case study on micro-crystalline cellulose and ion exchange resin showing strong cross-validated agreement with experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate calibration remains a critical challenge for DEM simulations, and the field needs more standardised and rigorous methods. The work contributes to a growing open-source library of digital models that faithfully replicate powder characterisation instruments.

Method: The authors develop digital models of two widely used powder characterisation instruments, the GranuFlow flow-through-orifice tester and the GranuPack tapped density analyser, designed to replicate the experimental instruments while including computational optimisations for efficiency without compromising accuracy. They demonstrate application through a calibration case study for micro-crystalline cellulose (MCC) and ion exchange resin, using cross-validation against experimental data. Specific optimisation techniques are not detailed in the abstract.

Result: The calibrated models show strong agreement with experimental data through cross-validation, with a relative percentage error of 0.57% for the Hausner ratio for the ion exchange resin and 4.17% for a Beverloo fitting parameter for MCC.

Conclusion: The validated, open-source models provide the DEM community with additional tools for more robust multi-instrument calibration and potential for further insight into bulk behaviours under different conditions.

Abstract: Accurate calibration remains a critical challenge in the field of discrete element method (DEM) simulations. To work towards more standardised and rigorous methods, this research contributes to a growing open-source library of digital models that faithfully replicate powder characterisation instruments. This paper expands that library by developing and validating digital models for two widely used instruments: the GranuFlow flow through an orifice tester and the GranuPack tapped density analyser. These models are designed to replicate the experimental instruments while incorporating computational optimisations to improve efficiency without compromising on accuracy. An example application of the digital models is shown through a calibration case study for two example materials: micro-crystalline cellulose (MCC) and ion exchange resin. The calibrated models show strong agreement with experimental data through cross-validation, yielding a relative percentage error of 0.57\% for the Hausner ratio (the ion exchange resin) and 4.17\% for a Beverloo fitting parameter (MCC). The addition of these validated, open-source models provides the DEM community with further models for more robust, multi-instrument calibration, and potential for more insight into bulk behaviours under different conditions.

</details>


### [111] [JAX-Nano: A GPU-Accelerated Differentiable Phonon Boltzmann Transport Solver for Nanoscale Heat Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28926)
*Bo Zhang, Amy Zhaomei Zhang*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: JAX-Nano is a GPU-accelerated, end-to-end differentiable finite-volume solver for steady-state and transient phonon Boltzmann transport in nanoscale heat transfer, validated across diffusive-to-ballistic regimes and demonstrated for 3D FinFET geometries, inverse parameter identification, and GPU scalability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As semiconductor device dimensions approach phonon mean free paths, heat transport becomes ballistic or quasi-ballistic and Fourier's law is inadequate. The phonon Boltzmann transport equation captures these nonequilibrium effects but is computationally demanding, motivating a unified, GPU-accelerated, differentiable solver.

Method: The paper introduces JAX-Nano, which integrates steady-state and transient phonon BTE formulations in a unified finite-volume framework supporting structured and unstructured meshes, mode-resolved transport, phonon scattering, volumetric heat generation, and multiple boundary conditions. Automatic differentiation provides gradients with respect to transport properties, boundary conditions, heat-generation rates, and geometric parameters without separately derived adjoint equations. The solver is validated against analytical solutions and established BTE solvers, applied to single-fin, double-fin, and arrayed FinFET configurations, used for gradient-based inverse learning, and benchmarked for GPU performance and scalability, including comparison with an MPI-based BTE solver.

Result: Numerical predictions were validated against analytical solutions and established BTE solvers across diffusive-to-ballistic regimes. Complex three-dimensional FinFET geometries were demonstrated. Gradient-based inverse learning enabled single- and multi-parameter identification, including simultaneous recovery of mode-resolved phonon relaxation times and spatially distributed heat-generation rates in steady-state and transient problems. GPU performance and scalability were evaluated with respect to spatial resolution, angular discretization, and phonon bands, including comparisons with an MPI-based BTE solver.

Conclusion: JAX-Nano provides a unified computational framework for three-dimensional phonon transport, automatic differentiation, and inverse parameter identification, enabling sensitivity analysis and optimization of nanoscale thermal systems.

Abstract: As semiconductor device dimensions approach phonon mean free paths, heat transport enters ballistic and quasi-ballistic regimes where Fourier's law becomes inadequate. The phonon Boltzmann transport equation (BTE) describes these nonequilibrium effects but presents significant computational challenges. This work introduces JAX-Nano, a GPU-accelerated, end-to-end differentiable phonon BTE solver for nanoscale heat transport. The solver integrates steady-state and transient BTE formulations within a unified finite-volume framework, supporting structured and unstructured meshes, mode-resolved transport, phonon scattering, volumetric heat generation, and multiple boundary conditions. Automatic differentiation enables gradient computation with respect to transport properties, boundary conditions, heat-generation rates, and geometric parameters without separately derived adjoint equations. Numerical predictions are validated against analytical solutions and established BTE solvers across diffusive-to-ballistic regimes. Applications to complex three-dimensional geometries are demonstrated using single-fin, double-fin, and arrayed fin field-effect transistor (FinFET) configurations. Gradient-based inverse learning enables single- and multi-parameter identification, including simultaneous recovery of mode-resolved phonon relaxation times and spatially distributed heat-generation rates in steady-state and transient problems. GPU performance and scalability are evaluated with respect to spatial resolution, angular discretization, and phonon bands, including comparisons with an MPI-based BTE solver. JAX-Nano provides a unified computational framework for three-dimensional phonon transport, automatic differentiation, and inverse parameter identification, enabling sensitivity analysis and optimization of nanoscale thermal systems.

</details>


### [112] [Accelerating wave-scattering simulations with learned search directions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28499)
*Haoran Sun, Tao Shan, Hongyu Zhou, Rui Guo, Shenheng Xu, Fan Yang, Maokun Li*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: BEMFormer learns search directions for boundary-element wave-scattering solvers, generalizes across geometries and excitations, and solves a 1.04-million-unknown quadcopter scattering problem 30 times faster than a preconditioned Krylov solver with about 1 dB radar-cross-section deviation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wave-scattering simulation requires solving a dense boundary-element system for every frequency, incidence, and design. Krylov solvers rebuild each search space from scratch, and that space grows with problem size, making large scattering problems expensive.

Method: The paper presents BEMFormer, a solver that learns to generate search directions across systems of one integral equation and supplies geometry-specific directions through a coarse-grid correction. It is evaluated for generalization to unseen geometries, meshes, and excitations, and to acoustic scattering after fine-tuning on eight geometries.

Result: BEMFormer generalizes to unseen geometries, meshes, and excitations, and to acoustic scattering after fine-tuning on eight geometries. It reproduces measured radar cross-sections of benchmark targets.
