On a quadcopter with 1.04 million unknowns, 38 times the largest training problem, it solves each system 30 times faster than a preconditioned Krylov solver; both reach a radar-cross-section deviation of about 1 dB, comparable to the difference between independent measurements and acceptable for engineering applications.

Conclusion: The results indicate that solving million-unknown scattering problems in minutes is possible when the search space is learned rather than built from scratch.

Abstract: Wave-scattering simulation requires solving a dense boundary-element system for every frequency, incidence and design. Krylov solvers build each search space from scratch, and it grows with problem size. Here we present BEMFormer, a solver that learns to generate search directions across systems of one integral equation and supplies geometry-specific directions through a coarse-grid correction. BEMFormer generalizes to unseen geometries, meshes and excitations, and to acoustic scattering after fine-tuning on eight geometries. It reproduces the measured radar cross-sections of benchmark targets. On a quadcopter with 1.04 million unknowns, 38 times the largest training problem, BEMFormer solves each system 30 times faster than a preconditioned Krylov solver. Both reach a radar cross-section deviation of about 1 dB, comparable to the difference between independent measurements and acceptable for engineering applications. These results show that solving million-unknown scattering problems in minutes is possible when the search space is learned rather than built from scratch.

</details>


### [113] [Machine Learning Electronic Hamiltonian with Long-Range Effects and External Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28450)
*Xueheng Kuang, Ruoyu Wang, Kwangrae Kim, Rongjing Guo, Yiyang Fu, Yuanyue Liu*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces MLEH-LR, a machine-learning electronic-Hamiltonian framework that incorporates long-range interactions and external fields, improving Hamiltonian and electronic-structure prediction and enabling large-scale calculations, demonstrated on zigzag boron nitride ribbons under transverse electric fields.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional machine-learning electronic-Hamiltonian models use a local-environment approximation and can miss long-range interactions and external-field effects.

Method: MLEH-LR incorporates long-range effects and external fields into Hamiltonian prediction. It is benchmarked on materials with different polarizations and dimensionalities and applied to zigzag boron nitride ribbons under transverse electric fields.

Result: MLEH-LR substantially improves predicted Hamiltonians and resulting electronic structures, supports accurate large-scale high-throughput calculations, yields a quantitative relation for the joint width and field dependence of the band gap, and reveals electronic mechanisms underlying the field response.

Conclusion: MLEH-LR provides an efficient method and tool for studying electronic structure in materials where long-range electrostatics and external fields are important.

Abstract: Conventional machine-learning electronic Hamiltonian (MLEH) models rely on a local-environment approximation and can miss the effects of long-range (LR) interactions and external fields. Here, we introduce MLEH-LR, a framework that incorporates these effects into Hamiltonian prediction. Benchmarks across materials with different polarizations and dimensionalities demonstrate substantial improvements in the accuracy of the predicted Hamiltonians and resulting electronic structures. MLEH-LR enables accurate, large-scale, high-throughput electronic-structure calculations, as demonstrated for zigzag boron nitride ribbons under transverse electric fields. These calculations allow us to formulate a quantitative relation describing the joint width and field dependence of the band gap and to reveal the electronic mechanisms underlying its field response. Our work provides an efficient method and tool for studying electronic structure in materials where long-range electrostatics and external fields play an important role.

</details>


### [114] [Kinetic versus refill-zone uncertainty in predicted pr--burning of methane-oxygen rotating detonation rocket engines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28404)
*Y. Sungtaek Ju*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper compares chemical-kinetic and refill-zone uncertainties in predicted pre-burning for methane-oxygen rotating detonation rocket engines. Five mechanisms differ by up to a factor of twenty in refill-zone ignition delay; mechanisms lacking methylperoxy chemistry fail to reproduce high-pressure laboratory ignition data below about 1150 K. After Bayesian calibration of CH3 + HO2 and use of data-consistent mechanisms, refill-zone uncertainty generally dominates the remaining kinetic uncertainty.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rotating detonation rocket engines can lose performance when fresh propellant ignites prematurely after mixing with hot recirculated products. Predicting this pre-burning requires high-pressure, product-diluted ignition chemistry that most engine-simulation mechanisms were not developed for, so the influence of mechanism choice and refill-zone characterization must be quantified.

Method: Using methane as the base fuel, the authors compare five existing chemical kinetics mechanisms for methane combustion in an idealized zero-dimensional refill-zone model. They test the mechanisms against published high-pressure ignition data, apply Bayesian methods to calibrate the most influential uncertain reaction, CH3 + HO2, while allowing for systematic differences between datasets, and assess which measurements would most improve predictions.

Result: Predicted refill-zone ignition delays differ by up to a factor of twenty among the five mechanisms. Mechanisms without methylperoxy chemistry, including two commonly used in engine simulations, do not reproduce laboratory data below about 1150 K. Calibration of CH3 + HO2 increases the relative importance of its chain-terminating channel, consistent with a direct rate measurement. With data-consistent mechanisms, remaining kinetic uncertainty in predicted pre-burnt fraction is in most cases several times smaller than uncertainty from refill-zone temperature, composition, and mixing.

Conclusion: Improved pre-burning predictions depend mainly on accurately characterizing the refill zone rather than solely on reducing kinetic uncertainty. Because the refill zone is represented by an idealized zero-dimensional model, the results are relative comparisons rather than direct engine predictions.

Abstract: Rotating detonation rocket engines can lose performance when fresh propellant mixes with hot recirculated combustion products and ignites before the detonation wave arrives. Predicting this pre-burning requires fuel ignition chemistry at high pressure and in product-diluted mixtures, conditions most chemical kinetic mechanisms used in engine simulations were not developed for. With methane as our base fuel, we quantify how strongly predicted pre-burning depends on the choice of mechanism, test the mechanisms against published high-pressure ignition data, and use Bayesian methods to calibrate the most influential uncertain reaction and to assess which measurements would best improve the predictions. As the refill zone is represented by an idealized zero-dimensional model, our results should be viewed as relative comparisons rather than direct engine predictions. Five existing chemical kinetics mechanisms for methane combustion predict refill-zone ignition delays that differ by up to a factor of twenty. Mechanisms without methylperoxy chemistry, including two commonly used in engine simulations, do not reproduce the laboratory data below about 1150 K. Calibrating the CH${_3}$ + HO${_2}$ reaction against these data, while allowing for systematic differences between datasets, increases the relative importance of its chain-terminating channel, consistent with a direct rate measurement. With the data-consistent mechanisms, the remaining kinetic uncertainty in the predicted pre-burnt fraction is, in most cases, several times smaller than the uncertainty arising from the state of the refill zone: the temperature and composition of the recirculated products and the way fresh and burnt gases mix. Improved pre-burning predictions are therefore expected to depend mainly on accurately characterizing the refill zone.

</details>


### [115] [Expressive Shallow Networks for Physics-Informed Learning: Why Curvature-Aware Optimization and FP64 Are Not Enough](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28393)
*Zachary Morrow, Yucheng Fu, Pratanu Roy, Michael Penwarden*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper studies Physics-Informed Shallow Universal Polynomial Networks (PI-SUPNs) as a shallow alternative to deep PINNs, showing improved trainability on challenging PDEs while demonstrating that the hardest cases still require FP64 precision and curvature-aware optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Physics-informed neural networks often fail on high-frequency and high-order PDEs because of optimization stiffness and spectral bias, and existing remedies largely work around difficulties caused by deep architectures rather than addressing their source.

Method: The authors replace deep nonlinear compositions with learnable polynomial expansions in a shallow computational graph, forming PI-SUPNs, and evaluate them on a high-frequency Helmholtz benchmark, higher vibrational modes of a fourth-order Euler-Bernoulli beam, and Allen-Cahn experiments. They also establish quasi-local approximation properties and spline-based convergence bounds for SUPNs.

Result: PI-SUPNs uniquely recover the Helmholtz solution where competing models fail even with FP64 and curvature-aware optimization; beam experiments show that accurate solutions require PI-SUPNs combined with FP64 and curvature-aware optimization; Allen-Cahn experiments show lower solution and derivative errors at reduced computational cost and more favorable loss landscapes.

Conclusion: Shallow architectures are an important mechanism for improving physics-informed trainability, but accurate solutions to the most challenging PDEs can still require FP64 precision and curvature-aware optimization.

Abstract: Physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) often struggle with high-frequency and high-order partial differential equations (PDEs) due to optimization stiffness and spectral bias, despite possessing sufficient approximation capacity. Existing remedies, including modified feature representations, domain decomposition, higher floating-point precision, and curvature-aware optimizers, can improve convergence but largely act as workarounds for optimization difficulties arising from deep architectures rather than addressing their underlying source. We investigate Physics-Informed Shallow Universal Polynomial Networks (PI-SUPNs), which replace deep nonlinear compositions with learnable polynomial expansions, retaining high approximation capacity within a shallow computational graph. On a high-frequency Helmholtz benchmark, PI-SUPNs are the only architecture to successfully recover the solution, while all competing models fail even under FP64 precision and curvature-aware optimization. However, experiments on higher vibrational modes of a fourth-order Euler-Bernoulli beam reveal that architectural improvements alone are insufficient: accurate solutions require the combination of PI-SUPNs, FP64 precision, and curvature-aware optimization. Additionally, Allen-Cahn experiments show that PI-SUPNs achieve lower solution and derivative errors at reduced computational cost, with more favorable loss landscapes than competing architectures. We also establish quasi-local approximation properties and spline-based convergence bounds for SUPNs. Our results show that shallow network architectures are an important mechanism for improving physics-informed trainability, but accurate solutions to the most challenging PDEs can still require FP64 precision and curvature-aware optimization.

</details>


### [116] [Lincege.jl: LINked Cluster Expansions on a GEneral GEometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31102)
*Pranav Seetharaman, Francisco Correia, Ehsan Khatami*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces Lincege.jl, a Julia package that generates clusters and sub-cluster information for numerical linked-cluster expansions on general finite or infinite lattices and outputs series coefficients, with visualization tools and a classical Ising solver template.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Numerical linked-cluster expansion (NLCE) is a powerful method for studying quantum lattice models directly in the thermodynamic limit, motivating a versatile, efficient, and easy-to-use package for generating the required clusters on general lattices.

Method: Lincege.jl takes Bravais lattice vectors, basis information, and the expansion unit as input and uses depth-first search to efficiently generate clusters, their lattice constants, and sub-cluster information. It outputs final series coefficients expressed through extensive cluster properties at different orders, and includes lattice/cluster visualization tools and a classical Ising model solver as a template for interfacing with other lattice-model solvers.

Result: The package is presented as versatile, efficient, and easy-to-use for NLCE cluster generation on general finite or infinite lattices. It can be installed via the Julia package manager, with example scripts on GitHub and online documentation; quantitative performance results are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: Lincege.jl provides a practical Julia-based framework for NLCE cluster generation and coefficient output on general geometries, supplemented by visualization and an Ising solver template for integration with other lattice-model solvers.

Abstract: The numerical linked-cluster expansion (NLCE) is a powerful tool for studying properties of quantum lattice models directly in the thermodynamic limit. Here, we introduce Lincege.jl, a versatile, efficient, and easy-to-use package for generating the clusters needed to perform the NLCE on a general finite or infinite lattice. Lincege.jl takes as input only the Bravais lattice vectors, the basis information, and the expansion unit and uses a depth-first search to efficiently generate clusters, their lattice constants, and the sub-cluster information. It outputs final coefficients of the series, expressed in terms of the extensive properties of the clusters, in different orders. The package also contains tools for visualizing the lattice and the generated clusters as well as a classical Ising model solver that can be used as a template for interfacing Lincege.jl with solvers of other lattice models. This package can be installed using the Julia package manager and utilized with example scripts from our GitHub repository at https://github.com/rightleftspin/Lincege.jl. The online documentation, including examples, can be found at https://rightleftspin.github.io/Lincege.jl/stable/.

</details>


### [117] [A new frequency-time hybrid RPML-BIE solver with few-mode corrections for the wave-equation problems in nonuniform closed waveguides](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30579)
*Yan Tan, Liwei Xu, Tao Yin*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes a frequency-time hybrid boundary integral equation solver for wave-equation problems in two-dimensional nonuniform closed waveguides with multiple channels, combining regionalized perfectly matched layer truncation, few-mode Dirichlet-to-Robin corrections, specialized quadrature, and complex-resonance extraction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Frequency-time hybrid Fourier/BIE methods are effective for time-dependent wave scattering, but waveguide modal expansions introduce cut-off frequencies that challenge both frequency-domain solver robustness and inverse Fourier transform accuracy because of non-smooth solution behavior and square-root singularities near cut-offs.

Method: A frequency-domain BIE method is developed using the free-space fundamental solution, regionalized perfectly matched layer truncation, and a few-mode Dirichlet-to-Robin modification, with a proof of uniform exponential convergence near cut-off frequencies. For the inverse Fourier transform, a sine-transform-based stationary-point Filon--Clenshaw--Curtis quadrature handles non-smoothness and square-root singularities, while complex resonances near the real axis are treated by AAA rational approximation and singularity subtraction.

Result: The method is rigorously proved to achieve uniform exponential convergence even at frequencies arbitrarily close to cut-off frequencies. Theoretical analysis shows that complex resonances close to the real axis may significantly affect Fourier transform convergence. Numerical experiments for frequency-domain and time-domain problems demonstrate efficiency and high accuracy; no quantitative metrics are provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: The proposed frequency-time hybrid RPML-BIE solver addresses cut-off-related frequency-domain challenges and non-smooth inverse Fourier transform behavior in nonuniform closed waveguides, with numerical experiments supporting its efficiency and accuracy.

Abstract: This paper is devoted to proposing a novel FTH integral equation solver for wave-equation problems in two-dimensional nonuniform closed waveguides with multiple channels. The FTH methodology, which combines the Fourier transform with frequency-domain BIE formulations, has demonstrated its effectiveness in solving time-dependent wave scattering problems. However, when applied to waveguide problems, the modal expansions arising from multiple scattering introduce cut-off frequencies. Such frequencies pose significant challenges, requiring both a frequency-robust solver for frequency-domain waveguide problems and an efficient treatment of the solution's non-smooth behavior near the cut-off frequencies for the evaluation of the inverse Fourier transform. The first issue is addressed by developing a new frequency-domain BIE method based on the free-space fundamental solution and regionalized perfectly matched layer truncation, together with a few-mode Dirichlet-to-Robin modification. The method is rigorously proved to achieve uniform exponential convergence, even at frequencies arbitrarily close to the cut-off frequencies. To address the second issue---the recovery of the time-domain solution via the inverse Fourier transform---a sine-transform based stationary-point Filon--Clenshaw--Curtis quadrature is introduced to accurately handle the non-smoothness and square-root singularities of frequency-domain solutions. Additionally, theoretical result shows that the complex resonances closing to the real axis may significantly effect the convergence of the Fourier transform algorithm and then a novel strategy of complex resonance extraction based on AAA rational approximation and singularity subtraction is utilized to avoid this influence. Numerical experiments for both frequency-domain and time-domain problems illustrate the efficiency and high accuracy of the proposed method.

</details>


### [118] [High-Throughput Screening of Phononic Bandgap in Experimentally Realized Materials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30344)
*Mohammad Bagheri, Kameyab Raza Abidi, Pekka Koskinen*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: Using a universal machine-learning interatomic potential validated against density-functional theory, the authors screened 154,879 materials and computed phonons for 42,347 thermodynamically stable, experimentally observed compounds, identifying 11,176 with a phononic band gap and highlighting candidates with acoustic-optical gaps and low lattice thermal conductivity, including three Li-metal binaries confirmed via first-principles phonon Boltzmann transport.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Phonon band gaps restrict phonon scattering and are attractive for functional materials in targeted thermal applications, but systematic exploration across broad material classes is hindered because first-principles methods and experiments are too expensive.

Method: The authors employed the universal machine-learning interatomic potential to quantify phonon and thermodynamic properties, validated the accuracy of machine-learning results against density-functional theory, screened 154,879 materials, performed phonon calculations for 42,347 thermodynamically stable and experimentally observed compounds, carried out phonon band gap analysis for dynamically stable materials, analyzed lattice thermal conductivity of materials with phonon gaps, and solved the linearized phonon Boltzmann equation from first principles for selected candidates.

Result: Of the screened materials, 11,176 dynamically stable compounds were found to possess a phononic gap, comprising 10,960 with optical-optical gaps and 270 with acoustic-optical gaps; analysis of lattice thermal conductivity identified candidates combining acoustic-optical gaps with low thermal conductivity, and first-principles phonon Boltzmann transport calculations demonstrated low room-temperature lattice thermal conductivity in three Li-metal binaries.

Conclusion: High-throughput machine-learning phonon screening is a practical route to discovering phononic-band-gap materials, and materials with acoustic-optical gaps, notably three Li-metal binaries, are promising candidates for low-thermal-conductivity thermal applications.

Abstract: Materials with a phonon band gap offer a unique mechanism to restrict phonon scattering, making them promising candidates for developing functional materials for targeted thermal applications. Unlocking this mechanism across broad material classes requires systematic exploration, but first-principles methods and experiments are too expensive. Here, we used the universal machine-learning interatomic potential to quantify phonons and thermodynamic properties. We validated the accuracy of machine-learning-calculated results by comparing them with density-functional theory. We screened 154,879 materials and performed phonon calculations for 42,347 thermodynamically stable and experimentally observed compounds. We then performed phonon band gap analysis for dynamically stable materials and identified 11,176 materials with a phononic gap, including 10,960 materials with optical-optical gaps and 270 with acoustic-optical gaps. We also analyzed the lattice thermal conductivity of materials with phonon band gaps to identify candidates with acoustic-optical gaps and low thermal conductivity. Finally, by solving the linearized phonon Boltzmann equation from first principles, we demonstrate low room-temperature lattice thermal conductivities in three Li-metal binaries.

</details>


### [119] [Atomistic insight into defect-mediated boron redistribution and high-temperature strengthening in TiAl alloys](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30330)
*Xiang Meng, Shuhei Shinzato, Zhiqiang Zhao, Linfeng Yu, Jian Guo, Liangli Chu, Kepeng Ouyang, Shigenobu Ogata, Zhuhua Zhang*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: Hybrid grand-canonical Monte Carlo/molecular dynamics with a machine-learning potential shows how boron segregates to defects in TiAl alloys, partitions to α2 and disordered regions, and strengthens the alloy at high temperature by pinning dislocations and suppressing twinning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Boron microalloying effectively refines as-cast TiAl microstructures and regulates mechanical properties, but the dilute-B configuration, its interactions with defects, and the resulting control of high-temperature deformation were not well understood.

Method: The authors used hybrid grand-canonical Monte Carlo/molecular dynamics simulations augmented with a newly developed high-fidelity machine-learning potential, together with simulated microstructural evolution and stacking-fault energetics, to study defect-mediated boron redistribution and deformation in TiAl alloys.

Result: Borides were markedly stiffer than the TiAl matrix and acted as brittle reinforcing phases, with boride/matrix interfacial adhesion depending on surface termination and stacking sequence. Interstitial B was trapped at Ti-rich octahedral sites by high migration barriers and could kinetically become substitutional near Al vacancies; increased tensile stress and vacancy concentration promoted bulk B enrichment. B enriched at semi-coherent γ/α2 boundaries, preferentially partitioned into α2, and accumulated mainly in disordered regions of polycrystalline TiAl, raising average flow stress by 9.4% at 1 at.% B and 900 K while reducing shear strain accumulation. B impeded dislocation glide via local pinning and suppressed deformation twinning.

Conclusion: The findings link boron redistribution to defect interactions and deformation, providing an atomistic framework for understanding and designing high-performance alloys based on interstitial solutes. The abstract does not report experimental validation.

Abstract: Boron (B) microalloying can effectively refine microstructures of as-cast TiAl alloys and regulate their mechanical properties. Yet, how dilute B exists in TiAl and interacts with defects and how these interactions govern high-temperature deformation remain elusive. Here, we systematically elucidate defect-mediated B redistribution in TiAl alloys and its consequences for their deformation using hybrid grand-canonical Monte Carlo/molecular dynamics simulations augmented with a newly developed high-fidelity machine-learning potential. As a typical product of B microalloying, borides are markedly stiffer than the TiAl matrix and act as brittle reinforcing phases, while borides/matrix interfacial adhesion depends on surface termination and stacking sequence. In the bulk, interstitial B tends to be trapped at Ti-rich octahedral sites by high migration barriers but can kinetically evolve into substitutional dopants upon approaching Al vacancies. Increased tensile stress and vacancy concentration further promote B enrichment in the bulk. B is enriched at semi-coherent $γ/α_2$ boundaries and preferentially partitions into the $α_2$ phase. In polycrystalline TiAl, B accumulates primarily in disordered regions, raising the average flow stress by 9.4% at 1 at.% B and 900 K. This strengthening is accompanied by reduced shear strain accumulation. Simulated microstructural evolution and stacking-fault energetics indicate that B impedes dislocation glide via local pinning and suppresses deformation twinning, thereby increasing resistance to plastic deformation. By linking solute redistribution to defect interactions and deformation, these findings establish an atomistic framework for understanding and designing high-performance alloys based on interstitial solutes.

</details>


### [120] [Growing Knot Embeddings into Spherical Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30245)
*Eren Gülmez, Joost de Graaf*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a computational growth and interfacial-energy relaxation construction that maps specific 3D knot embeddings to graphs embedded on the sphere. For standard embeddings of prime knots with up to seven crossings, the resulting growth-based graphs generally differ from Tait graphs. Non-minimal diagrams show the graphs are not knot invariants, while selected connected sums suggest some prime-factor graph features remain recognizable; behavior under isotopy is left for future study.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Knotted structures occur throughout soft matter and biology, where topological constraints can shape relaxation and spatial organization. The paper aims to connect specific 3D knot embeddings to spherical graph representations through a growth-based computational construction.

Method: A computational construction maps a specific 3D knot embedding to a graph embedded on the sphere using growth and interfacial-energy relaxation. The authors apply it to standard 3D embeddings of prime knots with at most seven crossings, compare the resulting graphs with Tait graphs, identify an empirical growth-graph reconstruction rule based on polygonal regions with alternating crossing structure in a knot diagram, and examine non-minimal diagrams and selected connected sums.

Result: The construction yields families of growth-based graphs that generally differ from the corresponding Tait graphs. An empirical reconstruction rule based on polygonal regions with alternating crossing structure is identified. Non-minimal diagrams demonstrate that the graphs are not knot invariants, while selected connected sums suggest that graph features associated with prime factors can remain recognizable.

Conclusion: Growth and interfacial-energy relaxation can map specific knot embeddings to spherical graphs, but these graphs are not knot invariants. Some features of prime factors may remain recognizable in selected connected sums. Determining how the graphs transform under isotopy and whether invariant features exist is left for future study.

Abstract: Knotted structures occur throughout soft matter and biology, where topological constraints can shape relaxation and spatial organization. Here, we introduce a computational construction that maps a specific 3D knot embedding to a graph embedded on the sphere through growth and interfacial-energy relaxation. For standard 3D embeddings of prime knots with at most seven crossings, we obtain a family of growth-based graphs that generally differ from the corresponding Tait graphs. We further identify an empirical growth-graph reconstruction rule based on polygonal regions with alternating crossing structure in a knot diagram. Non-minimal diagrams demonstrate that these graphs are not knot invariants, while selected connected sums suggest that graph features associated with their prime factors can remain recognizable. Determining how these graphs transform under isotopy may reveal invariant features, which we leave for future study.

</details>


### [121] [A Unified Energy-Density Framework for Moiré Interface Energetics and Mechanics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30152)
*Zhangke Lei, Weidong Yan, Zhicheng Feng, Wengen Ouyang*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops a reciprocal-space, energy-density framework for rigid van der Waals interfaces that connects registry-dependent microscopic interactions to the energetics and mechanics of infinite bilayers and finite crystalline contacts, including circular and polygonal moiré flakes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The mechanical response of moiré interfaces depends on atomic registry, contact size, and boundary geometry, so an analytical description is needed that retains discrete lattice information while linking microscopic interactions to the energetics of finite contacts.

Method: Registry-dependent pair potentials and atomic densities are expressed in reciprocal space to formulate a unified energy-density framework for rigid van der Waals interfaces. The formulation accommodates different lattice bases and atomic species and links single-atom and molecular adsorption to infinite bilayers and finite crystalline contacts. Explicit expressions for circular and regular polygonal flakes separate area contributions from boundary-dependent terms and relate microscopic corrugation coefficients to energy barriers, rotational torques, and interlayer shear stiffnesses. Direct-summation benchmarks and calculations for two-dimensional ice on graphene are used, with applications to interlayer shear and graphite compression.

Result: The boundary analysis recovers quarter-power area scaling of the barrier envelope for circular flakes and distinguishes bounded oscillations from square-root area scaling for polygonal contacts depending on edge orientation. A dimensionless criterion describes the polygon-to-circle crossover in terms of moiré wavelength, flake size, and side number. Direct-summation benchmarks support the finite-domain expressions, calculations for two-dimensional ice on graphene show the boundary-scaling trends in a molecular lattice, and applications connect the same microscopic interaction description to tangential and normal mechanical responses in interlayer shear and graphite compression.

Conclusion: The framework establishes an analytical connection between microscopic registry, moiré periodicity, and finite-domain geometry, providing a basis for understanding and tailoring the energetics and mechanics of van der Waals contacts.

Abstract: The mechanical response of moiré interfaces depends on how atomic registry combines with contact size and boundary geometry. An analytical description must retain this discrete lattice information while connecting microscopic interactions to the energetics of finite contacts. Here, we develop a unified energy-density framework for rigid van der Waals interfaces by expressing registry-dependent pair potentials and atomic densities in reciprocal space. The resulting formulation accommodates different lattice bases and atomic species, linking single-atom and molecular adsorption to infinite bilayers and finite crystalline contacts. Explicit expressions for circular and regular polygonal flakes separate the area contribution from boundary-dependent terms and connect microscopic corrugation coefficients to energy barriers, rotational torques, and interlayer shear stiffnesses. The boundary analysis recovers the quarter-power area scaling of the barrier envelope for circular flakes and distinguishes bounded oscillations from square-root area scaling for polygonal contacts according to edge orientation. A dimensionless criterion characterizes the polygon-to-circle crossover in terms of moiré wavelength, flake size, and side number. Direct-summation benchmarks support the finite-domain expressions, while calculations for two-dimensional ice on graphene demonstrate the corresponding boundary-scaling trends in a molecular lattice. Applications to interlayer shear and graphite compression connect the same microscopic interaction description to tangential and normal mechanical responses. The framework establishes an analytical connection between microscopic registry, moiré periodicity, and finite-domain geometry, providing a basis for understanding and tailoring the energetics and mechanics of van der Waals contacts.

</details>


### [122] [Beyond PINNs: Boundary-Enriched Holomorphic Networks for Laplace Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29922)
*Andrei Vlad Nicula, Kalp Shah, Robert-Stefan Sofroni, Mihnea Andrei Bârsan, Agneya Bhardwaj, Jan Jelínek, Chris Hoes, Remco Hogerwerf, Finn Govers, Storm Koper, Gerrit Janssen, Stef de Wildt, Alejandro Valverde Mahou*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper compares neural approaches for two-dimensional Laplace problems and shows that a holomorphic network enforcing harmonicity by construction, enriched with junction-specific singular functions, outperforms the compared neural models on singular benchmarks, although the enrichment span can make some closed-form cases trivially exact and classical least-squares fits can remain more accurate in other settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Physics-informed neural networks penalize PDE residuals, but this formulation can be difficult to optimize for solutions with singular behaviour. The authors exploit the strong analytical structure of the two-dimensional Laplace equation to enforce the governing equation directly in the neural representation.

Method: The authors compare a hybrid harmonic-neural model with a holomorphic neural network whose output is harmonic by construction and therefore requires only boundary training. They further enrich the holomorphic model with singular functions selected according to the interior angle and boundary conditions at each junction of boundary segments, and evaluate these models on singular benchmarks.

Result: On six singular benchmarks, the enriched model is the most accurate among the compared neural models. On four benchmarks with a closed-form solution, that solution lies in the span of the enrichment terms, and a least-squares fit of these terms alone is exact to rounding error. On Motz's problem and the cracked beam, the neural core reduces the relative error of the recovered leading singular coefficient from about 10^-2 for a least-squares fit with the same four terms to about 10^-6. On four variants whose solution has a smooth part outside the span, the network is 4×10^4 to 2×10^7 times more accurate than a fit of its singular terms alone; classical least-squares fits with more singular terms, or with harmonic polynomials and rational poles, are more accurate still.

Conclusion: Exploiting problem-specific analytical structure can provide an alternative to base and residual PINNs for challenging scientific computing problems. Details such as computational cost and generalization beyond the two-dimensional Laplace setting are not provided.

Abstract: Physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) solve partial differential equations by penalizing their residual, but this formulation can be difficult to optimize for solutions with singular behaviour. We focus specifically on the two-dimensional Laplace equation, whose strong analytical structure allows the governing equation to be enforced directly in the neural representation. We compare a hybrid harmonic-neural model with a holomorphic neural network whose output is harmonic by construction and therefore requires only boundary training. We further enrich the holomorphic model with singular functions chosen according to the interior angle and boundary conditions at each junction of boundary segments. On six singular benchmarks, the enriched model is the most accurate of the neural models we compare. On the four with a closed-form solution, however, that solution lies in the span of the enrichment terms, and a least-squares fit of these terms alone is exact to rounding error. On Motz's problem and the cracked beam, the neural core reduces the relative error of the recovered leading singular coefficient from about $10^{-2}$, for a least-squares fit with the same four terms, to about $10^{-6}$, and on four variants whose solution has a smooth part outside the span, the network is $4\times10^{4}$ to $2\times10^{7}$ times more accurate than a fit of its singular terms alone; classical least-squares fits with more singular terms, or with harmonic polynomials and rational poles, are more accurate still. Our results demonstrate how exploiting problem-specific analytical structure can provide an alternative to base and residual PINNs for challenging scientific computing problems.

</details>


### [123] [MagPot: A magnetoelastic potential generator for molecular dynamics simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29574)
*P. Nieves, I. Korniienko, S. Arapan, A. Fraile, R. Iglesias, D. Legut*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: MagPot is a Python program that generates Bethe–Slater parameter sets for spin-lattice molecular dynamics in LAMMPS, enabling atomistic simulation of magnetoelasticity and magnetostriction through modular magnetic interactions combined with non-magnetic potentials.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Magnetoelastic phenomena arise from coupling between atomic displacements and magnetic degrees of freedom, and their atomistic simulation requires interatomic models whose magnetic interactions depend explicitly on interatomic distance.

Method: MagPot generates Bethe–Slater parameter sets for LAMMPS SPIN simulations. From a compact set of material properties, it determines radial functions for Heisenberg exchange and the dipole and quadrupole contributions of the two-ion Néel interaction. Version 1.0 supports simple-cubic, body-centred-cubic and face-centred-cubic lattices with ferromagnetic and antiferromagnetic configurations, and its modular architecture allows additive combination with compatible non-magnetic interatomic potentials.

Result: The parameterisation workflow was assessed using equation-of-state, Curie-temperature, volume-magnetostriction, pressure-dependent magnetocrystalline-anisotropy and anisotropic-magnetoelastic tests. The abstract does not report quantitative outcomes.

Conclusion: MagPot automates construction of the magnetic contribution to the interatomic model, providing a straightforward and reproducible route from material properties to LAMMPS-ready magnetic interactions and facilitating large-scale atomistic simulations of magnetoelasticity, magnetostriction and phenomena associated with magnon–phonon coupling.

Abstract: Magnetoelastic phenomena originate from the coupling between atomic displacements and magnetic degrees of freedom, and their atomistic simulation requires interatomic models in which magnetic interactions depend explicitly on interatomic distance. This paper presents MagPot, a Python program that generates Bethe-Slater parameter sets for spin-lattice molecular dynamics simulations using the SPIN package of the Large-scale Atomic/Molecular Massively Parallel Simulator (LAMMPS). From a compact set of material properties, the program determines the radial functions describing Heisenberg exchange and the dipole and quadrupole contributions of the two-ion Néel interaction. Version 1.0 supports simple-cubic, body-centred-cubic and face-centred-cubic lattices with ferromagnetic and antiferromagnetic configurations. A central feature of MagPot is its modular architecture, which allows the magnetic contribution it generates to be combined additively with a broad range of compatible non-magnetic interatomic potentials. The parameterisation workflow is assessed through equation-of-state, Curie-temperature, volume-magnetostriction, pressure-dependent magnetocrystalline-anisotropy and anisotropic-magnetoelastic tests. By automating the construction of the magnetic contribution to the interatomic model, MagPot offers a straightforward and reproducible route from material properties to LAMMPS-ready magnetic interactions, facilitating large-scale atomistic simulations of magnetoelasticity and magnetostriction, as well as physical phenomena associated with magnon-phonon coupling.

</details>


### [124] [AmorphGen: A Python package for amorphous structure generation with machine-learning and classical interatomic potentials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29539)
*Chaiyawat Kaewmeechai, Louie Slocombe, Alexis G. Manche, David O. Scanlon*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: AmorphGen is an open-source Python package that generates and analyzes amorphous structures from a composition or crystal using random placement, melt-quench, or hybrid annealing/quenching routes with ASE-accessible classical and machine-learning potentials, plus structural and X-ray/neutron/EXAFS analysis tools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to simplify amorphous ensemble generation and standardize structural assessment so that amorphous models can be practically compared across compositions, potentials, and preparation routes, and new amorphous compositions can be screened.

Method: AmorphGen uses chemically informed rules to estimate initial densities and assign separation and coordination constraints. It provides three generation routes: random placement followed by relaxation, melt-quench from a crystal, and a hybrid route that anneals and quenches random structures. ASE provides access to classical and machine-learning potentials, with GPU batching for selected backends. Analysis tools compute coordination numbers, bond angles, rings, and X-ray or neutron structure factors, with an optional FEFF interface for EXAFS spectra.

Result: For amorphous Ga2O3, TiO2, and InGaZnO4, cation coordination numbers are close to reference models and measurements, and X-ray structure factors reproduce the main measured peaks. EXAFS spectra for Ga2O3 and InGaZnO4 capture the dominant first-shell experimental features. The hybrid route reduces bonding defects in SiO2 and As and builds the first sharp diffraction peak of SiO2.

Conclusion: AmorphGen makes it practical to compare amorphous models across compositions, potentials, and preparation routes and to screen new amorphous compositions by simplifying ensemble generation and standardizing structural assessment.

Abstract: AmorphGen is an open-source Python package for generating and analyzing amorphous structures from a composition or a crystal. Chemically informed rules estimate initial densities and assign separation and coordination constraints. Three routes are available: random placement followed by relaxation, melt-quench from a crystal, and a hybrid route that anneals and quenches random structures. ASE provides access to classical and machine-learning potentials, with GPU batching for selected backends. Analysis tools compute coordination numbers, bond angles, rings, and X-ray or neutron structure factors for comparison with measurements. An optional interface to the FEFF code computes extended X-ray absorption fine structure (EXAFS) spectra. For amorphous Ga2O3, TiO2 and InGaZnO4, the cation coordination numbers are close to reference models and measurements, and the X-ray structure factors reproduce the main measured peaks. The EXAFS spectra for Ga2O3 and InGaZnO4 also capture the dominant first-shell features observed experimentally. The hybrid route reduces bonding defects in SiO2 and As and builds the first sharp diffraction peak of SiO2. By simplifying ensemble generation and standardizing structural assessment, AmorphGen makes it practical to compare amorphous models across compositions, potentials, and preparation routes, and to screen new amorphous compositions.

</details>


### [125] [RxnScope: Accurate joint generation of product and transition-state pairs from reactants via flow matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29492)
*Yanyang Li, DaoJing Li, Manyi Yang, Shuhua Li*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: RxnScope is a flow-matching framework that jointly generates paired product and transition-state geometries from reactants, enabling reaction-network exploration and automatic reaction discovery at substantially lower computational cost than a single-ended growing string method.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reaction networks are important for understanding chemical reactivity and guiding reaction design, but constructing them is difficult when products are unknown because both products and their corresponding transition states must be identified across elementary steps.

Method: RxnScope uses flow matching to jointly generate paired product–transition-state geometries from reactants, conditioned on a potentially reactive region. Directed product-to-TS coupling lets evolving product geometries guide TS generation, and the paired outputs support product-topology ranking to prioritize candidates for validation. The abstract does not provide further architectural or training details.

Result: RxnScope recovered and validated 95.8% of reference pathways on Transition1x, performed robustly on multicomponent reactions, and enabled multistep pathway exploration. In automatic reaction discovery, it achieved low-energy pathway coverage comparable to the single-ended growing string method while using about 2% of its computational cost.

Conclusion: Joint product–transition-state generation is established as a new strategy for exploring reaction networks from reactants, with accurate pathway recovery and efficient automatic reaction discovery.

Abstract: Reaction networks are central to understanding chemical reactivity and guiding reaction design. Constructing them when products are unknown remains a major challenge in computational chemistry, requiring the identification of products and their corresponding transition states (TSs) across elementary steps. Here, we introduce RxnScope, a flow-matching framework that jointly generates paired product-TS geometries from reactants, conditioned on a potentially reactive region. Directed product-to-TS coupling allows evolving rather than fixed product geometries to guide TS generation, while paired outputs enable product-topology ranking to prioritize candidates for validation. RxnScope recovered and validated 95.8% of reference pathways on Transition1x, performed robustly on multicomponent reactions and enabled multistep pathway exploration. In automatic reaction discovery, it achieved low-energy pathway coverage comparable to that of the single-ended growing string method while using only about 2% of its computational cost. These results establish joint product-TS generation as a new strategy for exploring reaction networks from reactants.

</details>


### [126] [Nonempirical Determination of the Optimal TAO-DFT Fictitious Temperature for Molecular Systems in the Lowest Singlet State](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29414)
*Bing-Ying Li, Jeng-Da Chai*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes a nonempirical, spin-symmetry-restoration-based scheme to determine a system-dependent optimal fictitious temperature θ* in TAO-DFT for lowest-singlet molecular systems, and benchmarks it against fixed-θ TAO-DFT and standard Kohn-Sham DFT.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: TAO-DFT uses a fictitious temperature θ linked to static correlation, but the conventional system-independent fixed θ can be overly large for single-reference systems and too small for strongly multi-reference systems.

Method: A nonempirical scheme based on spin-symmetry restoration is used to determine the optimal system-dependent fictitious temperature θ* for molecular systems in their lowest singlet states. Benchmarks include multi-reference cases (H2, N2, Li2, and LiH bond dissociation and twisted ethylene torsional rotation) and single-reference cases (He2 and Ne2 dissociation).

Result: The nonempirical θ* approach shows superior overall accuracy compared with the conventional fixed-θ scheme. Relative to standard Kohn-Sham DFT (TAO-DFT with θ = 0), TAO-DFT with θ* substantially improves results for multi-reference systems while maintaining comparable accuracy for single-reference systems.

Conclusion: The proposed spin-symmetry-restoration-based nonempirical scheme overcomes the limitations of a fixed, preassigned θ by providing a system-dependent optimal fictitious temperature, improving TAO-DFT performance across multi-reference and single-reference molecular systems in the lowest singlet state.

Abstract: Thermally-assisted-occupation density functional theory (TAO-DFT) [J.-D. Chai, J. Chem. Phys., 2012, ${\bf 136}$, 154104] has been developed to investigate the ground-state properties of large electronic systems exhibiting varying multi-reference (MR) character. As a central parameter in TAO-DFT, the fictitious temperature $θ$ is closely linked to the degree of static correlation in an electronic system. While the conventional system-independent $θ$-scheme is computationally efficient and broadly applicable, a fixed, preassigned $θ$ can be overly large for single-reference (SR) systems yet too small for strongly MR systems. To overcome this limitation, we propose a nonempirical scheme based on spin-symmetry restoration to determine the optimal system-dependent fictitious temperature $θ^{*}$ for molecular systems in their lowest singlet states. Across benchmark tests encompassing both MR systems (the bond dissociation of H$_{2}$, N$_{2}$, Li$_{2}$, and LiH, as well as the torsional rotation of twisted ethylene) and pure SR systems (the dissociation of He$_{2}$ and Ne$_{2}$), our nonempirical approach demonstrates superior overall accuracy compared with the conventional fixed-$θ$ scheme. Furthermore, relative to standard Kohn-Sham density functional theory (TAO-DFT with $θ= 0$), TAO-DFT employing $θ^{*}$ yields substantial improvements for MR systems while preserving comparable accuracy in SR systems.

</details>


### [127] [CLFF: Automated Framework for Concurrent Learning Force Fields and Material Property Calculations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29187)
*Cao Thang Nguyen, Sung Youb Kim*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: CLFF is an open-source Python framework that automates concurrent-learning workflows for machine-learning interatomic potentials, integrating training, sampling, uncertainty-based selection, quantum-mechanical labeling, dataset augmentation, and materials property calculations while distributing tasks across heterogeneous remote computing resources.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Machine-learning interatomic potentials enable efficient large-scale atomistic simulations with near first-principles accuracy, but their development and deployment require intricate procedures, substantial manual effort, and domain expertise.

Method: The authors present CLFF, an open-source Python framework that unifies model training, MLIP-driven sampling, uncertainty-based candidate selection, quantum-mechanical labeling, and dataset augmentation. It supports several modern graph-based MLIP architectures, is compatible with widely used computational chemistry and materials science tools, asynchronously distributes tasks across heterogeneous remote computing resources, and provides automated workflows for initial data generation and material property calculations such as phonon dispersion and potential energy surface scans.

Result: CLFF provides a single controllable workflow that can aggregate fragmented hardware infrastructure, supports several graph-based MLIP architectures and widely used computational chemistry and materials science tools, and automates initial data generation and property calculations. The abstract reports no quantitative performance results.

Conclusion: By minimizing manual intervention and enhancing reproducibility, CLFF offers a flexible platform for developing and deploying machine-learning interatomic potentials in computational materials research.

Abstract: Machine learning interatomic potentials (MLIPs) offer an efficient paradigm for large-scale atomistic simulations with near first-principles accuracy. However, developing and deploying these potentials often involves intricate procedures that require substantial manual effort and domain expertise. In this work, we present CLFF, an open-source Python framework designed to automate concurrent learning workflows for MLIPs. CLFF integrates model training, MLIP-driven sampling, uncertainty-based candidate selection, quantum-mechanical labeling, and dataset augmentation into a unified workflow. The framework currently supports several modern graph-based MLIP architectures and is compatible with widely used computational chemistry and materials science tools. A distinguishing feature of CLFF is its ability to asynchronously distribute computational tasks across multiple heterogeneous remote computing resources, enabling users to aggregate fragmented hardware infrastructure within a single controllable workflow. In addition to concurrent learning, CLFF provides automated workflows for initial data generation and material property calculations, including phonon dispersion and potential energy surface scans. By minimizing manual intervention and enhancing reproducibility, CLFF offers a flexible platform for developing and deploying MLIPs in computational materials research.

</details>


### [128] [Data handling, production, and simulation in the Muon g-2 Experiment at Fermilab](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28851)
*Muon g-2 Collaboration, :, David Aguillard, Talal Albahri, Del Allspach, Jerry Annala, Karie Badgley, Stefan Baeßler, Lucy Bailey, Esra Barlas-Yucel, Tyler Barrett, Emanuela Barzi, Franco Bedeschi, Martin Berz, Meghna Bhattacharya, Hannah Binney, Paul Bloom, Jason Bono, Elia Bottalico, Themis Bowcock, Svende Braun, Matthew Bressler, Giovanni Cantatore, Robert M. Carey, Brendan Casey, Diego Cauz, Ritwika Chakraborty, Antoine Chapelain, Steve Chappa, Saskia Charity, Cheng Chen, Murong Cheng, Rebecca Chislett, Zhaolin Chu, Timothy Chupp, Christine Claessens, Francesco Confortini, Mary Convery, Simon Corrodi, Lorenzo Cotrozzi, Jason Crnkovic, Sultan Dabagov, Paul Debevec, Stefano Di Falco, Giuseppe Di Sciascio, Simone Donati, Brian Drendel, Anna Driutti, Michael Eads, Andrew Edmonds, Jessica Esquivel, Midhat Farooq, Renee Fatemi, Katherine Ferraby, Carlo Ferrari, Martin Fertl, Aaron Fienberg, Andrea Fioretti, David Flay, Sean Foster, Horst Friedsam, Nathan Froemming, Carlo Gabbanini, Irwin Gaines, Sudeshna Ganguly, Jimin George, Lawrence Gibbons, Antonio Gioiosa, Kevin Giovanetti, Paolo Girotti, Wesley Gohn, Lisa Goodenough, Tim Gorringe, Joseph Grange, Sam Grant, Frederick Gray, Selcuk Haciomeroglu, Tabitha Halewood-Leagas, Dariush Hampai, Fang Han, Jason Hempstead, David W. Hertzog, Gavin Hesketh, Emma Hess, Antony Hibbert, Zachary Hodge, Siew Yan Hoh, Kyun Woo Hong, Ran Hong, Tianqi Hu, Yuekai Hu, Michele Iacovacci, Marco Incagli, Scott Israel, Peter Kammel, Manolis Kargiantoulakis, Marin Karuza, Jarek Kaspar, David Kawall, Laura Kelton, Alex Keshavarzi, David Kessler, Kim Siang Khaw, Zepyoor Khechadoorian, Brendan Kiburg, Mandy Kiburg, On Kim, Nicolas Kinnaird, Eva Kraegeloh, Joshua LaBounty, Kevin Labe, Mark Lancaster, Soohyung Lee, Bingzhi Li, Dikai Li, Liang Li, Ivan Logashenko, Abel Lorente Campos, Zejia Lu, Alessandra Lucà, Gleb Lukicov, Alberto Lusiani, Adam L. Lyon, Brynn MacCoy, Robyn Madrak, Kyoko Makino, Stefano Mastroianni, Ryan McCarthy, James P. Miller, Silvia Miozzi, Baisakhi Mitra, James Morgan, William M. Morse, James Mott, Atanu Nath, Jun Kai Ng, Hogan Nguyen, Yuri Oksuzian, Zhanibek Omarov, Wren Osar, Rachel Osofsky, Seongtae Park, Giovanni Pauletta, Joseph Peck, Giovanni Maria Piacentino, Riccardo Nunzio Pilato, Kevin Pitts, Brad Plaster, Nick Pohlman, Chris C. Polly, Dinko Počanić, Joe Price, Breese Quinn, Mohammad Ubaidullah Hassan Qureshi, Greg Rakness, Suvarna Ramachandran, Erik Ramberg, René Reimann, B. Lee Roberts, David Rubin, Mikio Sakurai, Lorenzo Santi, Cristina Schlesier, Adam Schreckenberger, Yannis Semertzidis, Aria Soha, Matteo Sorbara, James Stapleton, Dean Still, Chris Stoughton, Diktys Stratakis, Dominik Stöckinger, Erik Swanson, George Sweetmore, David Sweigart, Michael Syphers, Yusuke Takeuchi, David Tarazona, Thomas Teubner, Alexander Tewsley-Booth, Vladimir Tishchenko, Hoai Nam Tran, Will Turner, Eremey Valetov, Dominika Vasilkova, Graziano Venanzoni, Tammy Walton, Adrian Weisskopf, Leah Welty-Rieger, Peter Winter, Yongyi Wu, Byungchul Yu, Mete Yucel, Estifa'a Zaid, Yonghao Zeng, Ce Zhang*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper describes computing strategies for the Fermilab Muon g-2 experiment, covering data production, handling, simulation, and analysis of over 10 petabytes of data while achieving 127 ppb precision. Fermilab software infrastructure, efficient resource use, and an improved reconstruction workflow increased throughput and reduced analysis turnaround time by an order of magnitude.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Muon g-2 experiment required precise measurement of the muon anomalous magnetic moment and needed to manage very large detector and simulation datasets, creating computational and operational challenges in data production, handling, and analysis.

Method: The collaboration used Fermilab-developed software infrastructure and production tools, made efficient use of resources, and improved its collaboration-developed reconstruction workflow to address the computing and operational challenges of large-scale data processing.

Result: The experiment processed over 10 petabytes of detector and simulation data and measured the muon anomalous magnetic moment with a precision of 127 ppb. It improved data throughput capacity and reduced analysis turnaround time by an order of magnitude over the experiment's lifetime.

Conclusion: The computing and operational challenges of the Muon g-2 experiment were overcome through Fermilab software infrastructure, efficient resource use, and reconstruction workflow improvements, enabling higher throughput and much faster analysis.

Abstract: This paper details the computing strategies of the Muon g-2 Experiment at Fermilab, which measured the muon's anomalous magnetic moment with a precision of 127 ppb by processing over 10 petabytes of detector and simulation data. This paper discusses the computational and operational challenges of data production, data handling, and data analysis of such large datasets. These challenges were overcome by using Fermilab-developed software infrastructure and production tools, making efficient use of resources, and improving the collaboration-developed reconstruction workflow. By overcoming these challenges, the experiment improved the data throughput capacity and reduced the analysis turnaround time by an order of magnitude over the course of its lifetime.

</details>


### [129] [Learning free energy across a differentiable temperature range by deep generative modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28810)
*Yaowen Zhang, Chen Chen, Wanyu Lin, Shuo-Hui Li, Ding Pan*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces learned temperature-differentiable free energy perturbation (TD-FEP), a deep generative framework that learns a temperature-conditioned invertible mapping between reference and target systems via data-free, energy-based optimization, enabling one-shot state generation and free energy calculation across a continuous temperature range.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional free energy methods such as thermodynamic integration and free energy perturbation typically require calculations at individual temperatures and are costly because extensive thermodynamic states must be sampled.

Method: TD-FEP is a deep generative framework that learns a temperature-conditioned, invertible mapping between reference and target systems through data-free, energy-based optimization, unifying one-shot state generation and free energy calculation over a continuous temperature range.

Result: TD-FEP was validated on isolated molecules, periodic materials, and chemical reactions. The abstract does not provide quantitative accuracy, efficiency, or benchmarking details.

Conclusion: TD-FEP provides a temperature-differentiable deep generative approach for free energy calculation and state generation across a continuous temperature range, with validation spanning molecular, materials, and reaction applications.

Abstract: Free energy governs the directionality, stability, and temperature sensitivity of physical, chemical, and biological transformations. Conventional free energy calculations, such as thermodynamic integration and free energy perturbation, often have to be performed at individual temperatures and are limited by the high cost of sampling an extensive set of thermodynamic states. Here, we introduce learned temperature-differentiable free energy perturbation (TD-FEP), a deep generative framework that learns a temperature-conditioned, invertible mapping between reference and target systems through data-free, energy-based optimization. TD-FEP thereby unifies one-shot state generation and free energy calculation across a continuous temperature range. We validated TD-FEP across a broad range of applications, including isolated molecules, periodic materials, and chemical reactions.

</details>


### [130] [LayerCake v1.0: a framework for low- to intermediate-order automated models development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28647)
*Jonathan Demaeyer, Oisín Hamilton, Stéphane Vannitsem*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: LayerCake v1.0 is a Python/SymPy framework that automates Galerkin projection of two-dimensional spatially extended geophysical models onto restricted spectral modes, producing model tendencies that can be evaluated numerically for time integration or symbolically for sensitivity and bifurcation analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper introduces a framework to support low- to intermediate-order automated model development for spatially extended geophysical models, simplifying spectral projection and enabling both numerical integration and symbolic analysis. The abstract does not state a more specific motivation or prior limitation.

Method: LayerCake is written in Python and uses the SymPy symbolic computation package to compute Galerkin projections of two-dimensional spatially extended geophysical models. The projections yield time evolution operators in spectral space on a restricted set of modes, and the resulting model tendencies can be evaluated numerically or symbolically.

Result: The framework is demonstrated with several simple geophysical model examples that showcase its capabilities, and the authors indicate how more complex systems can be designed. No quantitative performance or validation results are provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: LayerCake provides an automated Python/SymPy-based route from spatially extended geophysical model formulations to restricted-mode spectral tendency models usable for time integration and symbolic parameter-sensitivity and bifurcation studies, with indicated extensibility to more complex systems.

Abstract: A new model development framework called LayerCake is presented. It is written in the Python language and relies on the Sympy symbolic computation package to compute the Galerkin projection of two-dimensional spatially extended geophysical models. These projections provide their time evolution operator in the spectral space on a restricted set of modes. The resulting tendencies of the models can be evaluated either numerically (for time integration) or symbolically (for parameters sensitivity analysis and bifurcation analysis). We provide several simple examples of geophysical models to showcase the capabilities of the framework, and we hint at how more complex systems can be designed.

</details>


### [131] [Interstitial Magnetoelectricity in Altermagnetic Electrides](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28504)
*Yue Li, Shibo Fang, Jianhua Wang, Qiang Li, Weizhen Meng, Haiyu Meng, Xiong-Xiong Xue, Jianfeng Wang, Yi Du, Jing Lu, Yee Sin Ang*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper studies the magnetoelectric response and spin-polarized transport of the altermagnetic electride Zr2N monolayer, showing that an electric field can break the magnetic compensation of interstitial anionic electrons and enable electrical control of spin conductivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Electrides and altermagnets are distinct quantum-material classes based on interstitial anionic electrons and nonrelativistic spin splitting, respectively. The recently predicted Stoner-instability-driven altermagnetic electride offers a natural bridge between them, motivating investigation of its magnetoelectric response and related spin-polarized transport.

Method: The authors use a tight-binding Hubbard model and first-principles calculations to investigate the magnetoelectric response and spin-polarized transport in the Zr2N monolayer altermagnetic electride.

Result: The electric field can break the magnetic compensation between the two interstitial anionic electrons, inducing finite net magnetization and producing an interstitial-electron-mediated magnetoelectric response. This response is further demonstrated through electrical control of spin conductivity.

Conclusion: The results reveal a magnetoelectric effect in altermagnetic electrides and suggest a path toward electride-based spintronic devices.

Abstract: Electrides and altermagnets are two distinct yet intriguing classes of quantum materials, characterized by interstitial anionic electrons and nonrelativistic spin splitting, respectively. The recent prediction of Stoner-instability-driven altermagnetic electride provides a natural bridge between these two fields. Here, we investigate the magnetoelectric response and its related spin-polarized transport in the altermagnetic electride Zr$_2$N monolayer. Using the tight-binding Hubbard model and first-principles calculations, we find that the electric field can break the magnetic compensation between the two interstitial anionic electrons, inducing a finite net magnetization and giving rise to an interstitial electron mediated magnetoelectric response. The magnetoelectric response is further demonstrated through the electrical control of spin conductivity. Our results reveal a magnetoelectric effect in altermagnetic electrides, paving a way towards electride-based spintronic devices.

</details>


### [132] [An equivariant classical density functional for ionic fluids at complex interfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28501)
*Bingqing Cheng*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops a machine-learned, symmetry-adapted classical density functional for ionic fluids that combines a neural short-range free energy with explicit long-range electrostatics and transfers across complex charged interfaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Predicting how ionic packing and long-range electrostatics jointly determine behavior at complex interfaces is technologically important, but it remains challenging without atomistic simulations.

Method: The authors construct a machine-learned classical density functional for ionic fluids using a symmetry-adapted neural free energy for short-range correlations plus explicit long-range electrostatics. It is trained on three-dimensional equilibrium densities and bulk correlations of the full-Coulomb restricted primitive model, with self-consistent coupling to metal atoms at electrodes and mobilities determined independently from bulk molecular dynamics for dynamics.

Result: A single functional transfers to charge-patterned particles and atomically structured electrodes. Around anisotropic charged solutes, it reproduces ionic structure and mean forces from molecular dynamics, including reversals of the bare electrostatic force. At metal electrodes, it captures redistribution of ionic and electrode charge and loading-dependent capacitance enhancement from steps and bumps. With independently determined mobilities, it describes main features of charging dynamics and resolves geometry-dependent spatial relaxation.

Conclusion: The framework can be extended to more complex ionic fluids and enables efficient exploration of how composition and geometry control structure and response at complex interfaces.

Abstract: Predicting the combined effects of ionic packing and long-range electrostatics at complex interfaces is technologically important, but remains challenging without atomistic simulations. Here we present a machine-learned classical density functional for ionic fluids that combines a symmetry-adapted neural free energy for short-range correlations with explicit long-range electrostatics. Trained on three-dimensional equilibrium densities and bulk correlations of the full-Coulomb restricted primitive model, a single functional transfers to charge-patterned particles and atomically structured electrodes. Around anisotropic charged solutes, it reproduces the ionic structure and mean forces from molecular dynamics, including reversals of the bare electrostatic force. At metal electrodes, self-consistent coupling to metal atoms captures the redistribution of ionic and electrode charge and the loading-dependent capacitance enhancement produced by steps and bumps. Combined with mobilities determined independently from bulk molecular dynamics, the same functional describes the main features of charging dynamics and resolves geometry-dependent spatial relaxation. This framework can be extended to more complex ionic fluids, enabling efficient exploration of how composition and geometry control structure and response.

</details>


### [133] [PFASST-BISICLES: A Parallel-in-Time Ice Sheet Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28434)
*Weijia, Liu, Hans Johansen, Daniel F. Martin*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: PFASST-BISICLES couples the PFASST parallel-in-time method with the BISICLES ice sheet model to compute multiple timesteps concurrently. Tests on a sinusoidal ice stream and MISMIP+ show reduced temporal error versus forward Euler, limited spatial scaling near 16 processors, additional temporal-parallel speedup, and substantially reduced runtime while preserving main simulated features.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Efficient ice sheet simulation is needed to project ice sheet responses to climate forcing and contributions to sea level change. Modern computational systems require high parallelism, but traditional time-dependent ice sheet simulations parallelize the spatial domain while advancing time serially.

Method: The authors combine the Parallel Full Approximation Scheme in Space and Time (PFASST) with the BISICLES ice sheet model to integrate multiple timesteps concurrently. They evaluate numerical agreement and parallel performance using a fully grounded sinusoidal ice stream with adaptive mesh refinement and the MISMIP+ marine ice-sheet benchmark, including comparisons with forward Euler and spatial and temporal processor scaling tests.

Result: For the sinusoidal stream, PFASST-BISICLES yields temporal errors about an order of magnitude smaller than forward Euler at the same timestep, though both show approximately first-order convergence. Spatial scaling levels off near 16 processors, while temporal parallelism provides further acceleration with nearly linear speedup through approximately 16 temporal processors. In MISMIP+, it reproduces the main thickness and velocity patterns after 50 years of glacier evolution, with the largest differences near the grounding zone. With four spatial processors fixed, increasing temporal processors from one to four reduces runtime from 33.8 to 10.5 hours.

Conclusion: Temporal parallelism can complement spatial decomposition in ice-sheet modeling, reducing time to solution while maintaining agreement in the main simulated features. The results support PFASST-BISICLES as a parallel-in-time approach for ice sheet simulation, although spatial scaling is limited near 16 processors and the largest MISMIP+ differences occur near the grounding zone.

Abstract: Efficiently simulating ice sheet dynamics on modern computational systems is essential for projecting the response of ice sheets to climate forcing and the resulting contributions to sea level change. On modern computational systems, this demands high levels of parallelism to fully exploit modern architectures. Traditionally, time-dependent simulations like ice sheet models achieve parallelism entirely by distributing the spatial domain, while remaining serial in time, as solutions are marched forward. We present PFASST-BISICLES, which combines the Parallel Full Approximation Scheme in Space and Time (PFASST) with BISICLES ice sheet model to integrate multiple timesteps concurrently. We evaluate numerical agreement and parallel performance using a fully grounded sinusoidal ice stream with adaptive mesh refinement and the MISMIP+ marine ice-sheet benchmark. For the sinusoidal stream, PFASST-BISICLES produces temporal errors approximately an order of magnitude smaller than forward Euler at the same timestep, although both methods exhibit approximately first-order convergence. Spatial scaling levels off near 16 processors, while temporal parallelism provides further acceleration, with nearly linear speedup through approximately 16 temporal processors. In MISMIP+, PFASST-BISICLES reproduces the main thickness and velocity patterns after glacier evolution of 50 years, with the largest differences concentrated near the grounding zone. Increasing the temporal processor count from one to four, with four spatial processors fixed, reduces runtime from 33.8 to 10.5 hours. These results demonstrate that temporal parallelism can complement spatial decomposition in ice-sheet modeling, reducing time to solution while maintaining agreement in the main simulated features.

</details>
